Local and global universality of random matrix cokernels
Hoi H. Nguyen, Melanie Matchett Wood

Abstract
. In this paper we study the cokernels of various random integral matrix models, including random symmetric, random skew-symmetric, and random Laplacian matrices. We provide a systematic method to establish universality under very general randomness assumptions. Our highlights include both local and global universality of the cokernel statistics of all these models. In particular, we find the probability that a sandpile group of an Erdős-Rényi random graph is cyclic, answering a question of Lorenzini from 2008.



1.
Introduction

Given a graph
G
, there is a naturally associated abelian group
S_{G}
, which has gone in the literature by many names, including the sandpile group, the critical group, the Jacobian, and the Picard group (due to its independent appearance in many subjects ranging from statistical mechanics to combinatorics to arithmetic geometry), see
[
BdlHN97
,
Dha90
,
Lor89
,
Lor91
,
Rus90
]
. A combinatorial way to interpret this group is via Chip-Firing game
[
Big99
,
GK19
]
. More precisely, a (degree zero) divisor on
G
is a function
\delta:V(G)\to{\mathbb{Z}}
where
\sum_{v}\delta(v)=0
. One can naturally define the sum
\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}
of two such divisors
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
by
(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2})(v)=\delta_{1}(v)+\delta_{2}(v),v\in V(G)
. Let
Div^{0}(G)
be the group of (degree zero) divisors equipped with this addition. There is a natural equivalence relation over the elements of
Div^{0}(G)
, namely we say
\delta_{1}\sim\delta_{2}
if there is a sequence of chip-firing moves from
\delta_{1}
to
\delta_{2}
, where these moves include “borrow” (a borrow move at
v_{0}
changes
\delta
to
\delta_{{\mathbf{b}},v_{0}}
, where
\delta_{{\mathbf{b}},v_{0}}(v_{0})=\delta(v_{0})+d(v_{0})
and
d(v_{0})
is the degree of
v_{0}
, and
\delta_{{\mathbf{b}},v_{0}}(v^{\prime})=\delta(v^{\prime})-1
for all neighbors
v^{\prime}
of
v_{0}
, as well as
\delta_{{\mathbf{b}},v_{0}}(v)=\delta(v)
at all other vertices) and “firing” (a firing move at
v_{0}
changes
\delta
to
\delta_{{\mathbf{f}},v_{0}}
where
\delta_{{\mathbf{f}},v_{0}}(v_{0})=\delta(v_{0})-d(v_{0})
and
\delta_{{\mathbf{f}},v_{0}}(v^{\prime})=\delta(v^{\prime})+1
for all neighbors
v^{\prime}
of
v_{0}
, as well as
\delta_{{\mathbf{f}},v_{0}}(v)=\delta(v)
at all other vertices). The group
S_{G}
is then simply the quotient
Div^{0}(G)/\sim
.

We also invite the reader to Subsection
1.4
for further discussion on this fascinating group. One can see that
S_{G}
is the cokernel of the (combinatorial) Laplacian of
G
, and its order is the number of spanning trees of
G
. About fifteen years ago, Lorenzini
[
Lor08
]
asked how often sandpile groups of graphs are cyclic, and a specific answer was conjectured in
[
CKL+15
]
(see also Table
1
). In this paper, we answer that question, proving the conjecture.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
G\in G(n,1/2)
be an Erdős-Rényi graph on
n
vertices. We have

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(S_{G}\mbox{ is cyclic})=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\zeta(2i+1)^{-1}\approx.7935

where
\zeta(s)
is the Riemann zeta function.

This theorem is one application of our development of new techniques for proving global statistics of random symmetric integral matrices (many of which also apply to random skew-symmetric integral matrices). Previous work of the second author
[
Woo17
]
studied
local
statistics, in particular the Sylow
p
-subgroups of the cokernels of random symmetric matrices for any finite set of primes
p
.
Global
properties of a finite abelian group are those that cannot necessarily be determined from a finite list of Sylow
p
-subgroups, such as cyclicty as we see above.

Our work is the first to address the
universality
aspect of these global statistics of random symmetric matrices, i.e. the extent to which the statistics, asymptotically, do not depend on the distribution of the entries of the matrices.
A significant part of Random Matrix Theory is to study the universality phenomenon of empirical spectral distributions under different matrix symmetries, under different sources of randomness, and under different scalings. In this paper we are pursuing a different direction of universality of random matrices, namely the behaviour of their cokernels as abelian groups. (When the entries
m_{ij}
of an
n\times n
matrix
M
are integers, the cokernel is defined as
\mathbf{Cok}(M)={\mathbb{Z}}^{n}/M({\mathbb{Z}}^{n}).
)

We make the following definition to restrict the types of entries our random matrices will have.
Let
\alpha>0
be given, and fixed throughout the paper. We say a random integer
\xi
is
\alpha
-balanced
if for every prime
p
we have

(1.2)

\max_{r\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbf{P}}(\xi\equiv r\pmod{p})\leq 1-\alpha.

For i.i.d symmetric matrices, we have a universality result analogous to Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
M_{n}=M_{n\times n}=(x_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
be a random symmetric matrix
with upper triangular entries
x_{ij}
for
i\geq j
each i.i.d. copies of an integral
\alpha
-balanced random variable
\xi
.
We have

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\mathbf{Cok}(M_{n})\mbox{ is cyclic}\Big)=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\zeta(2i+1)^{-1}\approx.7935.

Our methods also apply to i.i.d skew-symmetric matrices, though the distributions are different (e.g. because the rank of a skew-symmetric matrix is always even).

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
A_{n}=(x_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
be a random skew-symmetric matrix where the upper diagonal entries
x_{ij},
for
i>j
, are i.i.d. copies of an integral
\alpha
-balanced random variable
\xi
.
Let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of finite abelian groups of the form
H\times H
, and for such a group
G
, let

\operatorname{Sp}(G)
be the group of automorphisms of
G
that preserve a fixed non-degenerate skew-symmetric bilinear pairing.
We have

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})\mbox{ is a square of a cyclic group}\Big)=\zeta(2)\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\zeta(2i+1)^{-1}\prod_{p\textrm{ prime}}(1-p^{-2}+p^{-3}).

Furthermore, for any abelian group
C

(1.5)

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})\simeq C\Big)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{|\operatorname{Sp}(B)|}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\zeta(2i+1)^{-1}&C={\mathbb{Z}}\times B,\textrm{ for }B\in\mathcal{S}\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}.

What allows us to prove global statistics is a new method to understand the behavior of the matrices modulo primes that are very large compared to
n
.
This is made more difficult by the dependence between the upper and lower triangular entries in the above models. Moreover, it is significantly more challenging to handle the Laplacian model in Theorem
1.1
because of the dependence of the diagonal on the other entries of the matrix. We discuss our new techniques to over come these difficulties later in the introduction.

n
\backslash
q

.3

.5

.7

15

.784255

.792895

.775746

30

.793807

.793570

.793375

.793308

.793962

.793637

60

.793436

.793694

.79354

Table 1
.
Clancy et.al. computed in
[
CKL+15
]
the Jacobians of
10^{6}
connected random graphs with
n
vertices and edge probability
q
and this chart gives the probability that the Jacobian is cyclic in each case.

Past work and further results of this paper

1.1.
Cokernels of random integral non-symmetric matrices

For an abelian group
G
and a prime
p
, we write
G_{p}
for the Sylow
p
-subgroup of
G
. For a set
P
of primes, we write
G_{P}:=\prod_{p\in P}G_{p}
, the product of the Sylow
p
-subgroups of
G
for all
p\in P
.
Motivated by the Cohen-Lenstra heuristics for the distribution of class groups of number fields, the following has been shown by the second author.

Theorem 1.6
.

[
Woo19
, Corollary 3.4]

Let
Q_{n}
be a random matrix with entries
i.i.d copies of a
\alpha
-balanced random integer
\xi
. Let
B
be any finite abelian group, and let
P
be a finite set of primes including all those that divide
|B|
. Then

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}\Big((\mathbf{Cok}(Q_{n}))_{P}\simeq B\Big)=\frac{1}{|\mathbf{Aut}(B)|}\prod_{p\in P}\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}(1-p^{-k}).

Remark 1.7
.

For a particular finite abelian group
B
, by taking
P
larger and larger and since
\prod_{p\textrm{ prime}}(1-p^{-1})=0
, we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(\mathbf{Cok}(Q_{n})\simeq B)=0.

While the above results hold for
local
statistics (i.e. when
\mathbf{Cok}(M_{n})_{P}
is isomorphic to a given finite abelian group), it is natural to study
global
statistics, such as how often
\mathbf{Cok}(Q_{n})
is cyclic
1
1

1

Clearly one could also ask about other global properties, but cyclicity seems to be one of the most natural ones.
.
For example, the following gives a main result of
[
NW22
]
in the case where the matrix entries do not change with
n
(the case of interest in this paper).

Theorem 1.8
.

[
NW22
, Theorem 1.2]
Let
Q_{n}=Q_{n\times n}=(x_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
be a random matrix where the entries
x_{ij}
are i.i.d. copies of an integral
\alpha
-balanced random integer
\xi
.
We have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\mathbf{Cok}(Q_{n\times n})\mbox{ is cyclic}\Big)=\prod_{p\textrm{ prime}}(1+\frac{1}{p(p-1)})\prod_{k=2}^{\infty}\zeta(k)^{-1}.

We also showed the following generalization of Theorem
1.8
.

Theorem 1.9
.

[
NW22
, Theorem 2.5]
Let
Q_{n}=Q_{n\times n}
be as in Theorem
1.8
.
Let
B
be a finite abelian group and let
k_{0}
be larger than any prime divisor of
|B|
, and define
C_{B}=\{B\times C\,|\,C\textrm{ cyclic, }p\nmid|C|\textrm{ for }1<p<k_{0}\}
, the set of groups differing from
B
by a cyclic group with order only divisible by primes at least
k_{0}
.
Then, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\mathbf{Cok}(Q_{n\times n})\in C_{B}\Big)=\frac{1}{|\mathbf{Aut}(B)|}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<k_{0}\\
p\textrm{ prime}\end{subarray}}(1-p^{-1})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq k_{0}\\
p\textrm{ prime}\end{subarray}}(1+\frac{1}{p(p-1)})\prod_{k=2}^{\infty}\zeta(k)^{-1}.

1.2.
Cokernels of random integral symmetric matrices

Since the behavior of the empirical spectral distribution is quite different for general versus symmetric matrices (see for instance
[
BS10
,
Ede88
,
TV10a
]
and
[
Meh67
,
Pas72
,
Wig58
]
), and also because the cokernels of symmetric matrices naturally have pairings
[
CLP15
]
, one might expect that the cokernel statistics of symmetric matrices are different from the non-symmetric case.
For local statistics, the second author showed the following result on the local statistics.

Theorem 1.10
.

[
Woo17
, Corollary 9.2]
Let
M_{n}=M_{n\times n}=(x_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
be a random symmetric matrix
with upper triangular entries
x_{ij}
, for
i\geq j
, that are i.i.d. copies of an
\alpha
-balanced random integer
\xi
.
Let
B
be any finite abelian group, and let
P
be a finite set of primes including all those that divide
|B|
. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}((\mathbf{Cok}(M_{n}))_{P}\simeq B)=\frac{\#\{\mbox{symmetric, bilinear, perfect }\phi:B\times B\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}\}}{|B||\mathbf{Aut}(B)|}\prod_{p\in P}\prod_{k\geq 0}(1-p^{-2k-1})

and for any
p\in P

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(M_{n}/p)=n-r)=p^{-r(r+1)/2}\prod_{i=r+1}^{\infty}(1-p^{-i})\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i})^{-1}.

Here
M/p
is the matrix of entries modulo
p
. Note that if
B=\oplus_{i}{\mathbb{Z}}/p^{\lambda_{i}}{\mathbb{Z}}
with
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
, and with conjugate partition
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\geq\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\geq\dots
then

\#\{\mbox{sym., bilinear, perfect }\phi:B\times B\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}\}=p^{-\sum_{i}\lambda_{j}^{\prime}(\lambda_{i}^{\prime}+1)/2}\prod_{i=1}^{\lambda_{1}}\prod_{j=1}^{\lfloor(\lambda_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda_{i+1}^{\prime})/2\rfloor}(1-p^{-2j})^{-1}.

Remark 1.11
.

Similarly to Remark
1.7
, for a particular finite abelian group
B
, by taking
P
larger and larger, for random symmetric matrices
M_{n}
as above we also have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(\mathbf{Cok}(M_{n})\simeq B)=0.

Now for global statistics such as cyclicity, we will first explain a heuristic guess for this probability. Note that
\mathbf{Cok}(M_{n})
is cyclic if and only if its reduction to modulo
p
is cyclic for all primes
p
. We then make two idealized heuristic assumptions on
M_{n}
. (i) (uniformity assumption) Assume that for each prime
p
the entries of
M_{n}
are uniformly distributed modulo
p
. In this case, it is classical that the probability that
M_{n}
is cyclic is
(1-p^{-n-1})\prod_{i=1}^{(n-2)/2}(1-p^{-2i-1})
when
n
is even and

\prod_{i=1}^{(n-1)/2}(1-p^{-2i-1})
when
n
is odd. (ii) (independence assumption) We next assume that the statistics of
M_{n}
reduced to modulo
p
are asymptotically mutually independent for all primes
p
. Under these assumptions, as
n\to\infty
, the probability that
M_{n}
is surjective would be asymptotically the product of all of the surjectivity probabilities modulo
p
, which leads to the conjecture that
\mathbf{Cok}(M_{n})
is cyclic with asymptotic probability
\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\zeta(2i+1)^{-1}
, which is around 0.7935.
The matrices in this paper do not have to satisfy either assumption, and indeed they can violate them dramatically. For example, if the matrix entries only take values
0
and
1
, then they cannot be uniformly distributed mod any prime
>2
, and the matrix entries mod
3
are not only not independent from the entries mod
5
, but they are in fact determined by the entries mod
5
.

One of the main goals of this paper is to show that the heuristic gives a correct prediction even when the matrices fail the above assumptions dramatically.
The result for the probability of cyclicity was stated in Theorem
1.3
.
We can in fact show a little bit more, similarly to Theorem
1.9
, and one of our main results is the following.

Theorem 1.12
.

Let
M_{n}=M_{n\times n}
be random symmetric matrices as in Theorem
1.3
.
Let
B
be a finite abelian group and let
k_{0}
be larger than any prime divisor of
|B|
, and define
C_{B}=\{B\times C\,|\,C\textrm{ cyclic, }p\nmid|C|\textrm{ for }1<p<k_{0}\}
, the set of groups differing from
B
by a cyclic group with order only divisible by primes at least
k_{0}
.
Then, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\mathbf{Cok}(M_{n\times n})\in C_{B}\Big)=\frac{\#\{\mbox{symmetric, bilinear, perfect }\phi:B\times B\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}\}}{|B||\mathbf{Aut}(B)|}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<k_{0}\\
p\textrm{ prime}\end{subarray}}(1-p^{-1})\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\zeta(2i+1)^{-1}.

1.3.
Cokernels of skew-symmetric matrices

Now we discuss another random matrix model whose cokernel universality aspect has not been addressed in the literature
2
2

2

We refer the reader to
[
MR
,
SS16
]
and the references therein for universality aspects of the empirical spectral distribution of these random matrices.
. Let
A_{n}=(x_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
be an skew-symmetric (i.e. alternating) random matrix where
x_{ij},
for
1\leq i<j\leq n
are i.i.d. copies of an
\alpha
-balanced random integer
\xi
(and
x_{ii}=0
and
x_{ji}=-x_{ij}
). In the special case that
\xi_{ij}
are i.i.d. taking values in
\{-1,0,1\}
, we can view
A_{n}
as the adjacency matrix of a random tournament graph on
n
vertices.
The cokernel distribution of Haar distributed skew-symmetric matrices over
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
was studied in
[
BKL+15
, Theorem 3.9]
in connection to heuristics for various statistics of elliptic curves.

To introduce our results, we will need some more notation. We let
{\bf 1}_{n\textrm{ odd}}
be
1
if
n
is odd and
0
otherwise. For a finite set of primes
P
,
we say a group is a
P
-group
if its order is a product of powers of primes in
P
, and we
let
\mathcal{S}_{P}
be the set of
P
-groups in
\mathcal{S}
(squares of abelian groups). A finite abelian group has a non-degenerate skew-symmetric linear pairing to
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
if and only if it is of the form
H\times H
(see
[
Del01
, Proposition 2]
). A group
G
of this form has a unique such pairing up to isomorphism, and we let
\operatorname{Sp}(G)
be the group of automorphisms of
G
that preserve the pairing. If
A
is a skew-symmetric integral matrix, then it has even rank and

\mathbf{Cok}_{\operatorname{tors}}(A)
, the set of elements of
\mathbf{Cok}(A)
of finite order, is in
\mathcal{S}

[
BKL+15
, Sections 3.4 and 3.5]
.

In this paper, we first show the following local statistics analogous to Theorems
1.6
and
1.10
.

Theorem 1.13
.

Let
A_{n}=(x_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
be a random skew-symmetric matrix where the upper diagonal entries are
x_{ij},
for
i>j
are i.i.d. copies of an
\alpha
-balanced random integer
\xi
.
Let
B
be any finite abelian group, and let
P
be a finite set of primes including all those that divide
|B|
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(A_{n})=n-{\bf 1}_{n\textrm{ odd}})

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}((\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n}))_{P}\simeq B)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{|B|}{|\operatorname{Sp}(B)|}\prod_{p\in P}\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1})&B\in\mathcal{S}_{P}\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}((\mathbf{Cok}_{\operatorname{tors}}(A_{2n+1}))_{P}\simeq B)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{|\operatorname{Sp}(B)|}\prod_{p\in P}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1})&B\in\mathcal{S}_{P}\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Furthermore, for any prime
p
and non-negative integer
r
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(A_{2n}/p)=2n-2r)

\displaystyle=p^{-r(2r-1)}\frac{\prod_{i=r}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1})}{\prod_{k=1}^{r}(1-p^{-2k})}

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(A_{2n+1}/p)=2n-2r)

\displaystyle=p^{-r(2r+1)}\frac{\prod_{i=r+1}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1})}{\prod_{k=1}^{r}(1-p^{-2k})}.

The distribution of the torsion of the cokernels is different for odd and even dimensional matrices. Note the additional factor of
|B|
in the even case, and well as the normalization constants starting their products in different places.

Remark 1.14
.

Similarly to Remarks
1.7
and
1.11
, for a particular finite abelian group
B
we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})\simeq B)=0.

Then, for global statistics we prove Theorem
1.4
, giving the cyclicity probability in the even dimensional case, and the non-zero probabilities of each group in the odd dimensional case.

Remark 1.15
.

In contrast to Remark
1.14
, we have that the probabilities of Theorem
1.4
for the odd dimensional case in (
1.5
) sum to
1

[
Del01
, Theorem 9]
. Thus one can, using Fatou’s lemma, determine the asymptotic probability that the cokernel has any property (e.g. cyclicity) by summing the probabilities of the groups with that property (see
[
NW22
, Lemma 2.4]
).

Additionally, analogously to Theorems
1.9
and
1.12
, one of our main results is the following.

Theorem 1.16
.

Let
A_{n}
be as in Theorem
1.4
.
Let
B\in{\mathcal{S}}
be a finite abelian group and let
k_{0}
be larger than any prime divisor of
|B|
, and define
C_{B}=\{B\times C\times C\,|\,C\textrm{ cyclic, }p\nmid|C|\textrm{ for }1<p<k_{0}\}
. Then, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})\in C_{B}\Big)=\frac{|B|}{|\operatorname{Sp}(B)|}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\zeta(2i+1)^{-1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<k_{0}\\
p\textrm{ prime}\end{subarray}}(1-p^{-1})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq k_{0}\\
p\textrm{ prime}\end{subarray}}(1-p^{-2})^{-1}(1-p^{-2}+p^{-3}).

1.4.
Laplacian of random graphs

Now we turn to one of our main motivating applications. Given a matrix
M=(x_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
, let
L_{M}
be the Laplacian corresponding to
M
, with entries

(1.17)

L_{ij}=\begin{cases}x_{ij}&\mbox{ if }j\neq i\\
-\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq n\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}x_{ki}}&\mbox{ if }j=i,\end{cases}

and note the columns of
L_{M}
all sum to
0
.
Let
{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}
be the set of vectors in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
of zero-sum. We write
S_{M}
for the cokernel
{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}^{n}/L_{M}{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
.
In this paper we will be focusing on the case when
M
is the adjacency matrix of a graph
G
(directed or undirected), in which case we write
L_{G}
for
L_{M}
. In this case this abelian group, denoted by
S_{G}
, is called the
sandpile group
(or the Jacobian, the Picard group, the critical group) of
G
. It is well known that the order of
S_{G}
is the number of spanning trees of
G
when
G
is undirected, and there is a similar result for rooted spanning trees when
G
is directed.
Note that the study of sandpile groups for directed and undirected graphs and their implications has been an extremely active research direction in recent years. (There is a vast literature on this, which is impossible to list even a small portion of it; we refer the reader to for instance
[
BN09
,
AV12
,
FL16a
,
FL16b
,
GK19
]
and the references therein.)
The behavior of
S_{G}
in general is highly non-trivial, for instance it is not clear if there is a way to relate
S_{G}
to
S_{G^{\prime}}
if they differ by only a few graph theoretic operations! The task of describing
S_{G}
for a given
G
is naturally daunting, and it is perhaps too complicated to wish for a theory to describe
S_{G}
properly for most
G
. In another direction, it is natural to trade off a detailed description of each
G
to have a general pictures on which group structures they might have.

Motivated by this, the current authors showed the following analog of Theorem
1.6
for sandpile groups of random directed graphs.

Theorem 1.18
.

[
NW22
, Theorem 1.6]
Let
G
be a random directed Erdős-Rényi graph where each directed edge is chosen independently with probability
1/2
.
Let
B
be a finite abelian group. Then

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(S_{G}\simeq B\Big)=\frac{1}{|B||\mathbf{Aut}(B)|}\prod_{i=2}^{\infty}\zeta(i)^{-1}.

Remark 1.19
.

As in Remark
1.15
, these limiting probabilities sum to
1

[
Woo19
, Lemma 3.2]
and thus imply limiting probabilities of any other properties.

This result has an interesting combinatorial application
that about
45.58\%
of simple digraphs
G=(V,E)
has the property that a chip configuration
\sigma
on
\Gamma
stabilizes after a finite number of legal firings if and only if
|\sigma|\leq|E|-|V|
; we refer the reader to
[
FL16a
,
NW22
]
for further details. Notice that the results in
[
NW22
]
also extend to the regime that
\alpha
is allowed to depend on
n
(such as
\alpha_{n}\geq n^{-1+o(1)}
and to rectangular matrices, and other important statistics).

Now we turn to undirected graphs.
For local statistics, the second author showed the following analog of Theorem
1.10

Theorem 1.20
.

[
Woo17
, Theorem 1.1]
Let
G\in G(n,1/2)

be an Erdős-Rényi graph on
n
vertices. Let
B
be a finite abelian group. Let
P
be a finite set of primes including all those dividing

|B|
. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}((S_{G})_{P}\simeq B)=\frac{\#\{\mbox{symmetric, bilinear, perfect }\phi:B\times B\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}\}}{|B||\mathbf{Aut}(B)|}\prod_{p\in P}\prod_{k\geq 0}(1-p^{-2k-1}).

Definition of
L_{n}
:

Sometimes it is more convenient to work with the model
L_{n}
, obtained from
L_{G}=L_{M}
(see (
1.17
)) where
M
is the adjacency matrix of
G
, an Erdős-Rényi graph on
n+1
vertices, by deleting the last row and column. More precisely, we let
x_{ij}
be independent copies of a uniform random element of
\{0,1\}
for
1\leq i<j\leq n+1
and let
L_{n}
be the
n\times n
matrix with entries

(1.21)

L_{ij}=\begin{cases}x_{ij}&\mbox{ if }1\leq i<j\leq n\\
x_{ji}&\mbox{ if }1\leq j<i\leq n\\
-\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq n+1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}x_{ki}}&\mbox{ if }1\leq i=j\leq n.\end{cases}

Unlike
L_{G}
, the determinant of
L_{n}
is not necessarily zero.
When
G
is an undirected graph, projection onto the first
n
coordinates gives an isomorphism
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n+1}_{0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
that induces an isomorphism
\mathbf{Cok}(L_{n})\simeq S_{G}
(where

\mathbf{Cok}(L_{n})={\mathbb{Z}}^{n}/L_{n}{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
). (This happens when
G
is undirected because
L_{G}
has rows summing to
0
, so the last column does not contribute to the column space.)

We note that the cyclicity event considered in Theorem
1.1
applied to
L_{n}
(and its Smith Normal Form) is equivalent with the event that the greatest common divisor of all
(n-1)\times(n-1)
minors of
L_{n}
is 1. Hence Theorem
1.1
asserts that this event has probability
\approx.7935
as well.
The global statistics of Theorem
1.1
are one of the central applications of the new methods of this paper.
We can also extend Theorem
1.12
to the model
S_{G}
and
L_{n}
.

Theorem 1.22
.

Let
G\in G(n,1/2)

be an Erdős-Rényi graph on
n
vertices.
Let
B
be a finite abelian group and let
k_{0}
be larger than any prime divisor of
|B|
, and define
C_{B}=\{B\times C\,|\,C\textrm{ cyclic, }p\nmid|C|\textrm{ for }1<p<k_{0}\}
. Then, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(S_{G}\in C_{B})=\frac{\#\{\mbox{symmetric, bilinear, perfect }\phi:B\times B\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}\}}{|B||\mathbf{Aut}(B)|}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<k_{0}\\
p\textrm{ prime}\end{subarray}}(1-p^{-1})\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\zeta(2i+1)^{-1}

and similarly for
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(\mathbf{Cok}(L_{n})\in C_{B})
.

1.5.
Proof methods

Our approach is to study the cokernels over all primes in order to understand the cokernels over
{\mathbb{Z}}
. For each
n
, the approach we use for each prime
p
depends on the size of
p
relative to
n
.

•

(Small primes, local statistics, Sections
3
and
4
) In our first interval of primes we use Theorem
1.10
, Theorem
1.13
, and Theorem
1.20
to study the
P
-parts of the cokernels, where
P
is a product of a few fixed primes, and
n\to\infty
.
In this paper, we only need to prove Theorem
1.13
for the skew-symmetric case. In
[
Woo17
]
, the second author proved the local statistics for symmetric matrices by determining the (group-theoretic) moments of the distribution and proving that moments determine a unique distribution when they don’t grow too quickly. In this paper, we find the moments in the skew-symmetric case, but they grow too quickly to determine a unique distribution. Indeed, even and odd dimensional skew-symmetric matrices have the same moments but very different distributions. However, one can leverage further deterministic information about the group structures that can arise to prove that under those restrictions (which are different in the odd and even dimensional cases) that the moments indeed determine a unique distribution.

•

(Moderate primes, dynamics and rank statistics, Sections
5
,
6
, and
7
) Next we study the cokernel modulo a prime
p
as long as
p
is sufficiently large and
p\leq\exp(n^{c})
for some small constant
c
. Here we provide a very fine approximation of the rank evolution by a combinatorial method, which uses some ingredients from
[
FJ19
,
FJLS21
,
KN22
]
and
[
LMN20
]
. This method centers around the study of non-structureness of the normal vectors of random subspaces spanned by the columns (Propositions
5.18
,
5.19
). One of the most challenging parts here is to find the right notion of structures, for which we can estimate very precisely the number of structured vectors even when
p
is sub-exponentially large and when the matrix entries are not dependent, especially in the Laplacian case.

•

(Large primes, simultaneously trivial statistics, Sections
8
and
9
) In the last stage we study primes
\exp(n^{c})<p<n^{n/2}
. We provide an inverse result characterizing quadratic forms of large concentration probability by building on our previous works
[
Ngu12
,
NW22
]
. Two highlights of this part for the Laplacian case include the passing of certain rare events from all such large
p
simultaneously to an event over
{\mathbb{Z}}
(Lemma
9.18
), and the innovative part of passing from the Laplacian model to the random symmetric one with prescribed diagonal entries via Lemmas
9.9
,
9.10
. Here, unlike the combinatorial structures used in the moderate prime parts, our structures are arithmetic (GAP), an extremely important structure that we must have to pass to all primes.

As mentioned, for moderate and large primes, the Laplacian model poses a significant challenge because the diagonal entries of this model depend on all other entries. For instance, although this is not our main focus, to our best understanding it is not even known before our work that the Laplacian matrix
L_{n}
is non-singular with sub-exponentially high probability, see Corollary
7.18
. Beside the highlights above, among our other technical contributions, the proofs of Lemma
6.5
, Proposition
6.29
, and Lemma
9.10
involve “structure propagation”, a way to deal with partially structured vectors by a series of conditionings. This method seems to be useful and of independent interest.

1.6.
Notations

We use
[n]
for
\{1,\dots,n\}
. For an index set
I\subset[n]
, we write
I^{c}
for the complement of
I
in
[n]
. We denote the order of groups and sets using either absolute value signs
|\cdot|
or
\#
.

Probability:
We write
{\mathbf{P}}
for probability and
{\mathbf{E}}
for expected value. For an event
\mathcal{E}
, we write
\bar{\mathcal{E}}
for its complement.
We use
\wedge
for logical
and
.

Analysis:
We write
\exp(x)
for the exponential function
e^{x}
. We write
\|.\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}
to be the distance to the nearest integer. Throughout this paper, if not specified otherwise,
\alpha,A,C,C_{\ast},c,c^{\prime},c_{i},K,T,\delta,\eta,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}
, etc,
will denote positive constants. When it does not create confusion, the same letter may denote different constants in different parts of the proof.
The value of the constants may depend on other constants we have chosen, but will never depend on the dimension
n
, which is regarded as an asymptotic parameter going to infinity. More specifically, we say “
f(n,\dots)=O_{S}(g(n,\dots))
”, or “
f(n,\dots)\ll_{S}g(n,\dots)
”, where
S
is a subset of the parameters, to mean for any values
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
of the parameters in
S
,
there is exists a constant
K>0
depending on
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
, such that for all
n
,
|f(n,\dots)|\leq Kg(n,\dots).

Also, we write
f(n,\dots)=\Theta_{S}(g(n,\dots))
if
f(n,\dots)=O_{S}(g(n,\dots))
and
g(n,\dots)=O_{S}(f(n,\dots))
. In many cases
S
can be empty, in which case “
f(n,\dots)=O(g(n,\dots))
”, or “
f(n,\dots)\ll g(n,\dots)
”, means
|f(n,\dots)|\leq Kg(n,\dots)
where
K
is an absolute positive constant. We also write
k=\omega(1)
if
k\to\infty
with
n
.

Linear Algebra:
For a vector
{\mathbf{w}}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})
we let
\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{w}})=\{i\in[n]|w_{i}\neq 0\}
. We will also write
X\cdot{\mathbf{w}}
for the dot product
\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}w_{i}
.
We say
{\mathbf{w}}
is a
normal
vector for a subspace
H
if
X\cdot{\mathbf{w}}=0
for every
X\in H
. For a given index set
J\subset[n]
and a vector
X=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, we write
X|_{J}
or sometimes
X_{J}
to be the subvector of
X
of components indexed from
J
. In this case we say
\operatorname{supp_{index}}(X_{J})=J
(that is
\operatorname{supp_{index}}(.)
gives the index set for all the coordinates of a vector, even those that are
0
), and
|X_{J}|
, the dimension of
X_{J}
, is simply
|J|
.
Similarly, if
H
is a subspace of
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{n}
then
H|_{J}
or
H_{J}
is the subspace spanned by
X|_{J}
for
X\in H
. Finally, for
I,J\subset[n]
, the matrix
M_{I\times J}
is the submatrix of the rows and columns indexed from
I
and
J
respectively. Sometimes we will also write
M_{n}
for
M_{n\times n}
if there is no confusion. Sometimes, for a matrix
M
we write
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(M)
and
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}(M)
for the
i
-th row and column respectively.

For a prime
p
, we write
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
for the finite field with
p
elements.
For a matrix
M
with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}
, we write
M/p
for the matrix with coefficients in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
obtained from
M
by reduction mod
p
.

Group Theory
: The exponent of a finite abelian group is the smallest positive integer
a
such that
aG=0
. For a prime
p
, a finite abelian
p
-group is isomorphic to

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}{\mathbb{Z}}/p^{\lambda_{i}}{\mathbb{Z}}
for some positive integers
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{r}
.
We call the partition
\lambda
the
type
of the abelian
p
-group.
The symmetric power
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G
is defined to be the quotient of
G\otimes G
by the subgroup generated by elements of the form
g_{1}\otimes g_{2}-g_{2}\otimes g_{1}
.
If
G_{p}
is type
\lambda
, generated by
e_{i}
with relations
p^{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}=0
, then

\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G_{p}
is generated by the
e_{i}e_{j}
for
i\leq j
with relations

p^{\lambda_{j}}e_{i}e_{j}=0
.
Similarly,
\wedge^{2}G
is defined to be the quotient of
G\otimes G
by the subgroup generated by elements of the form
g\otimes g
, and
\wedge^{2}G_{\lambda}=\oplus_{i}({\mathbb{Z}}/p^{\lambda_{i}}{\mathbb{Z}})^{i-1}
. We write
\langle g_{1},\dots\rangle
for the subgroup generated by
g_{1},\dots
.

Random groups
: For a random group
X
, the
G
-moment
of
X
is
{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(X,G))
, where
\operatorname{Sur}(X,G)
denotes the surjective group homomorphisms from
X
to
G
.

1.7.
Laplacian sampling

We now introduce introduce various equivalent models of Laplacian matrices to be used.

Laplacian models:
for undirected graphs we will sample the Laplacian as follows.

Definition 1.23

(Laplacian for random graphs)
.

The model
L_{n}=(L_{ij})
can be obtained via two phases of randomness.

•

Phase 1: Assume that the vertices of
G=G_{n+1}
are ordered as
{\mathcal{O}}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{n},v_{n+1})
. We first sample
x_{ij}
(via
G(n+1,1/2)
) and compute the degrees
d_{1},\dots,d_{n+1}
of the vertices, and then subtract those from the diagonals of
M_{G}
to form
L_{G}
. We delete the last row and column to form
L_{n}
. Notice that hence the column vectors of
L_{n}
are not necessarily orthogonal to
\mathbf{1}=(1,\dots,1)
.

•

Phase 2: Given an ordering
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}=(v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{n}^{\prime},v_{n+1})
of the vertices, we reshuffle the neighbors of the consecutive vertex pairs adapted to
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
as follows.
For
N=0,\dots,n-1
,
we make the following modifications to the graph.
We consider the set
I_{N}
of indices
1\leq j\leq N
(or vertices
v_{i}^{\prime}
from
\{v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{N}^{\prime}\}
) where
v_{j}^{\prime}
is connected to
exactly one
of
v_{N+1}^{\prime}
or
v_{N+2}^{\prime}
. Then for each
j\in I_{N}
,
we flip a fair coin to either keep or swap whether each of
(v_{j}^{\prime},v_{N+1})
and
(v_{j}^{\prime},v_{N+2})
is an edge (see Figure
1
).
In other words, if
X=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
and
Y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{N})
are the (restricted) column vectors associated to
v_{N+1}^{\prime}
and
v_{N+2}^{\prime}
, then
I_{N}
is the collection of indices
j
where
(x_{j},y_{j})=(0,1)
or
(1,0)
. We then flip a fair coin to reassign
(x_{j},y_{j})
to
(0,1)
or
(1,0)
. We iterate this process until
N=n
, and call this model
L_{n}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
.

Definition 1.24
.

Let
L_{n}
be the random matrix (reduced Laplacian) defined above in terms of the random integers
x_{ij}
. For
A,B
disjoint subsets of
[n+1]
, and an involution
b\mapsto\bar{b}
on
B
,
we define the
AB
-shuffle
of
L_{n}
to be the
n\times n
matrix
L^{\prime}_{n}
with entries as follows.
For each
i\in A
, we (independently) make a random set
B_{i}
by putting each orbit of
B
under the involution in
B_{i}
independently with probability
1/2
. Then, for
1\leq i\neq j\leq n+1
we define random integers

x^{\prime}_{ij}=x_{i\bar{j}}
for
i\in A
and
j\in B_{i}
; and

x^{\prime}_{ij}=x_{\bar{i}j}
for
j\in A
and
i\in B_{j}
; and
x^{\prime}_{ij}=x_{ij}
for all other
i\neq j
.
We then define
L^{\prime}_{n}
as in (
1.21
) with the
x^{\prime}_{ij}
replacing the
x_{ij}
.

One can see that for any
A,B,
the
AB
-shuffle of
L_{n}
has the same distribution as
L_{n}
by considering the probability of obtaining any particular matrix.
Critically for our applications, the
AB
-shuffle leaves all the diagonal entries of the
([n]\setminus B)\times([n]\setminus B)
submatrix of
L_{n}
fixed.
We can use the same definition to apply the
AB
-shuffle to our other models
M_{n}
and
A_{n}
, and we sometimes do in order to give a proof for all three cases at once, but the shuffle is never necessary in these cases.

I_{N}
.
v_{j}^{\prime}
.
v_{N+1}^{\prime}
.
v_{N+2}^{\prime}
I_{N}
.
v_{j}^{\prime}
.
v_{N+1}^{\prime}
.
v_{N+2}^{\prime}

Figure 1
.
Swapping neighbors.

To some extent, our reshuffling is similar to
[
McK81
]
and
[
Coo17
,
LLT+21
]
where shufflings/switchings were used within random graphs and random matrices. However our implementation here is rather straightforward. In Phase 1, by Chernoff’s bound

(1.25)

{\mathbf{P}}(\wedge_{i=1}^{n+1}(n/2-t\sqrt{n}\leq d_{i}\leq n/2+t\sqrt{n})\geq 1-2n\exp(-2t^{2}),t>0.

It is clear that the neighbor reshuffling process in Phase 2 does not change the distribution of
L_{n}
, which we summarize below

Fact 1.26

(Equivalence of models)
.

The distribution of
L_{n}
obtained in Phase 1, is the same as the distribution of
L_{n}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})

obtained in Phase 2, for any ordering
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show for
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}=(v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{n}^{\prime},v_{n+1})
,
that step
N
of our reshuffling does not change the distribution of the Erdős-Rényi graph
G(n+1,1/2)
or its adjacency matrix.
However, it is easy to see that conditioned on the upper-left
N\times N
submatrix of the adjacency matrix, after the shuffling at step
N
,
every possible upper-left
(N+2)\times(N+2)
submatrix of the adjacency matrix is equally likely, which proves the claim.
∎

Additionally, we will also rely on the following Chernoff’s bound which says that the degrees are near
n/2

and the reshuffling process significantly creates extra randomness,. For
c>0
, we have

(1.27)

{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\wedge_{k\geq cn}|I_{k}|\in(k/2-t\sqrt{n},k/2+t\sqrt{n})\Big)\geq 1-n\exp(-\Theta_{c}(t^{2})),t>0.

In later applications we will choose either
t=c\sqrt{n}
or
t=n^{1/2-c}
for a small constant
c
. To end the discussion, in Section
9
we will also make use of the following model (of random graphs of given degree sequence). Let
c>0
be a constant, and assume that
(1/2-c)n\leq d_{i}\leq(1/2+c)n
for all
1\leq i\leq n+1
. The number of such degree sequences
{\mathbf{d}}
is simply bounded by
(2cn)^{n+1}
. Thus with
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{d}}}
being the collection of all simple graphs on
n+1
vertices with degrees
{\mathbf{d}}
, we have

\sum_{{\mathbf{d}},{\mathbf{P}}(G(n+1,1/2)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{d}}})<n^{-4n}}{\mathbf{P}}\big(G(n+1,1/2)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{d}}}\big)\leq(2cn)^{n+1}n^{-4n}\leq n^{2n}.

Hence, conditioning on the event
(1/2-c)n\leq d_{i}\leq(1/2+c)n
for all
1\leq i\leq n+1
, it is natural to only focus on degree sequences
{\mathbf{d}}
where

{\mathbf{P}}\big(G(n+1,1/2)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{d}}}\big)\geq n^{-4n}.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{g}=\mathcal{D}_{g}(c,n)
denote the collection of such degree sequences.

Definition 1.28

(Laplacian for random graphs of good degree sequence)
.

Let
c>0
be given sufficiently small.

•

Phase 1: we will choose each
{\mathbf{d}}
from the degree sequence
\mathcal{D}_{g}
with probability

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbf{d}}\text{ is sampled})=\frac{{\mathbf{P}}\big(G(n+1,1/2)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{d}}}\big)}{\sum_{{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{g}}{\mathbf{P}}\big(G(n+1,1/2)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}}\big)}.

•

Phase 2: we sample a random graph
G
from
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{d}}}
with probability

\frac{{\mathbf{P}}\big(G(n+1,1/2)=G\big)}{{\mathbf{P}}\big(G(n+1,1/2)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{d}}}\big)}.

•

We then obtain
L_{n}
from the Laplacian of
G
as usual.

2.
Proof of the global statistics from the main inputs

In this section we give the proofs of Theorems
1.3
,
1.4
and
1.1

from their main inputs, which will then be the focus of the rest of the paper.
As mentioned, for the small primes we will use Theorems
1.10
and
1.20
on symmetric matrices and graph Laplacians, and we will prove
Theorem
1.13
on skew-symmetric matrices in Section
4
. For the moderate primes and for the symmetric and Laplacian models we show the following.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
G_{n}
be either
M_{n}
as in Theorem
1.10
or
L_{n}
as in Theorem
1.22
. There exists a sufficiently small
c>0
such that the following holds for every prime
p\leq e^{n^{c}}

(2.2)

{\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(G_{n}/p)\leq n-2)=O(\frac{1}{p^{3}}+e^{-n^{c}}).

The behavior for the skew-symmetric case is slightly different, as the ranks are always even.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
A_{n}
be as in Theorem
1.13
.
There exists a sufficiently small
c>0
such that the following holds for every prime
p\leq e^{n^{c}}
:

(2.4)

{\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(A_{n}/p)\leq n-3)=O(\frac{1}{p^{3}}+e^{-n^{c}}).

Even such a small error bound cannot be summed over all primes, and so for very large primes we consider all primes together. Here we separate into two cases, for the symmetric and Laplacian model we show the following.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
G_{n}
be either
M_{n}
as in Theorem
1.10
or
L_{n}
as in Theorem
1.22
. For any given
C,c>0
the following holds for sufficiently large
n

(2.6)

{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\exists\mbox{ prime }p\geq e^{n^{c}}:{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{n}/p)\leq n-2\Big)\leq n^{-C}.

For the skew-symmetric case we show the following.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
A_{n}
be as in Theorem
1.13
. For any given
C,c>0
, the following holds for sufficiently large
n

(2.8)

{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\exists\mbox{ prime }p\geq e^{n^{c}}:{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{n}/p)\leq n-3\Big)\leq n^{-C}.

Propositions
2.1
and
2.3
will follow from our work in Sections
5
,
6
, and
7
, while Propositions
2.5
and
2.7
will be justified in Sections
8
and
9
.
We will now show how these results can be combined to obtain our main results.

Proof of Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
.

We will use the fact that
\mathbf{Cok}(G_{n})
(where
G_{n}
is either the symmetric
M_{n}
or the Laplacian
L_{n}
) is cyclic if and only if for every prime
p
, the matrix
G
mod
p
has rank at least
n-1
.
Fix an integer
k_{0}
.
By Proposition
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}\Big({\operatorname{rank}}(G_{n}/p)\leq n-2\mbox{ for some $k_{0}\leq p\leq e^{n^{c/2}}$ }\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=k_{0}}^{e^{n^{c/2}}}O(p^{-3}+e^{-n^{c}})

\displaystyle=O(\frac{1}{k_{0}^{2}}+e^{-n^{c/2}}).

Combined with Proposition
2.5
we obtain

{\mathbf{P}}\Big({\operatorname{rank}}(G_{n}/p)\leq n-2\mbox{ for some }p\geq k_{0}\Big)=O(\frac{1}{k_{0}^{2}}+e^{-n^{c/2}}+n^{-C}).

Let
P
be the set of primes
<k_{0}
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be the set of all cyclic abelian
P
-groups.
We note that the probabilities in Theorems
1.10
and
1.20
sum to
1
over all
P
-groups (e.g., from
[
CKL+15
, Proposition 7]

and the orbit-stabilizer theorem as in the proof of
[
Woo17
, Corollary 9.2]
).
Thus, as in Remark
1.15
, we can determine the asymptotic probability that
(\mathbf{Cok}(G_{n}))_{P}\in\mathcal{C}
by summing the probabilities of Theorem
1.10
over all groups in
\mathcal{C}
, which is done in
[
CKL+15
, Proposition 9]
(note the sum factors over primes
p
).
So we conclude,

(2.9)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}((\mathbf{Cok}(G_{n}))_{P}\in\mathcal{C})=\prod_{p<k_{0}}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1}).

As this is true for any fixed
k_{0}
, we can take
k_{0}\rightarrow\infty
and combine with the bounds for
p\geq k_{0}
to obtain

\liminf_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\mathbf{Cok}(G_{n})\mbox{ is cyclic}\Big)\geq\prod_{p\textrm{ prime}}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1})=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\zeta(2i+1)^{-1}.

The upper bound for
\limsup
follows from (
2.9
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

The group
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})
is of the form
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2m}\times G
, for some
G\in\mathcal{S}
.
The even rank follows from the fact that the rank of a skew-symmetric matrix is even. The condition on the torsion part follows from the fact that the torsion has a skew-symmetric non-degenerate perfect pairing (
[
BKL+15
, Sections 3.4 and 3.5]
and
[
Del01
, Proposition 2]
).
Thus
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})

is the square of a cyclic group if and only if for every prime
p
, the matrix
A_{2n}
mod
p
has rank at least
2n-2
.
The rest of the proof is exactly like the proof of Theorems
1.3
and
1.1
above, using
Theorem
1.13
and Propositions
2.3
and
2.7
as input.
To see that the probabilities in Theorem
1.13
sum to
1
, we can use an argument as in
[
Del01
, Theorem 9]
, but only taking the product over finitely many primes. Then, to compute the sum of the probabilities over squares of cyclic
P
-groups, we can reason as in
[
Del01
, Example E]
.

The group
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})
is of the form
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2m+1}\times G
, for some
G\in\mathcal{S}
, by the same reasoning as in the even dimensional case.
If
P
is the set of primes
<k_{0}
, where we take
k_{0}
larger than the largest prime dividing
C
, then we have that
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}\times C

if and only if
(\mathbf{Cok}_{\operatorname{tors}}(A_{2n+1}))_{P}\simeq C
and for all primes
p\geq k_{0}
we have

{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{2n+1}/p)\geq(2n+1)-2
. Then the proof follows as above, using Theorem
1.13
,
Propositions
2.3
and
2.7
, and
[
Del01
, Theorem 9]
.
∎

The proofs of Theorem
1.12
, Theorem
1.16
, and Theorem
1.22
are very analogous.

3.
Treatment for small primes: determination of the moments

We will prove Theorem
1.13
by finding the moments (as random groups, see
[
CKL+15
, Section 3.3]
) of the distribution
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})
, in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
A_{n}
be as in Theorem
1.13
, and
G
be any finite abelian group. We have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{E}}(|\operatorname{Sur}(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{n}),G)|)=|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G|.

However, these moments are exactly large enough that they do not determine a unique distribution. In particular, the moments
do not see whether
n
is even or odd, yet we know the distributions of
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{n})
are quite different in these cases because they are usually finite groups when
n
is even
and always infinite groups when
n
is odd. So we will prove a new theorem on the moment problem for finite abelian groups to show that when we take into account this further information, that a unique distribution is determined by the moments.

3.1.
Proof of Theorem
3.1

In fact, we will prove the rate of convergence in Theorem
3.1
is exponential in
n
.
The proof of this follows the proof of
[
Woo17
, Theorem 1.2]
closely, which is the analogous result for symmetric matrices.
Only small modifications are required, so we will be brief.

Let
a
be a positive integer and let
G
be a finite abelian group with
aG=0
. Let
R
be the ring
{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For an
R
-module
A
, let
A^{*}:=\operatorname{Hom}(A,R)
.
We define the
R
-module
V=R^{n}
, with a distinguished basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
of
V
, and a dual basis
v_{1}^{*},\dots,v_{n}^{*}
of
V^{*}
.
We have

(3.2)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}(|\operatorname{Sur}(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{n}),G|)=\sum_{F\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G)}{\mathbf{P}}(FA_{n}=0).

Let
\xi
be a primitive
a
th root of unity. We view
A_{n}
as an element of
\operatorname{Hom}(V^{*},V)
, and have

{\mathbf{P}}(FA_{n}=0)=\frac{1}{|G|^{n}}\sum_{C\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Hom}(V^{*},G),R))}{\mathbf{E}}(\xi^{C(FA_{n})}).

We have a natural isomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Hom}(V^{*},G),R))\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(V,G^{*})
and so we often view
C
in this latter group.
We write
e:G^{*}\times G\rightarrow R
for the map that evaluates a homomorphism.
Since
A_{n}
is skew-symmetric, we have

\displaystyle C(FA_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}e(C(v_{j}),F(v_{i}))x_{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}(e(C(v_{j}),F(v_{i}))-e(C(v_{i}),F(v_{j})))x_{ij}.

For
i<j
we define,

E(C,F,i,j):=e(C(v_{j}),F(v_{i}))-e(C(v_{i}),F(v_{j}))
, and so

(3.3)

{\mathbf{P}}(FA_{n}=0)=\frac{1}{|G|^{n}}\sum_{C\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Hom}(V^{*},G),R))}\prod_{i\leq j}{\mathbf{E}}(\xi^{E(C,F,i,j)x_{ij}}).

We will show many of these
E(C,F,i,j)
coefficients are non-zero.

For
F\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G)
and
C\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G^{*})
, we have a map

\phi_{F,C}\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G\oplus G^{*})
given by adding
F
and
C
.
Similarly, we have a map
\phi_{C,F}\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G^{*}\oplus G)
given by adding
C
and
F
.
Note has
V
has distinguished submodules
V_{\sigma}
generated by the
v_{i}
with
i\not\in\sigma
for each
\sigma\subset[n]
. So
V_{\sigma}
comes from not using the coordinates in
\sigma
.
Clearly, for any submodule
U
of
V
,

\ker(\phi_{F,C}|_{U})\subset\ker(F|_{U}).

Now we will define the key structural properties of
C
and
F
that determines if enough of the coefficients
E(C,F,i,j)
are non-zero.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
0<\gamma<1
be a real number which we will specify later in the proof.
Given
F
, we say
C
is
robust
(for
F
) if for every
\sigma\subset[n]
with
|\sigma|<\gamma n
,

\ker(\phi_{F,C}|_{V_{\sigma}})\neq\ker(F|_{V_{\sigma}}).

Otherwise, we say
C
is
weak
for
F
.

We say that
F\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G)
is a
code
of distance
w
, if for every
\sigma\subset[n]
with
|\sigma|<w
, we have
FV_{\sigma}=G
.
In other words,
F
is not only surjective, but would still be surjective if we throw out (any) fewer than
w
of the standard basis vectors from
V
.

For
C=0
, of course all the
E(C,F,i,j)
are
0
. However, given
F
, there are other
C
for which this can happen, and next we will identify those
C
.
Given an
F\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G)
, we have a map

\displaystyle m_{F}:\operatorname{Hom}(V,G^{*})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\wedge^{2}V^{*}

(3.5)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}(e(C(v_{j}),F(v_{i}))-e(C(v_{i}),F(v_{j})))v_{i}^{*}\wedge v_{j}^{*}.

We now determine some elements
C\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G^{*})
that are in the kernel of
m_{F}
, i.e. all the
E(C,F,i,j)
are
0
. The following construction corrects the construction in
[
Woo17
]
and translates it to the skew-symmetric case.
Let
\operatorname{Sym}(G\otimes G,R)
denote the subset of
\operatorname{Hom}(G\otimes G,R)
that are symmetric, i.e.
\alpha
such that
\alpha(g_{1}\otimes g_{2})=\alpha(g_{2}\otimes g_{1})
for all
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
.
So we have a map

\displaystyle s_{F}:\operatorname{Sym}(G\otimes G,R)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(V,G^{*})

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(v\mapsto(g\mapsto\alpha(F(v)\otimes g))\right).

Using Equation (
3.1
), we will check that
\operatorname{im}(s_{F})\subset\ker(m_{F})
.
The
v_{a}^{*}\wedge v_{b}^{*}
coefficient of
m_{F}(s_{F}(\alpha))
is

\displaystyle e(C(v_{b}),F(v_{a}))-e(C(v_{a}),F(v_{b}))=\alpha(F(v_{b})\otimes F(v_{a}))-\alpha(F(v_{a})\otimes F(v_{b}))=0.

We call the
C
in
\operatorname{im}(s_{F})

special
for
F
.

Lemma 3.6
.

If
FV=G
, then we have that
s_{F}
is injective. In particular,
\#\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G|\#\ker(m_{F})
.

Proof.

Note
|\operatorname{Sym}(G\otimes G,R)|=|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G|
. It suffices to show that
\#\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G|\#\operatorname{im}(s_{F})
.
Since everything in sight can be written as a direct sum of Sylow
p
-subgroups, we can reduce to the case that
G
is a
p
-group of type
\lambda
(and accordingly assume
R=Z/p^{e}{\mathbb{Z}}
). Let
r=\lambda_{1}^{\prime}
.

By
[
Woo17
, Lemma 3.4]
, we can find
\tau\subset[n]
with
|\tau|=r
such that
Fv_{i}
generate
G
for
i\in\tau
. Let
W
be the submodule of
V
generated by the
v_{i}
for
v\in\tau
.
Let
e_{i}
generate
G
with relations
p^{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}=0
.
Let
w_{j}\in W
be such that
Fw_{j}=e_{j}
.
Let
W^{\prime}\subset W
be the subgroup of
W
generated by the
w_{j}
. As in
[
Woo17
, Lemma 3.6]
, by Nakayama’s Lemma we have that
W^{\prime}=W
.
Since the
r
elements
w_{1},\dots,w_{r}
, generate the free rank
r

R
-module
W
, they must be a basis, and we have a dual basis
w_{i}^{*}
of
W^{*}
.

We have that
\operatorname{Hom}(G\otimes G,R)
is generated by
e_{ij}^{*}
with relations

p^{\min(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})}e_{ij}^{*}
and
e_{ij}^{*}(e_{a}\otimes e_{b})
1 if
a=i
and
b=j
and
0
otherwise. Also,

\operatorname{Sym}(G\otimes G,R)
is generated by
e_{ij}^{*}+e_{ji}^{*}
for
i<j
and
e_{ii}^{*}
.
Let
G^{*}
be generated by
e_{1}^{*},\dots,e_{r}^{*}
with relations
p^{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}^{*}=0
,
and such that
e_{i}^{*}e_{i}=p^{e-\lambda_{i}}
, and for
i\neq j
we have
e_{i}^{*}e_{j}=0
.

Recall we have

s_{F}:\operatorname{Sym}(G\otimes G,R)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(V,G^{*}).

We can take the further quotient

s^{\prime}_{F}:\operatorname{Sym}(G\otimes G,R)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(W,G^{*}).

We see that by the definition of
s_{F}

s_{F}(e_{ij}^{*}+e_{ji}^{*})(w_{a})(e_{b})=(e_{ij}^{*}+e_{ji}^{*})(e_{a}\otimes e_{b})

and

s_{F}(e_{ii}^{*})(w_{a})(e_{b})=(e_{ii}^{*})(e_{a}\otimes e_{b})

Recall that since
W
is a free
R
-module, the natural map
W^{*}\otimes G^{*}\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(W,G^{*})
is an isomorphism.
By noting the values on each
w_{a}
and
e_{b}
above, we can confirm that for
i<j

s^{\prime}_{F}(e_{ij}^{*}+e_{ji}^{*})=w_{i}^{*}\otimes e_{j}^{*}+p^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}w_{j}^{*}\otimes e_{i}^{*},.

which has order
p^{\lambda_{j}}
.
Also,

s^{\prime}_{F}(e_{ii}^{*})=w_{i}^{*}\otimes e_{i}^{*}.

which has order
p^{\lambda_{i}}
.
We can conclude that

p^{\lambda_{1}+2\lambda_{2}+2\lambda_{3}+\dots+r\lambda_{r}}\mid\#\operatorname{im}(s^{\prime}_{F})\mid\#\operatorname{im}(s_{F}).

∎

We now give a good bound on the probability that a code descends to a map from the cokernel of a random matrix.

Lemma 3.7
.

Given
\delta>0
, there there is a
c>0

and a real number
K
(depending only on
\alpha,\delta,a,
and
G
)
such that for
F\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G)
a code of distance
\delta n
and
A\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{*},G)
, we have, for all
n
,

\displaystyle\left|{\mathbf{P}}(FA_{n}=0)-|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G||G|^{-n}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K\exp(-cn)}{|G|^{n}}

and

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(FA_{n}=A)\leq K|G|^{-n}.

Proof of Lemma
3.7
.

We closely follow the proof of
[
Woo17
, Lemma 4.1]
.
We have

{\mathbf{P}}(FA_{n}=A)=\frac{1}{|G|^{n}}\sum_{C\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G^{*})}{\mathbf{E}}(\xi^{C(FA_{n}-A)}),

and we break the sum into
3
pieces based on when
G
is special, not special and weak, or robust.

Given
F
, there are
|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G|
special
C
for which
\xi^{C(FA_{n})}=1
for all
A_{n}
by Lemma
3.6
.
In the sum above, these
C
contribute
|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G||G|^{-n}
when
A=0
and at most
|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G||G|^{-n}
in absolute value for any
A
.

From
[
Woo17
, Lemma 3.1]
, we have
that the number of
C\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G^{*})
such that
C
is weak for
F
is at most

C_{G}\binom{n}{\lceil\gamma n\rceil-1}|G|^{\gamma n}.

If
C
is not special for
F
, we have

|{\mathbf{E}}(\xi^{C(FA_{n}-A)})|=|{\mathbf{E}}(\xi^{C(-A)})\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}{\mathbf{E}}(\xi^{E(C,F,i,j)x_{ij}})|\leq\exp(-\alpha\delta n/(2a^{2})).

This follows because
[
Woo17
, Lemma 3.7]
tells us there are at least
\delta n/2
of the
E(C,F,i,j)
are non-zero, and then
[
Woo17
, Lemma 4.1]

bounds those factors by
\exp(-\alpha/a^{2})
. (The proof of
[
Woo17
, Lemma 3.7]
goes through for our definition of
m_{F}
using Lemma
3.6
in place of
[
Woo17
, Lemma 3.6]
, and
with the roles of
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}
and
\wedge^{2}
being reversed through the proof.)

Now, given a robust
C
for
F
,
[
Woo17
, Lemma 3.5]
gives a lower bound on the number of non-zero
E(C,F,i,j)
.
The proof of
[
Woo17
, Lemma 3.5]
goes through in this setting as long as we modify the definition of the map
t
in

[
Woo17
]
so that now
t((a_{1},b_{1}),(b_{2},a_{2})=e(b_{2},a_{1})-e(b_{1},a_{2})
. With this definition, the proofs of
[
Woo17
, Lemma 3.2, Corollary 3.3, Lemma 3.5]
still hold.
We then have that at least
\gamma\delta n^{2}/(2|G|^{2}|P|)
of the
E(C,F,i,j)
are non-zero (where
P
is the set of primes dividing
a
).
So if
C
is robust for
F
, we conclude that

|{\mathbf{E}}(\xi^{C(FA_{n}-A)})|\leq\exp(-\alpha\gamma\delta n^{2}/(2|G|^{2}|P|a^{2})).

Putting these bounds together, for any
c>0
such that
c<\alpha\delta/(2a^{2})
, given
given
\delta,\alpha,G,c
, we can choose
\gamma
sufficiently small so that we have

\displaystyle\left|{\mathbf{P}}(FX=A)-\frac{1}{|G|^{n}}\sum_{C\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G^{*}),\textrm{ special}}{\mathbf{E}}(\xi^{C(FX-A)})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|G|^{n}}\left(C_{G}\exp(-cn)+\exp(\log(|G|)n-\alpha\gamma\delta n^{2}/(2|G|^{2}|P|a^{2}))\right),

and the lemma follows.
∎

Definition 3.8
.

For an integer
D
with prime factorization
\prod_{i}p_{i}^{e_{i}}
, let
\ell(D)=\sum_{i}e_{i}
.
The
depth
of an
F\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G)
is the maximal positive
D
such that
there is a
\sigma\subset[n]
with
|\sigma|<\ell(D)\delta n
such that
D=[G:FV_{\sigma}]
, or is
1
if there is no such
D
.

Now we will complete the proof of Theorem
3.1
, using the sum in (
3.2
).
We let
K
change in each line, as long as it is a constant depending only on
\alpha,G,\delta,a
.
We then apply
[
Woo17
, Lemmas 5.2, 5.4]
(whose proofs go through in the current case) to bound the number of
F
of each depth and their corresponding probabilities of
FA_{n}=0
, and we
obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in\operatorname{Sur}(V,G)\\
F\textrm{ not code of distance $\delta n$}\end{subarray}}{\mathbf{P}}(FA_{n}=0)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D>1\\
D\mid\#G\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in\operatorname{Sur}(V,G)\\
F\textrm{ depth $D$}\end{subarray}}{\mathbf{P}}(FA_{n}=0)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D>1\\
D\mid\#G\end{subarray}}K\binom{n}{\lceil\ell(D)\delta n\rceil-1}|G|^{n}D^{-n+\ell(D)\delta n}e^{-\alpha(1-\ell(D)\delta)n}(a|G|/D)^{-(1-\ell(D)\delta)n}

\displaystyle\leq Ke^{-cn},

for any
0<c<\alpha
, and
\delta
chosen small enough in terms of
c
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in\operatorname{Hom}(V,G)\\
F\textrm{ not code of distance $\delta n$}\end{subarray}}|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G||G|^{-n}

\displaystyle\leq Ke^{-cn},

for any
0<c<\log(2)
, and
\delta
small enough in terms of
c
.
Using Lemma
3.7
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in\operatorname{Sur}(V,G)\\
F\textrm{ code of distance $\delta n$}\end{subarray}}\left|{\mathbf{P}}(FA_{n}=0)-|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G||G|^{-n}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ke^{-cn}.

Combining these estimates, we conclude the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

4.
Treatment for small primes: Moments determining the distribution

Now we prove Theorem
1.13
by showing that the moments in Theorem
3.1
determine unique distributions over certain restricted families of groups.
When the moments are bounded by quantities only slightly smaller than those in Theorem
3.1
, the result

[
Woo17
, Theorem 8.3]
shows that moments determine a unique distribution on finite abelian
P
-groups (see
[
Woo17
]
for some of the history of this moment problem). However, the moments in Theorem
3.1
,
famously, do not determine unique distribution. These moments arise as the distribution of moments of the predicted distribution of Selmer groups of random elliptic curves in the heuristics of Poonen and Rains
[
PR12
]
, further developed by Bhargava, Kane, Lenstra, Poonen and Rains
[
BKL+15
]
.
There are two different distributions predicted depending on whether the parity of the elliptic curve is even or odd, and those are the distributions we see as
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{n})
in the even and odd dimensional cases, respectively. With a bit more information on the groups, we show that we can recover the distribution as follows.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
P
be a finite set of primes, and
X_{n}
and
Y_{n}
random abelian
P
-groups for each integer
n

either (1) all supported on groups in
\mathcal{S}_{P}
, or (2) all
supported on groups of the form
{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}}\times G\times G
, for some integer
a
and all abelian
P
-groups
G
with
aG=0
.
If there is a constant
C
such that for every finite abelian
P
-group
A
we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(X_{n},A))=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(Y_{n},A))\leq C|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}A|,

then for every finite abelian
P
-group
A
we have
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(X_{n}\simeq A)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(Y_{n}\simeq A).

This theorem can be shown in few different ways, and the following convenient argument is based on work of the second author with W. Sawin.

Proof.

A finite abelian
P
-group
G
is in
\mathcal{S}_{P}
if and only if for each prime
p\in P
, if the Sylow
p
-subgroup
G_{p}
is of type
\lambda
, then all the
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}
are even. Similarly, a finite abelian
P
-group is of the form in the second condition if and only if for each
p\in P
, if the Sylow
p
-subgroup
G_{p}
is of type
\lambda
, we have that
\lambda_{1}=e
where
p^{e}
is the maximal power of
p
dividing
a
, and all the non-zero
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}
are odd. Let
\mathcal{T}
be the set of groups under consideration (either
\mathcal{S}_{P}
or the groups described in the second case).

Given a finite abelian group
G\in\mathcal{T}
, we can determine the isomorphism type of
G
from any quotient of
G
by one element.
To see this, we note that the Hall polynomial
g^{\lambda}_{\mu,\nu}(p)
counts the number of subgroups of type
\mu
of the finite abelian
p
-group of type
\lambda
where the quotient has type
\nu
. A subgroup generated by one element will correspond to a
\mu
with
\mu_{2}=0
. The Hall polynomial is zero, i.e. there are no such subgroups, when the Littlewood-Richardson coefficient
c^{\lambda}_{\mu,\nu}=0

[
Mac15
, II:(4.3)]
.
By the Pieri rule, we have that if
\mu_{2}=0
, then
c^{\lambda}_{\mu,\nu}=0
whenever
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}-\nu^{\prime}_{i}\geq 2
for some
i
.
Thus given any partition
\nu
, there is at most one partition
\lambda\in\mathcal{T}
such that a group of type
\nu
is a quotient of q group of type
\lambda
by one element.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{G}
be the (finite) set of all finite abelian groups that are a quotient of
G
by one element.

Let
Z
be a random group and
z
a uniform random element of
Z
. Note that for every finite abelian
P
-group
A
, we have

{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(Z/\langle z\rangle,A))={\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(Z,A))/|A|,

since or each surjection
Z\rightarrow A
, there is a
|A|^{-1}
probability that the image of
z
is
0
. Let
x_{n},y_{n}
be uniform random elements of
X_{n},Y_{n}
respectively.
Then for every finite abelian
P
-group
A
, we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(X_{n}/\langle x_{n}\rangle,A))=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(Y_{n}/\langle y_{n}\rangle,A))\leq C|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}A|/|A|=C|\wedge^{2}A|.

These moments do grow slowly enough to determine a unique distribution.
By the proof of
[
Woo17
, Theorem 8.3]
, we have that for all
A

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(X_{n}/\langle x_{n}\rangle\simeq A)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(Y_{n}/\langle y_{n}\rangle\simeq A),

and thus since

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(X_{n}\simeq A)=\sum_{B\in\mathcal{A}_{G}}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(X_{n}/\langle x_{n}\rangle\simeq A),

and similarly for
Y_{n}
, we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(X_{n}\simeq A)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(Y_{n}\simeq A).

∎

4.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.13

Now we show that these results can be applied to
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{n})
to determine their distribution.
We will need a distribution that we know about to compare moments with.
For a prime
p
, let
Y_{n}(p)
be the cokernel of a skew-symmetric matrix with entries in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
(the
p
-adic integers) drawn from the additive Haar measure on such skew-symmetric matrices.
Then for any finite abelian
p
-group
G
, we have
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(Y_{n}(p),G))=|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G|
.
This follows, for example, by letting
a
be the exponent of
G
, and noting than if we choose our entries
\zeta
uniform from
0
to
a-1
, then
Y_{n}
and
A_{n}
have the same distribution mod
a
, and thus the same
G
-moment (though also a much simpler argument can be given in the uniform case, as in
[
CKL+15
, Theorem 11]
).

It has been shown
[
BKL+15
, Theorem 3.9]
that as
n\rightarrow\infty
the distributions of
Y_{2n}(p)
approach the distribution of a random finite abelian
p
-group
Y(p)
with

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(Y_{2n}(p)\simeq G)={\mathbf{P}}(Y(p)\simeq G)=\frac{|G|}{|\operatorname{Sp}(G)|}\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1}),

for
G\in\mathcal{S}_{p}
(and
{\mathbf{P}}(Y(p)\simeq G)=0
for all other
G
). In the proof of
[
Woo17
, Theorem 8.3]
, it is shown that if we have sequence of random finite abelian
P
-groups
Z_{n}
such that for every finite abelian
P
-group
A
, the limits
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(Z_{n}\simeq A)
and

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(Z_{n},A))
exist and are finite, then

\sum_{B\textrm{ $P$-group}}(\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(Z_{n}\simeq B))\#\operatorname{Sur}(B,A)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(Z_{n},A)).

Thus,
{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(Y(p),G))=|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G|
.

Further, we have, with probability
1
, that
Y_{2n+1}(p)\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\times G
for a finite group
G
. This is because the upper
n-1\times n-1
submatrix has determinant
0
with probability
0
(the Haar measure of a hypersurface in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{k}
is 0, see e.g.
[
BKL+15
, Proposition 2.1 (b)]
in the easy case when
X
is affine space and the measure is Haar measure). By
[
BKL+15
, Theorem 3.11]
, we have a random finite abelian
p
group
Z(p)
such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(Y_{2n+1}(p)\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\times G)={\mathbf{P}}(Z(p)\simeq G)=\frac{1}{|\operatorname{Sp}(G)|}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1}).

for
G\in\mathcal{S}_{p}
(and
{\mathbf{P}}(Y_{2n+1}(p)\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\times G)=0
for all other
G
).
We can consider the random finite abelian groups
Y_{2n+1}(p)\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/p^{e}{\mathbb{Z}}
.
So as above, taking
e
so that
p^{e}G=0
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\times Z(p),G))=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(({\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\times Z(p))\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/p^{e}{\mathbb{Z}},G))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(Y_{2n+1}(p)\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/p^{e}{\mathbb{Z}},G))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G|.

Now we return to
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{n})
. In the even dimensional case, as mentioned in the proof of Theorem
1.4
, the group
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})
is of the form
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2m}\times G
, for some
G\in\mathcal{S}
.
Since
m
could be positive with positive probability, we need to reduce mod
a
for some
a
so we are in the setting of finite abelian groups.
For a positive integer
a
, we have that
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}}
is a random finite abelian group satisfying the first condition of Theorem
4.1
.
Let
P
be the set of the primes dividing
a
, and for every finite abelian
P
-group
G
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}},G)|)={\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(\prod_{p\in P}Y(p)\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}},G))=\begin{cases}|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G|&\textrm{ if $aG=0$}\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Thus, by Theorem
4.1
, we have that, for all
G
,

(4.2)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq G)={\mathbf{P}}(\prod_{p\in P}Y(p)\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq G).

In particular, given a finite set of primes
P
, and an abelian
P
-group
G
, we can take
a=\prod_{p\in P}p^{e+1}
, where

(\prod_{p\in P}p^{e})G=0
. Let
{\mathbb{Z}}_{P}:=(\prod_{p\in P}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
.
Then we have
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{P}\simeq G
if and only if
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq G
, and similarly for
\prod_{p\in P}Y(p)
.
So, we conclude for every finite abelian
P
-group
G
that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{P}\simeq G)={\mathbf{P}}(\prod_{p\in P}Y(p)\simeq G)=\begin{cases}\frac{|G|}{|\operatorname{Sp}(G)|}\prod_{p\in P}\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1})&\textrm{ if $G\in\mathcal{S}_{P}$}\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

In particular, since
Y(p)
is supported on finite groups, we have
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(|\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})|<\infty)=1
by Fatou’s lemma.
Thus, it follows that with asymptotic probability
1
, we have
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{P}\simeq(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n}))_{P}
.

In the odd dimensional case, the group
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})
is of the form
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2m+1}\times G
, for some
G\in\mathcal{S}
.
For a positive integer
a
, we have that
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}}
is a random finite abelian group satisfying the second condition of Theorem
4.1
.
Let
P
be the set of the primes dividing
a
, and for every finite abelian
P
-group
G
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}},G))={\mathbf{E}}(\#\operatorname{Sur}(\prod_{p\in P}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\otimes Z(p))\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}},G))=\begin{cases}|\operatorname{Sym}^{2}G|&\textrm{ if $aG=0$}\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Thus, by Theorem
4.1
, we have that, for all
G
,

(4.3)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq G)={\mathbf{P}}(\prod_{p\in P}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\otimes Z(p))\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq G).

In particular, given a finite set of primes
P
, and an abelian
P
-group
G
, we can take
a=\prod_{p\in P}p^{e+1}
, where

(\prod_{p\in P}p^{e})G=0
.
Then we have
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{P}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{P}\times G
if and only if
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}/a{\mathbb{Z}}\times G
, and similarly for
\prod_{p\in P}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\times Z(p)
.
So, we conclude for every finite abelian
P
-group
G
that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{P}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{P}\times G)={\mathbf{P}}(\prod_{p\in P}Z(p)\simeq G)=\begin{cases}\frac{|G|}{|\operatorname{Sp}(G)|}\prod_{p\in P}\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1})&\textrm{ if $G\in\mathcal{S}_{P}$}\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

In particular, since
Z(p)
is supported on finite groups, we have
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1}))=2n)=1
by Fatou’s lemma. Thus, it follows that with asymptotic probability
1
, we have
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}\times G
, and

\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{P}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{P}\times G
if and only if
(\mathbf{Cok}_{\operatorname{tors}}A_{2n+1})_{P}\simeq G
.

It also follows from (
4.2
) and (
4.3
), using
a=p
, that the ranks of
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n})
and
\mathbf{Cok}(A_{2n+1})
tensored with
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, in the limit, match those of
Y(p)
and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\times Z(p)
tensored with
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, respectively. These ranks are given in
[
Del01
, Example F]
(see also
[
BKL+15
, Theorem 1.10]
). This gives the matrix rank statement of Theorem
1.13
.

5.
Treatment for moderate primes: structures of the generalized normal vectors

Throughout Sections
5
,
6
and
7
of our treatment of the moderate primes
N
is a running parameter where, if not specified otherwise, we will assume

cn\leq N\leq n,

for some constant
c>0
.
In this first section of the treatment we will gather various useful ingredients, most of which are of different natures. Two key results are Proposition
5.18
and Proposition
5.19
.

5.1.
Odlyzko’s type results

As a warm-up let us introduce some elementary tools. The first ingredient is a variant of Odlyzko’s lemma (see
[
KKS95
,
Map13a
,
NW22
,
TV07
]
).

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
0<\alpha<1
be given. Let
{\mathbb{F}}
be a field.
For a deterministic subspace
V
of
{\mathbb{F}}^{N}
of dimension
d
and a random vector
X\in{\mathbb{F}}^{N}
with entries being i.i.d. copies of
\xi
satisfying (
1.2
), we have

{\mathbf{P}}(X\in V)\leq(1-\alpha)^{N-d}.

We also have a slight variation of Lemma
5.1
that has the same proof.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
V
be a deterministic subspace of
{\mathbb{F}}^{N}
of dimension
d
and
I\subset[N]
, for which
V_{I}
has full dimension
d
.
Let
J
be a subset of
I^{c}
such that
|J|=k
, and let
X\in{\mathbb{F}}^{N}
be a random vector where the entries over
J
and
J^{c}
are independent, and the entries over
J
are i.i.d. copies of
\xi
satisfying (
1.2
). Then we have

{\mathbf{P}}(X\in V)\leq(1-\alpha)^{k}.

Definition of
L_{N}
:

In what follows, we abuse notation by writing
L_{N}
for the matrix obtained from
L_{n}
(defined in the paragraph after Theorem
1.20
)
by restricting to its first
N
rows and columns.
In particular, whenever we write
L_{N}
, there is also always an implicit
n
and random integers
x_{ij}
for
1\leq i<j\leq n
.
In particular,
L_{N}
is a function of
all
the random variables
x_{kl},1\leq k<l\leq n+1
, including those with
k,\ell>N
.
This notation only applies when the subscript of
L
is the capital letter
N
.

Remark 5.3
.

When our random matrix is symmetric or skew-symmetric, the sparsity (number of
0
coefficients) of vectors plays a large role in the arguments. When the matrix is the graph Laplacian, the analog of sparseness is having a large number of coefficients of the same value, even if that value is not
0
. We will, when referring non-technically to our arguments, call this analog
sparsity
as well.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta>0
be sufficiently small constants (given
\alpha
from (
1.2
)), and let
N

be a sufficiently large positive integer (given
\varepsilon,\delta
and
\alpha
) and
n\geq N
.
Let
p
be a given prime.

(1)

(full rank of thin matrices) Let
1\leq k_{0}\leq(1-\varepsilon)n/2
. Then with probability at least
1-n(1-1/2)^{n-2k_{0}-1}
the matrix generated by
k_{0}
columns
X_{1},\dots,X_{k_{0}}
of
L_{n}
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
has full rank. In particular we have that
{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{n}/p)\geq(1-\varepsilon)n/2

with probability at least
1-n(1-1/2)^{\varepsilon n-1}
.

(2)

(non-sparsity of generalized normal vectors) Let
Y_{0}
be a fixed vector in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
. The following holds with probability at least
1-(1-1/2)^{N/2}
: any non-zero vector
{\mathbf{v}}
for which
L_{N}{\mathbf{v}}
agrees with
Y_{0}
in at least
(1-\varepsilon)N
coordinates must have the property that
{\mathbf{v}}-a\mathbf{1}
has at least
\delta N
non-zero components for any
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
.

The same conclusions holds for the symmetric and skew-symmetric models
M_{n}
and
A_{n}
, where
(1-1/2)
is replaced by
(1-\alpha)
, and in (2) the vector
{\mathbf{v}}-a\mathbf{1}
is replaced by
{\mathbf{v}}
.

In the special case that
Y_{0}
is the zero vector, this shows that with high probability the (actual)
normal vectors are non-sparse.

Note that in the proof it suffices to choose
\varepsilon,\delta
to be slightly smaller than
\alpha
. When
\varepsilon,\delta
get smaller the probability bounds get better in (2), but we obtain a worse bound on the number of non-zero entries. In applications, we usually assume
N\geq\delta_{0}n
for a given positive constant
\delta_{0}
.

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

In what follows, for the Laplacian case we understand that
\alpha=1/2
. For (1), for each
k\leq k_{0}
let
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
be the event that
X_{k+1}\in span(X_{1},\dots,X_{k})
. This belongs to the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{\prime}
that
X_{k+1}^{\prime}\in span(X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{k}^{\prime})
, where
X_{i}^{\prime}
is obtained from
X_{i}
by eliminating the first
(k+1)
coordinates. Now for this event, conditioning on any realization of
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{k}^{\prime}
, the probability
X_{k+1}^{\prime}
belongs to
span(X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{k}^{\prime})
, as
X_{k+1}^{\prime}
is now independent from
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{k}^{\prime}
, is at most
(1-\alpha)^{(n-1-k)-k}
by Lemma
5.1
. Thus

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{k})\leq{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{\prime})\leq(1-\alpha)^{n-1-2k}.

Summing over
k
we obtain (1).

We now prove (2).
Let
L^{\prime}
be the
(N+1)\times(N+1)
symmetric matrix with with upper-left
N\times N
submatrix
L_{N}
and rows summing to
0
.
We let
M
be the matrix of interest, either
L^{\prime}
or
M_{N}
or
A_{N}
, and let
X_{i}
be the columns of
M
.

We will show that the complement of the event under consideration in (2) implies that there exist
I,J\subset[N+1]
and
K\subset J\setminus I

with
|I|=|K|=\lfloor\delta N\rfloor+1
and
|J|=\lceil(1-\epsilon)N\rceil

such that either

(a)

M_{(J\setminus I)\times I}
has linearly dependent columns, or

(b)

M_{K\times I}
is full rank and
Y_{J\setminus I}
is in the span of
(X_{i})_{J\setminus I}
for
i\in I
.

If the event of (2) fails, in the Laplacian case we let
{\mathbf{w}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N+1}
be the vector whose first
N
coordinates agree with
{\mathbf{v}}-a\mathbf{1}
and whose last coordinate is
-a
. We have
(L^{\prime}{\mathbf{w}})_{[N]}=L_{N}{\mathbf{v}}
. We let
{\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{v}}
in the
M_{N}
or
A_{N}
cases.
Then we have
{\mathbf{w}}
such that
(M{\mathbf{w}})_{[N]}
agrees with
Y_{0}
in at least
(1-\epsilon)N
coordinates, and let
J
be the set of such coordinates. Further
{\mathbf{w}}
has at most
\lfloor\delta N\rfloor+1
non-zero entries, and we let
I
index a set containing those entries.
If (a) fails, then we let
K
be a subset of rows of
M_{(J\setminus I)\times I}
such that
M_{K\times I}
is full rank.
Then
(M{\mathbf{w}})_{J}=(Y_{0})_{J}
and
\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{w}})\subset I
implies that
Y_{J\setminus I}
is in the span of
(X_{i})_{J\setminus I}
for
i\in I
.

Now we bound the probability of (a) and (b) for fixed
I,J,K
.
Note that
M_{J\setminus I\times I}
has independent,
\alpha
-balanced entries, so the chance that it has linearly dependent columns is at most

|I|(1-\alpha)^{|J\setminus I|-|I|+1}
by applying Lemma
5.1
as we expose one column at a time.
In case
(b)
, we condition on the values of
M_{J\setminus I\times I}
in rows
K
.
We have
Y_{K}=\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}(X_{i})_{K}
for unique (non-random, after our conditioning) values
c_{i}
not all
0
(since
M_{K\times I}
is full rank). Since
Y_{J\setminus I}
is in the span of
(X_{i})_{J\setminus I}
for
i\in I
, we must
have
Y_{J\setminus I}=\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}(X_{i})_{J\setminus I}
. Suppose
c_{j}\neq 0
for some index
j\in I
. Then we further condition on all values of
M_{J\setminus I\times I}
except those in column
j
, and we see that
Y_{J\setminus I}=\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}(X_{i})_{J\setminus I}
implies each coordinate of
(X_{j})_{(J\setminus I)\setminus K}
must be some fixed value, which happens with probability at most
(1-\alpha)^{|(J\setminus I)\setminus K|}
.

Taking union bounds over the choices of
I,J,K
, we have that the complement of the event in (2) happens with probability at most

\binom{N+1}{\lfloor\delta N\rfloor+1}^{2}\binom{N+1}{\lfloor\varepsilon N\rfloor}(\delta N+2)(1-\alpha)^{(1-\varepsilon-2\delta)N-2}.

As long as
\varepsilon,\delta
are sufficiently small and
N
is sufficiently large, the above is bounded by
(1-\alpha)^{N/2}
.
∎

5.2.
Quadratic repulsion

In our next lemma we provide another useful estimate, which in practice will be more powerful than (1) of Lemma
5.4
.

Lemma 5.5
.

There exist positive constants
c,c^{\prime}
(which might depend on
\alpha
in the symmetric and skew-symmetric cases) such that the following holds. We let
k\leq N\leq m\leq n
be positive integers. Let
G_{n}
be either the model
M_{n},A_{n}
or
L_{n}
from Theorem
1.3
, Theorem
1.4
or Theorem
1.22

respectively. Then for each prime
p
,
with probability at least
1-\exp(-ck^{2}+c^{\prime}m)
, for any subset
I,J
of size
N
of
[m]
the matrix
G_{I\times J}/p
has rank at least
N-k
.

Note that this result (as well as Lemma
5.4
above) holds for any
p
. In can be seen as a finite field analog of the quadratic repulsion phenomenon of eigenvalues of Wigner matrices (see for instance
[
ESY10
,
NTV17
,
Ngu18
]
). Also, in application we will assume
k\geq n^{1/2+\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon>0
, in which case, even after taking a union bound over all choices of
I
and
J
,
