we still obtain a bound of type
1-\exp(-\Theta(k^{2}))
when
n
is sufficiently large.

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

The complement of the event in the lemma implies that there exist
I,J\subset[m]
with
|I|=|J|=N
and
I_{0}\subset I
and
J_{0}\subset J
with
|I_{0}|=|J_{0}|<N-k
such that

G_{I_{0}\times J_{0}}
is non-singular and
{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{I\times J})={\operatorname{rank}}(G_{I_{0}\times J_{0}}).
We now bound the probability of this latter event for a fixed
I,J,I_{0},J_{0}
.

Since
|I\setminus I_{0}|,|J\setminus J_{0}|>k
, we can choose
A\subset I\setminus I_{0}
and
B\subset J\setminus J_{0}
such that
A
and
B
are disjoint,
|B|
is even, and
|A|\geq k/2
and
k/2\geq|B|\geq k/2-2
.
(Here

A
takes
k/2
or
(k+1)/2
, and then
B
takes
k/2
minus 0 or
1
or
(k-1)/2
minus 0 or 1, to be even.)
We pick an arbitrary fixed-point-free involution on
B
.
In the Laplacian case, we will start with
G_{n}
, and then do an
AB
-shuffle on
G_{n}
resulting in
G^{\prime}_{n}
(with entries
x^{\prime}_{ij}
), and we will bound the probability that
G^{\prime}_{I_{0}\times J_{0}}
is non-singular and

{\operatorname{rank}}(G^{\prime}_{I\times J})={\operatorname{rank}}(G^{\prime}_{I_{0}\times J_{0}}).
In the non-Laplacian case, we let
G^{\prime}_{n}=G_{n}
.

In the Laplacian case, let
B_{0}
contain one element of each involution orbit of
B
.
For
j\in B_{0}
, let
A_{j}
be the subset of
i\in A
for which
x_{ij}\neq x_{i\bar{j}}
.
We call
j\in B_{0}
bad if
|A_{j}|<|A|/4
.
By the Chernoff bound,
we have that
j
is bad with probability at most
\exp(-|A|/8)
.
Let
\mathcal{E}
be the event that there are at least
|B_{0}|/2
bad
j
.
There are at most
2^{|B_{0}|}
subsets of
B_{0}
of size at least
|B_{0}|/2
, and so

{\mathbf{P}}(\mathcal{E})\leq 2^{|B_{0}|}\exp(-|A|/8)^{|B_{0}|/2}
.

We now condition on
G_{n}
in the Laplacian case and
G^{\prime}_{I_{0}\times J}
and
G^{\prime}_{I\times J_{0}}
in the other cases.
Let
X_{i}
be the
i
th column of
G^{\prime}_{n}
. If
G_{I_{0}\times J_{0}}=G^{\prime}_{I_{0}\times J_{0}}
is non-singular and
{\operatorname{rank}}(G^{\prime}_{I\times J})={\operatorname{rank}}(G^{\prime}_{I_{0}\times J_{0}}),

then for each
j\in J\setminus J_{0}
, we have that there are unique constants
c_{j\ell}
(determines by our conditioned values) such that

(X_{j})_{[I_{0}]}=\sum_{\ell\in J_{0}}c_{j\ell}(X_{\ell})_{[I_{0}]}
.
In order for
{\operatorname{rank}}(G^{\prime}_{I\times J})={\operatorname{rank}}(G^{\prime}_{I_{0}\times J_{0}}),
we must also have, for each
j\in B
, that

(X_{j})_{A}=\sum_{\ell\in J_{0}}c_{j\ell}(X_{\ell})_{A}
.

In the Laplacian case, after our conditioning,
the matrix entries of
G^{\prime}_{A\times B_{0}}
are independent. Further, an entry
x^{\prime}_{i,j}
for
i\in A
and
j\in B_{0}
is
1/2
-balanced if
x_{ij}\neq x_{i\bar{j}}
.
Unless
\mathcal{E}
occurs, we have at least
|B_{0}|/2
values
j\in B_{0}
such that for at least
|A|/4
values of
i\in A
we have that

x^{\prime}_{i,j}
is
1/2
-balanced.
The probability that

(X_{j})_{A_{j}}=\sum_{\ell\in J_{0}}c_{j\ell}(X_{\ell})_{A_{j}}
for such a
j\in B_{0}
is at most
(1/2)^{|A|/4}
, and thus
the probability this holds for all such
j\in B_{0}

is at most
(1/2)^{|A||B_{0}|/8}+{\mathbf{P}}(\mathcal{E})
.

In the non-Laplacian case, the entries of
G^{\prime}_{A\times B}
are independent and
\alpha
-balanced.
Thus the probability that

(X_{j})_{A}=\sum_{\ell\in J_{0}}c_{j\ell}(X_{\ell})_{A}
for all
j\in B
is at most
(1-\alpha)^{|A||B|}.

Thus, in any case, the probability that, for a fixed
I,J,I_{0},J_{0}
, we have
G^{\prime}_{I_{0}\times J_{0}}
is non-singular and

{\operatorname{rank}}(G^{\prime}_{I\times J})={\operatorname{rank}}(G^{\prime}_{I_{0}\times J_{0}})
is at most

2^{|B_{0}|}e^{-|A||B_{0}|/16}+\max(1/2,1-\alpha)^{|A||B_{0}|/8}\leq e^{k/4-k(k/4-1)/32}+e^{-c_{0}k(k/4-1)/16},

for some
c_{0}
dependong on at most
\alpha
.
Since, there are at most
2^{4m}
choices of
I,J,I_{0},J_{0}
, and
G^{\prime}_{n}
has the same distribution as
G_{n}
, the lemma follows.
∎

5.3.
Concentration discrepancy for general
\alpha
-balanced random variables

We next introduce some modifications to simplify the general models of symmetric, skew-symmetric and Laplacian matrices
M_{N},A_{N},L_{N}
considered in Theorem
1.3
, Theorem
1.4
, and Theorem
1.22
.

Throughout Section
5.3

we assume that
p\geq 3
is a prime number. Let
{\mathbf{w}}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})
be a non-zero deterministic vector in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
. We use a strategy
from
[
NW22
]
to bound the probability
{\mathbf{P}}(x_{1}w_{1}+\dots+x_{N}w_{N}=a)
, where
x_{i}
are i.i.d. copies of a random
\alpha
-balanced
\xi\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
.

Let
\psi:=\xi-\xi^{\prime}
be the symmetrization of
\xi
and let
\psi^{\prime}
be a lazy version of
\psi
so that

{\mathbf{P}}(\psi^{\prime}=x)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{P}}(\psi=x)\mbox{ if }x\neq 0\\
\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{P}}(\psi=x)+\frac{1}{2},\mbox{ if }x=0.\end{cases}

Notice that
\psi^{\prime}
is symmetric as
\psi
is symmetric (i.e.
{\mathbf{P}}(\psi^{\prime}=a)={\mathbf{P}}(\psi^{\prime}=-a)
for all
a
), and we can check that
\max_{x}{\mathbf{P}}(\psi=x)\leq 1-\alpha
, and so

\max_{x\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}{\mathbf{P}}(\psi^{\prime}=x)\leq 1-\alpha/2.

Let
\pm t_{1},\pm t_{2},\dots
be the non-zero values taken by
\psi^{\prime}
with positive probability (with
\pm t_{i}\neq t_{j}
for
i\neq j
), and let

\beta_{j}:=2{\mathbf{P}}(\psi^{\prime}=t_{j})=2{\mathbf{P}}(\psi^{\prime}=-t_{j})
and
\beta_{0}:={\mathbf{P}}(\psi^{\prime}=0)
.
Let

(5.6)

S:=x_{1}w_{1}+\dots+x_{n}w_{N}=X\cdot{\mathbf{w}},

where
X=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
.

Consider
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
where
{\mathbf{P}}(S=a)
is maximum (or minimum). Using the standard notation
e_{p}(x)
for
\exp(2\pi\sqrt{-1}x/p)
, we have

{\mathbf{P}}(S=a)={\mathbf{E}}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}e_{p}(t(S-a))={\mathbf{E}}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}e_{p}(tS)e_{p}(-ta)=\frac{1}{p}+{\mathbf{E}}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}e_{p}(tS)e_{p}(-ta).

So by independence,

\displaystyle|{\mathbf{P}}(S=a)-\frac{1}{p}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}|{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(tS)|\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\prod_{i=1}^{N}|{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(tx_{i}w_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(\frac{1}{2}(|{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(tx_{i}w_{i})|^{2}+1))\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\prod_{i=1}^{N}|{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(t\psi^{\prime}w_{i})|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(\beta_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j}\cos\frac{2\pi tt_{j}w_{i}}{p})\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(\beta_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j}|\cos\frac{\pi tt_{j}w_{i}}{p}|)

where we made the change of variable
t\rightarrow t/2
(in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
for
p\geq 3
) and used the triangle inequality.

By convexity, we have that
|\sin\pi z|\geq 2\|z\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}
for any
z\in{\mathbb{R}}
, where
\|z\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}
is the distance of
z
to the nearest integer. Thus,
|\cos\frac{\pi x}{p}|\leq 1-\frac{1}{2}\sin^{2}\frac{\pi x}{p}\leq 1-2\|\frac{x}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}
. Hence for each
w_{i}

\beta_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j}|\cos\frac{\pi tt_{j}w_{i}}{p}|\leq 1-2\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j}\|\frac{tt_{j}w_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}\leq\exp(-2\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j}\|\frac{tt_{j}w_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}).

Consequently, by Jensen’s inequality and the fact that
\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j}=1-\beta_{0}\geq\alpha/2
we obtain a key inequality

\displaystyle|{\mathbf{P}}(S=a)-\frac{1}{p}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(\beta_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j}|\cos\frac{\pi tt_{j}w_{i}}{p}|)\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\exp(-2\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j}\|\frac{tt_{j}w_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\exp(-2\sum_{j=1}^{l}\frac{\beta_{j}}{\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j}}(\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j})\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\frac{tt_{j}w_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\sum_{j=1}^{l}\frac{\beta_{j}}{\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j}}\exp(-2(\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j})\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\frac{tt_{j}w_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{l}\frac{\beta_{j}}{\sum_{j=1}^{l}\beta_{j}}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\exp(-2(\alpha/2)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\frac{tt_{j}w_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2})

(5.7)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\exp(-\alpha\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\frac{tw_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}).

Motivated by this, we define
\rho({\mathbf{w}})=\rho_{\alpha}({\mathbf{w}})
, the
concentration discrepancy
of
{\mathbf{w}}
, to be

(5.8)

\rho({\mathbf{w}}):=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\exp(-\alpha\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\frac{tw_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}).

Similarly, when we analyze a Laplacian matrix
we will define

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{w}})=\rho_{L,\alpha}({\mathbf{w}})
as

(5.9)

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{w}}):=\max_{w}\rho({\mathbf{w}}-w\mathbf{1})=\max_{w}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}\exp(-\alpha\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\frac{t(w_{i}-w)}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}).

For later use we also remark the following homogenized inequality

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\frac{t(w_{i}-w)}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor(N-1)/2\rfloor}(\|\frac{t(w_{2k}-w)}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}+\frac{t(w_{2k+1}-w)}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor(N-1)/2\rfloor}(\|\frac{t(w_{2k}-w_{2k+1})}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}).

Hence with
{\mathbf{w}}^{\prime}=(w_{1}-w_{2},\dots,w_{2\lfloor(N-1)/2\rfloor}-w_{2\lfloor(N-1)/2\rfloor+1})
we have

(5.10)

\rho_{L,\alpha}({\mathbf{w}})\leq\rho_{\alpha/2}({\mathbf{w}}^{\prime}).

An Erdős-Littlewood-Offord type result for finite fields
is as follows.

Theorem 5.11

(
[
NP20
, Theorem A.15]
)
.

Let
p
be a prime and
N
a positive integer. Let
{\mathbf{w}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
. Let
0<c_{nsp}<1
be a number that might depend on
N

and assume that

(5.12)

|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{w}})|\geq c_{nsp}N.

Then for
X=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
with
x_{i}
being i.i.d. copies of an
\alpha
-balanced random integer
\xi
,

\sup_{r\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}|{\mathbf{P}}(X\cdot{\mathbf{w}}=r)-\frac{1}{p}|\leq\rho({\mathbf{w}})=O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{c_{nsp}N}}).

Furthermore, if we assume that for all
w\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}

(5.13)

|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{w}}-w\mathbf{1})|\geq c_{nsp}N.

Then for
X=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
with
x_{i},
for
1\leq i\leq N-1
, being i.i.d. copies of
\xi
and with
x_{N}=-\sum_{1\leq i\leq N-1}x_{i}
we have the following affine analog

\sup_{r\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}|{\mathbf{P}}(X\cdot{\mathbf{w}}=r)-\frac{1}{p}|\leq\rho_{L}({\mathbf{w}})=O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{c_{nsp}N}}).

Here the implied constants depend on
\alpha
, but not on
p,N,c_{nsp}
.

Hence, for instance in the case that
X=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
with
x_{i}
being i.i.d. copies of
\xi
, as long as the
w_{i}
are non-zero, the random sum
X\cdot{\mathbf{w}}
spreads out quickly in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
in such a way that the discrepancy with respect to the uniform distribution is
O(1/\sqrt{N})
.
This rate of decay is best possible (in terms of
N
) if one does not have extra information on
{\mathbf{w}}
.

We next give simple but useful bounds on
\rho({\mathbf{w}}),\rho_{L}({\mathbf{w}})
of the sort used in the proof of Lemma
3.7
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
N
be a positive integer, and let
p
a prime. Let
{\mathbf{w}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
.

•

If
|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{w}})|\geq c_{nsp}N

for some
c_{nsp}>0
then

\rho({\mathbf{w}})\leq\exp\big(-\alpha\frac{c_{nsp}N}{p^{2}}\big).

•

If
|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{w}}-w\mathbf{1})|\geq c_{nsp}N

for some
c_{nsp}>0
and for all
w\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
then

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{w}})\leq\exp\big(-\alpha\frac{c_{nsp}N}{p^{2}}\big).

Proof.

For any
t,w_{i}\neq 0
in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
we have

\|tw_{i}/p\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}\geq(\frac{1}{p})^{2}.

Hence if
{\mathbf{w}}
has at least
c_{nsp}N
non-zero coordinates and
t\neq 0
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\frac{tw_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}\geq\frac{c_{nsp}N}{p^{2}}.

The lemma then follows from the definitions of
\rho({\mathbf{w}}),\rho_{L}({\mathbf{w}})
.
∎

5.4.
A simple structure result

Another elementary observation is that one can obtain some useful structure on the
w_{i}
when
\rho({\mathbf{w}})
(or
\rho_{L}({\mathbf{w}})
) are sub-exponentially small.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\delta<1
be a positive constant. Let
p
be a prime number and
N
a positive integer. Let
{\mathbf{w}}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{N})\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
such that

\rho({\mathbf{w}})\geq\exp(-N^{\delta}).

Then for any
1\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
, there is a set
W^{\prime}
of
N-N^{\prime}
components
w_{i}
and an arithmetic progression
Q
(i.e. a GAP of rank one, cf. Definition
8.1
) in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
that contains
W^{\prime}
, where

(5.16)

|Q|\leq 2\alpha^{-1}p\sqrt{\lceil N^{\delta}+1\rceil/N^{\prime}}.

Similarly, assume that

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{w}})\geq\exp(-N^{\delta}).

Then there is an arithmetic progression
Q
satisfying (
5.16
) that contains
N-N^{\prime}
components
w_{i}
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the first case because translation of an arithmetic progression is also an arithmetic progression.
Consider the level sets
S_{m}:=\{x\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\,|\,x\neq 0\mbox{ and }\alpha\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\frac{xw_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}\leq m\}
. We have

\exp(-N^{\delta})\leq\rho({\mathbf{w}})=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},x\neq 0}\exp(-\alpha\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\frac{xw_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2})\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{m\geq 1}\exp(-(m-1))|S_{m}|.

Since
\rho({\mathbf{w}})\geq\exp(-N^{\delta})
and
\sum_{m\geq N^{\delta}+2}\exp(-(m-1))<\exp(-N^{\delta})
, there must be a level set
S_{m}
in the range
1\leq m\leq\lceil N^{\delta}+1\rceil
such that
S_{m}
is non-empty, and so there exists
x_{0}\neq 0
so that

\alpha\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\frac{x_{0}w_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}\leq m.

So, with
W^{\prime}
being the set of
w_{i}
such that
\alpha\|\frac{x_{0}w_{i}}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2}\leq\frac{m}{N^{\prime}}
, we have that
W^{\prime}
has at least
N-N^{\prime}
elements.
By definition, for
w_{i}\in W^{\prime}
we have
\alpha\|\frac{x_{0}w_{i}}{p}\|^{2}\leq\frac{m}{N^{\prime}},
and this implies that after a dilation by
x_{0}
the set
W^{\prime}
belongs to the arithmetic progression
P
where

P:=\Big\{x\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\,|\,\|\frac{x}{p}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}\leq\alpha^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{m}{N^{\prime}}}\Big\}.

Notice that the size of
P
is bounded by
2\alpha^{-1}p\sqrt{m/N^{\prime}}\leq 2\alpha^{-1}p\sqrt{\lceil N^{\delta}+1\rceil/N^{\prime}}
as desired.
∎

5.5.
Generalized normal vectors

Here and later we will need the concept of generalized normal vectors (that was mentioned in Lemma
5.4
).

Definition 5.17
.

Given a vector
Y_{0}
and an index set
I\subset[N]
(usually of size
(1-o(1))N
), we say that a vector
{\mathbf{v}}
is a
generalized normal vector
of
G_{N}
(with respect to
Y_{0}
and
I
) if

(G_{N}{\mathbf{v}})_{I}=(Y_{0})_{I}.

Now we mention a key result of the section, which says that as long as
p
is not too small and not too large (
p\leq\exp(n^{c})
for some sufficiently small constant
c
), with very high probability the random walks formed by generalized normal vectors of
M_{N},A_{N}
and
L_{N\times N}
spread out in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
in such a way that the discrepancy from the uniform distribution is sub-exponentially small.

Proposition 5.18

(Non-local structure of the normal vectors: symmetric and skew-symmetric cases)
.

Let
N
be a positive integer and
G_{N}
be either
M_{N}
or
A_{N}
from Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.4
. Let
0<\lambda\leq 1
be a given constant. Then there exists a positive constants
c
(given
\lambda
and
\alpha
from (
1.2
))
such that the following holds for
p
sufficiently large (depending on
\lambda,\alpha
)
but
p\leq\exp(N^{c})
. Let
I_{0}\subset[N]
be an index set of size
\lfloor\lambda N\rfloor
. For any non-zero fixed vector
Y_{0}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-n^{c})
(with respect to
G_{N}
), for any vector
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
such that
G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
in at least
N-N^{c}
coordinates, we have (with
\rho(-)
from (
5.8
))

\rho({\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}})\leq\exp(-N^{c}).

We also have similar result for Laplacian matrices.

Proposition 5.19

(Non-local structure of the normal vectors: Laplacian case)
.

Let
0<\lambda\leq 1
be a given constant. Then there exists a positive constant
c
(given
\lambda
) such that the following holds for
p
prime and sufficiently large (depending on
\lambda
).
Let
n,N
be positive integers and let
L_{n}
be as in Theorem
1.22
, and let
L_{N}
be the
[N]\times[N]
submatrix of
L_{n}
.
We assume that
p\leq\exp(N^{c})
. Let
I_{0}\subset[N]
be an index set of size
\lfloor\lambda N\rfloor
that might depend on the randomness of
x_{kl},
for
N+1\leq k\text{ or }N+1\leq l
, but is independent of the randomness of
x_{kl},
for
1\leq k<l\leq N
.
For any non-zero fixed vector
Y_{0}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-N^{c})
,
for any vector
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
such that
L_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
in at least
N-N^{c}
coordinates we have (with
\rho_{L}(-)
from (
5.9
))

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}})\leq\exp(-N^{c}).

We will present the proofs of these results in the next section and deduce Proposition
2.1
and Proposition
2.3
in Section
7
. To complete this section, we remark that in fact for the symmetric and skew-symmetric case, to prove Proposition
2.1
(or Propositions
7.12
and
7.13
)
one only needs Proposition
5.18
for
\lambda
close to 1. On the other hand, for the Laplacian case, the extra randomness created by neighbor reshuffling will be limited, and hence we will need the full strength of Proposition
5.19
for any given
\lambda
.

6.
Treatment for moderate primes: proof of Propositions
5.18
and
5.19

To prepare for the proofs we first introduce a decomposition trick (originating from
[
Ver14
]
) which will be useful. Let
{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{Y}}_{0}
be fixed vectors in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
, and let
G_{N}
be either
M_{N},A_{N}
or
L_{N}
. Assume that we would like to bound the probability of the event
G_{N}{\mathbf{a}}=Y_{0}
. For this, for any
I\subset[N]
we can write

G_{N}=\Big(\begin{array}[]{cc}G_{([N]\backslash I)\times([N]\backslash I)}&G_{([N]\backslash I)\times I}\\
G_{I\times([N]\backslash I)}&G_{I\times I}\end{array}\Big).

The equation
(G_{N}{\mathbf{a}})_{[N]\backslash I}=(Y_{0})_{[N]\backslash I}

can be written as

(6.1)

G_{([N]\backslash I)\times([N]\backslash I)}{\mathbf{a}}_{[N]\backslash I}+G_{([N]\backslash I)\times I}{\mathbf{a}}_{I}=(Y_{0})_{[N]\backslash I}.

[N]\backslash I
I
G_{([N]\backslash I)\times I}

Figure 2
.
Decomposition into i.i.d. parts.

In the non-Laplacian case, we condition on the entries of
G_{([N]\backslash I)\times([N]\backslash I)}
(see Figure
2
). Then we can view (
6.1
) as
G_{([N]\backslash I)\times I}{\mathbf{a}}_{I}=D
, where
D
is deterministic and the entries of
G_{([N]\backslash I)\times I}
are independent.
In the Laplacian case, we condition on all
x_{kl},
for
1\leq k<l\leq n
where
(k,l),(l,k)\notin([N]\backslash I)\times I
,
and we let
X_{j}
be the
j
th row of

G_{([N]\backslash I)\times I}
.
After conditioning,
(
6.1
) is equivalent to

(6.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}{x_{ji}a_{i}}

\displaystyle=d_{j}\quad\textrm{ for all $j\in[N]\setminus I$ (non-Laplacian case), or}

\displaystyle-x_{j(n+1)}a_{j}+\sum_{i\in I}{x_{ji}(a_{i}-a_{j})}

\displaystyle=d_{j}\quad\textrm{ for all $j\in[N]\setminus I$ (Laplacian case)},

for some deterministic
d_{i}
. Also, the
x_{ji}
above
are all independent.
In the non-Laplacian case we have

(6.3)

{\mathbf{P}}(G_{N}{\mathbf{a}}=Y_{0})\leq\sup_{D}{\mathbf{P}}(G_{([N]\backslash I)\times I}{\mathbf{a}}_{I}=D|G_{([N]\backslash I)\times([N]\backslash I)})\leq(\frac{1}{p}+\rho({\mathbf{a}}_{I}))^{N-|I|},

and in the Laplacian case we have

(6.4)

{\mathbf{P}}(G_{N}{\mathbf{a}}=Y_{0})\leq\sup_{\{d_{j}\}_{j}}{\mathbf{P}}(X_{j}\cdot({\mathbf{a}}_{I}-a_{j}\mathbf{1})=d_{j}\textrm{ for all $j\in[N]\setminus I$})\leq(\frac{1}{p}+\rho_{L}({\mathbf{a}}_{I}))^{N-|I|}.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
G_{N}
be either the random matrix
M_{N},A_{N}
from Theorem
1.3
or Theorem
1.4
, or the random matrix
L_{N}

that is the
[N]\times[N]
submatrix of the
L_{n}
from Theorem
1.22
. For every sufficiently small
constant
\delta>0
(where how small might depend on
\alpha
in the symmetric and skew-symmetric case), for
N
sufficiently large depending on
\alpha
and
\delta
the following holds. Let
Y_{0}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
be a fixed vector. Let
N^{-\delta}\leq\lambda_{N}\leq 1
be a parameter that might depend on
N
, and let
p
be a prime where

\max\{\frac{1}{\delta^{3}},\frac{1}{\lambda_{N}^{3}}\}\leq p\leq\exp(N^{\delta}).

Then with probability at least
1-\exp(-N^{1-\delta})
, for any
non-zero
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
such that
G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
in at least
N-N^{\delta}
coordinates,

(1)

|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{v}})|\geq N-\lambda_{N}N
in the
M_{N},A_{N}
case;

(2)

|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{v}}-a\mathbf{1})|\geq N-\lambda_{N}N
for any
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
in the
L_{N}
case. In other words the highest multiplicity among the components of
{\mathbf{v}}
is at most
\lambda_{N}N
.

Remark 6.6
.

Note that we can take
\lambda_{N}
to be a constant in the above result, which yields an analog of (2) of Lemma
5.4
in which
\delta
(of that result) can be close to 1 (unlike in Lemma
5.4
itself), assuming that
p
is sufficiently large but not too large.

Proof of Lemma
6.5
.

It suffices to assume
\lambda_{N}\leq\delta
because the statements are weaker for
\lambda_{N}>\delta
.
Let
\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}
be the event that there is a
non-zero
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
such that
G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
in at least
N-N^{\delta}
coordinates and

{\mathbf{v}}
has at least
\lambda_{N}N
coordinates of the same value.
We will show
{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}})\leq e^{-N^{1-\delta}}
, which implies the lemma since if the event of the lemma fails, then
\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}
occurs.
We take
\delta<1/4
, and will indeed take it smaller later, depending on at most
\alpha
. Throughout the proof, we always assume that
N
is sufficiently large given
\alpha
and our choice of
\delta
.

First, we will bound the probability of the following event
\mathcal{E}
:
there exists a
{\mathbf{v}}
whose first
\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil
coordinates are the same and
G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
.
Let
k:=\lfloor\lambda_{N}N/4\rfloor
.
We can partition
[\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil+1,N]
into subsequences
J_{1},\dots,J_{\ell}
of consecutive numbers so that
|J_{i}|=k
for
i<\ell
and
k\leq|J_{\ell}|<2k
.
We have

k\geq\lambda_{N}N/8\quad\textrm{and}\quad\ell<8N^{\delta}.

Given
{\mathbf{v}}
, we partition
[N]
into two subsets,
I_{m}
(mixed) and
I_{s}
(sparse).
We let
I_{s}
be the union of the
J_{i}
such that
{\mathbf{v}}_{J_{i}}
has highest entry multiplicity larger than
|J_{i}|-N^{1-5\delta}
, along with
[\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil]
.
We let
I_{m}=[N]\setminus I_{s}
.
We write
{\mathbf{v}}_{m}:={\mathbf{v}}_{I_{m}}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}:={\mathbf{v}}_{I_{s}}
.

Claim 6.7
.

Given
I_{s}
, there are at most
e^{N^{1-2\delta}}

possibilities for
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
for
{\mathbf{v}}
giving that
I_{s}
and whose first
[\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil]
coefficients are the same.

Proof.

The number of
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
is at most

\binom{2k}{\lfloor N^{1-5\delta}\rfloor}^{\ell}p^{\ell+1}(p^{N^{1-5\delta}})^{\ell}\leq N^{8N^{1-4\delta}}e^{16N^{2\delta}+8N^{1-3\delta}}.

On the left above, the first factor is for the index choices of the coordinates that are not contributing to the highest entry multiplicity in
|J_{i}|
, the second
factor is for the choices of the entry value with highest entry multiplicity in each
J_{i}
and in
[\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil]
, the third factor is for the choices of the other entry values in the
J_{i}
.
The claim follows.
∎

Case 1.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be the event that there exists a non-zero
{\mathbf{v}}
whose first
\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil
coordinates are the same,
G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
, and the resulting
I_{m}
(from
{\mathbf{v}}
) is empty.
By Claim
6.7
, there are at most
e^{N^{1-2\delta}}
choices of
{\mathbf{v}}
that can arise with empty
I_{m}
.
For each of these choices of
{\mathbf{v}}
, we apply (
6.2
) to bound
{\mathbf{P}}(G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0})
, letting
I
be the coordinate of a single non-zero coefficient in
{\mathbf{v}}
.
In the Laplacian case, note that in (
6.2
) since
a_{i}\neq 0
, if
a_{i}-a_{j}=0
, then
a_{j}\neq 0
.
Then we have

{\mathbf{P}}(G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0})\leq(1-\alpha)^{N-1}.

Summing over the possible
{\mathbf{v}}
, we conclude

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{1})\leq e^{N^{1-2\delta}}(1-\alpha)^{N-1}\leq e^{-c_{1}N}

for some
c_{1}>0
depending on
\alpha
.

Case 2.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be the event that there exists a
{\mathbf{v}}
whose first
\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil
coordinates are the same,
G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
, and the resulting
I_{m}
is not empty.
We have
|I_{m}|\geq\lambda_{N}N/8\geq N^{1-\delta}/8

and
|I_{s}|\geq\lambda_{N}N
.

We now describe a function
f
from subsets of
[N]
(that can occur as
I_{s}
) to subsets of
[N]
. We describe
f(I_{s})
,
and write
I_{m}
for
[N]\setminus I_{s}
, but note that
f
does not depend
on
{\mathbf{v}}
. If
|I_{s}|>|I_{m}|
, we let
f(I_{s})
be the first
|I_{m}|
elements of
I_{s}
.
Otherwise, we let
J_{*}
be the first
J_{i}
in
I_{m}
and we let
f(I_{s})
be the union of
I_{s}
and the first
|I_{m}|-|I_{s}|
elements of
I_{m}
that are not in
J_{*}
.
This is possible because
|J_{*}|\leq\lambda_{N}N/2\leq|I_{s}|.
In either case, we have

|f(I_{s})|=|I_{m}|
and if
I_{m}
is non-empty, it contains some
J_{i}
that does not intersect
f(I_{s}).

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{drop}
be the event that there is a square submatrix
G_{A\times B}
of
G_{N}

of dimension
\geq\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor

with rank less than
|A|-\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor
.
Lemma
5.5
tells us that

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{drop})\leq Ne^{-c^{\prime\prime}(\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor)^{2}+c^{\prime}N}
(for some
c^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime}>0
depending on
\alpha
),
and thus

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{drop})\leq e^{-c_{drop}N^{1+2\delta}},

for some
c_{drop}>0
depending on
\alpha
.

We wish to bound the probability of
\mathcal{E}_{2}\setminus{\mathcal{E}}_{drop}
.
Since for
{\mathbf{v}}
causing
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we have
|I_{m}|>N^{1-\delta}/8
, we have
|I_{m}|\geq\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor
.
So outside of
{\mathcal{E}}_{drop}
, the matrix
G_{f(I_{s})\times I_{m}}
has rank
\geq|I_{m}|-\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor
,
and hence it has a square submatrix
G_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\times{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}
of dimension
|I_{m}|-\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor
which has full rank.

Given subsets
M
,
{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}
, and
{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}
of
[N]

such that
|{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}|=|{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}|
and
{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}\subset M
and
{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\subset f([N]\setminus M)
, and such that

M
is non-empty and can occur as
I_{m}
, let
\mathcal{E}_{2.1}(M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}})
be the event that there is a
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
such that
the first
\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil
entries of
{\mathbf{v}}
are the same,
G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
, and
I_{m}=M
, and
G_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\times{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}
is full rank.
In particular, above we just saw that
\mathcal{E}_{2}\setminus{\mathcal{E}}_{drop}
implies

\mathcal{E}_{2.1}(M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}})
for some
M
,
{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}
, and
{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}
with
|M|-|{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}|=\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor

and
N-|M|\geq\lambda_{N}N
.

Claim 6.8
.

For all
M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{2.1}(M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}})
is defined, we have

{\mathbf{P}}(\mathcal{E}_{2.1}(M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}))\leq e^{N^{1-2\delta}}p^{|M|-|{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}|}\left(O(1)/\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}+1/p\right)^{N-|M|-\lambda_{N}N/2}

Proof.

For
{\mathbf{v}}
with the first
\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil
coordinates the same and
I_{m}=M
, there are at most
e^{N^{1-2\delta}}

possible values of
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
by Claim
6.7

and there are at most
p^{|M|-|{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}|}
possible values for
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{m}\setminus{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}.

We fix a choice of
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{m}\setminus{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}},
and we will bound the desired probability just for
{\mathbf{v}}
with these values.
We let
F=f([N]\setminus M)
.
We condition on
G_{F\times[N]}
and compute the conditional probability.
If
G_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\times{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}
is not full rank, then the desired (conditional) probability is
0
.
Otherwise, the equation
(G_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\times{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}},G_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\times([N]\backslash{{I^{\prime}_{m}}})}){\mathbf{v}}=(Y_{0})_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}}
implies

(6.9)

{\mathbf{v}}_{{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}=(G_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\times{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}^{-1})\left((Y_{0})_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}}-G_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\times([N]\backslash{{I^{\prime}_{m}}})}{\mathbf{v}}_{[N]\setminus{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}\right).

Everything on the right above is determined, and thus so is
{\mathbf{v}}_{{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}
, and we are considering a single fixed value of
{\mathbf{v}}
.
We let
J_{*}
be one of the
J_{i}
that is in
M
but has no intersection with
F
.

For each
i\in([N]\setminus F)\setminus J_{*}
, we let
X_{i}
be the
i
th row of
G_{N}
. The equation
X_{i}{\mathbf{v}}=(Y_{0})_{i}
implies

(6.10)

\sum_{j\in J_{*}}x_{ij}(v_{j}-v_{i})=(Y_{0})_{i}-\sum_{j\in[N]\setminus(J_{*}\cup\{i\})}x_{ij}(v_{j}-v_{i})+\sum_{j\in[n+1]\setminus[N]}x_{ij}v_{i},

where
v_{i}
are the entries of
{\mathbf{v}}
.

The
x_{ij}
for
i\in([N]\setminus F)\setminus J_{*}
and
j\in J_{*}
are all independent.
We further condition on
x_{ij}
for
i\in([N]\setminus F)\setminus J_{*}
and
j\not\in J_{*}
.
Since
J_{*}\cap F=\emptyset
, none of the
x_{ij}
for
i\in([N]\setminus F)\setminus J_{*}
and
j\in J_{*}
have been conditioned on.
Thus after our conditioning, the probability of (
6.10
) holding is at most
\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{J_{*}}).

We have
N-|F|-|J_{*}|
independent such equations that are implied by
G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
.
Thus the probability that, for a given
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{m}\setminus{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}},
we have the event in the claim, is at most

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{J_{*}})^{N-|F|-|J_{*}|}.

Since
J_{*}
is one of the
J_{i}
that is a subset of
I_{m}
, by definition of
I_{m}
and
the highest multiplicity of coordinates of
{\mathbf{v}}_{J_{*}}
is
<|J_{*}|-N^{1-5\delta}
, by Theorem
5.11
we have that

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{J_{*}})\leq O(1)/\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}+1/p.

We have
|J_{*}|\leq\lambda_{N}N/2
and
|F|=|M|
. Thus the probability that, for a given
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{m}\setminus{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}},
we have the event in the claim, is at most

\left(O(1)/\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}+1/p\right)^{N-|M|-\lambda_{N}N/2}.

Summing over the possible values for
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{m}\setminus{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}
(which are bounded in number above), we obtain the claim.
∎

The event
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
is the union of
{\mathcal{E}}_{drop}
with
\mathcal{E}_{2.1}(M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}})
over
M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}
with
|M|-|{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}|=\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor

and
|N|-|M|\geq\lambda_{N}N
and
M
a possible value of
I_{m}
. There are at most
2^{\ell}\leq 2^{8N^{\delta/2}}
possible values of
I_{m}
(and hence
M
).
Given
M
, there are at most
\binom{|M|}{\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor}
choices of
{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}
, and
\binom{|f([N]\setminus M)|}{\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor}=\binom{|M|}{\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor}
choices of
{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}
. Hence,
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c_{drop}N^{1+2\delta}}+2^{8N^{\delta/2}}N^{2N^{1/2+\delta}}e^{N^{1-2\delta}}e^{N^{1/2+2\delta}}\left(O(1)/\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}+1/p\right)^{\lambda_{N}N/2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c_{drop}N^{1+2\delta}}+e^{N^{1-\delta}}\left(O(1)/\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}+1/p\right)^{\lambda_{N}N/2},

for
\delta
sufficiently small. Thus, there is some
c_{0}>0
(depending only on
\alpha
) such that for
\delta
sufficiently small and
N
sufficiently large given
\delta
,

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}})\leq{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{1})+{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{2})\leq e^{-c_{0}N}+e^{N^{1-\delta}}\left(O(1)/\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}+1/p\right)^{\lambda_{N}N/2}.

To bound the probability of
\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}
and prove the lemma, we need to account also for the analog of
{\mathcal{E}}
where the first
\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil
entries are replaced with any
\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil
entries, and
Y_{0}
is replaced by any vector that shares at least
N-N^{\delta}
coordinates with it.
This gives

\binom{N}{\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil}\binom{N}{\lfloor N^{\delta}\rfloor}p^{\lfloor N^{\delta}\rfloor}

total events with the same probability bound as we showed above for
{\mathcal{E}}
, whose union is
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
.
We then have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}})\leq

\displaystyle\left(\binom{N}{\lceil\lambda_{N}N\rceil}\binom{N}{\lfloor N^{\delta}\rfloor}p^{\lfloor N^{\delta}\rfloor}\right)\left(e^{-c_{0}N}+e^{N^{1-\delta}}\left(O(1)/\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}+1/p\right)^{\lambda_{N}N/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{Ne}{\lambda_{N}N}\right)^{\lambda_{N}N+1}N^{N^{\delta}}e^{N^{2\delta}}\left(e^{-c_{0}N}+e^{N^{1-\delta}}\left(O(1)/\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}+1/p\right)^{\lambda_{N}N/2}\right)

(6.11)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{N}N+1+\lambda_{N}N\log\lambda_{N}^{-1}+\log\lambda_{N}^{-1}+N^{\delta}\log N+N^{2\delta}}\left(e^{-c_{0}N}+e^{N^{1-\delta}}\left(O(1)/\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}+1/p\right)^{\lambda_{N}N/2}\right).

The
e^{-c_{0}N}
factor (and, e.g., its 20th root) is much smaller than all the terms it is multiplied by (in (
6.11
)) except perhaps

e^{\lambda_{N}N+\lambda_{N}N\log\lambda_{N}^{-1}}
, but if we take
\delta
small enough we can guarantee
\lambda_{N}N+\lambda_{N}N\log\lambda_{N}^{-1}\leq c_{0}N/20
. We have that

(O(1)/\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}+1/p)
is bounded by either
O(1)/\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}
or
2/p
,
and so we consider these cases separately.
We have

\left(\frac{O(1)}{\sqrt{N^{1-5\delta}}}\right)^{\lambda_{N}N/2}\leq e^{O(\lambda_{N}N)-\lambda_{N}N(1-5\delta)\log N/4}.

The
e^{-\lambda_{N}N(1-5\delta)\log N/4}
factor
(and its 20th root)
is much smaller than all the terms it is multiplied by (from (
6.11
)) except perhaps
e^{\lambda_{N}N\log\lambda_{N}^{-1}}
. Since
\lambda_{N}^{-1}\leq N^{\delta}
,
for all
\delta>0
small enough we have
\lambda_{N}N\log\lambda_{N}^{-1}\leq\lambda_{N}N(1-5\delta)\log N/80
.

We have

\left(\frac{2}{p}\right)^{\lambda_{N}N/2}\leq(2\lambda_{N}^{-3})^{\lambda_{N}N/2}=e^{(\log 2)\lambda_{N}N/2-1.5\lambda_{N}N\log\lambda_{N}^{-1}}.

The
e^{-1.5\lambda_{N}N\log\lambda_{N}^{-1}}
factor cancels partially with

e^{\lambda_{N}N\log\lambda_{N}^{-1}}
(from (
6.11
)), giving a remaining term of

e^{-.5\lambda_{N}N\log\lambda_{N}^{-1}}
, whose 20th root
is smaller than all the terms it is multiplied by (above and in (
6.11
)).
Thus we conclude

{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}})\leq e^{-c_{0}N/20}+e^{-\lambda_{N}N\log\lambda_{N}^{-1}/100}.

Since
x\log x^{-1}
is decreasing for
x\geq e
and
\lambda_{N}\geq N^{-\delta}
, the lemma follows.
∎

We remark that in the proof of Lemma
6.5
above the upper bound assumption on
p
is important. One cannot expect a similar conclusion for extremely large
p
.

6.1.
Proof of Proposition
5.18
and Proposition
5.19

Generally speaking, some part of our treatment here is motivated by
[
FJ19
]
where the authors used the machinery of
[
FJLS21
]
to give an explicit singularity bound for random symmetric matrices by passing to matrices modulo a very large prime (of order
e^{N^{c}}
). However, there are significant differences in our results: (1) We study the discrepancy
\rho
, which controls
\sup_{a}|{\mathbf{P}}(X=a)-1/p|
instead of the concentration probability
\sup_{a}{\mathbf{P}}(X=a)
. This is the correct concept to measure how a probability distribution in a finite group deviates from the uniform distribution. (2) Our result works as long as
p
is sufficiently large (instead of focusing only on
p
of order
e^{N^{c}}
; in fact if we only focus on the skew-symmetric or symmetric case, one may be able to take
p\geq 3
, but we will not elaborate on this). As we have seen in Section
2
, this wide range of
p
is in fact important to our main theorems. (3) Above all, perhaps the most innovative part of this section is to carry out the study for the Laplacian model. Here we have to study the
segmentwise
structures of the generalized normal vectors, that is
\rho(Z_{I_{0}})
, over any fixed set
I_{0}
of size
\lambda n
. With these goals in mind, we will need to develop additional tools and borrow some more ideas from
[
LMN20
]
. The most innovative part of this subsection is the proof of Proposition
6.29
where we exploit structures using the “propagation method” of Lemma
6.5
.

Let
{\mathbf{z}}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})
be any vector in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
. We first record an elementary relation (recalling
\rho(.)
from (
5.8
)).

Fact 6.12
.

For any
I\subset[n]
we have

\rho({\mathbf{z}}_{I})\geq\rho({\mathbf{z}})

and

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{z}}_{I})\geq\rho_{L}({\mathbf{z}})

We next need the following definitions and results from
[
FJLS21
]
.

Definition 6.13
.

For an
{\mathbf{w}}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})
be the number of solutions of the form
(s,(i_{1},\dots,i_{2k}))\in\{+,-\}^{2k}\times[N]^{2k}

to
\pm w_{i_{1}}\pm\dots\pm w_{i_{2k}}=0\pmod{p}
. More generally, for given
\delta\in[0,1]
we define
R_{k}^{\delta}({\mathbf{w}})
to be the number of solutions to

\pm w_{i_{1}}\pm w_{i_{2}}\cdots\pm w_{i_{2k}}=0\,\text{mod}\,p

that satisfy
|\{i_{1},\dots,i_{2k}\}|\geq(1+\delta)k
.

It is easy to show that
R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})
is never much larger than
R_{k}^{\delta}({\mathbf{w}})
.

Lemma 6.14
.

[
FJLS21
, Lemma 1.6]

For all integers
k,N
with
k\leq N/2
and any prime
p
,
{\mathbf{w}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
and
\delta\in[0,1]
,

R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})\leq R_{k}^{\delta}({\mathbf{w}})+(40k^{1-\delta}N^{1+\delta})^{k}.

The following Halász-type result connects the above combinatorial structure to
\rho(.)
(see
[
LMN20
, Theorem 5.1]

3
3

3

Although in
[
LMN20
, Theorem 5.1]
the statement is for
\rho=\sup_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}}|{\mathbf{P}}(\mu_{1}w_{1}+\cdots+\mu_{N}w_{N}=a)-\frac{1}{p}|
, the proof works identically for
\rho
as in (
5.8
). The constant
C
can be taken to be
\alpha^{-1}C_{0}
where
C_{0}
is absolute.
).

Theorem 6.15
.

Let
k\geq 1
be an integer. Let
f:{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}
be any function such that
f(x)\leq x/100
.
For any non-zero vector
{\mathbf{w}}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{N})\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
we have

\rho({\mathbf{w}})\leq C\frac{R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})}{2^{2k}N^{2k}\sqrt{f(|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{w}})|)}}+e^{-f(|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{w}})|)/2}

for
k\leq N/f(|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{w}})|)
, where
C
is a constant depending on
\alpha
.

As we will have to deal with subvectors many times in this section, for convenience by
{\mathbf{b}}\subset{\mathbf{a}}
we mean that
{\mathbf{b}}
is a truncation of
{\mathbf{a}}
. We use
|{\mathbf{a}}|
to mean the dimension of the vector
{\mathbf{a}}
, that is

|{\mathbf{a}}|:=|\operatorname{supp_{index}}({\mathbf{a}})|.

Definition 6.16

(Choices of parameters)
.

Let
k,s_{1},s_{2},d\in[n]
and such that
s_{1}\leq s_{2}
. The parameters can be chosen in a flexible manner but for instance our arguments will work when

•

\delta>0
is sufficiently small given
\alpha
;
N
is sufficiently large given
\alpha,\delta
;

•

and also

(6.17)

\displaystyle k=\Theta(N^{1/4}),s_{1}=\Theta(N^{1-4\delta}),s_{2}=\Theta(N^{1-2\delta}),d\geq N^{2/3}\text{ and }N^{12\delta}\leq p\leq\exp(N^{\delta/2}).

Note that we choose
p\geq N^{12\delta}
here to exploit Lemma
6.5
in the setting that
\lambda_{N}=N^{-4\delta}
.
In our proof of Propositions
5.18
and
5.19
later we will extend this range to cover all sufficiently large
p
.

In what follows
C
is a sufficiently large constant that is allowed to depend on
\alpha
and can be different in each statement. For each
t>0
,
given
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq d
we define

\displaystyle{\mathbf{G}}_{d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N):=

\displaystyle\Big\{{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N},|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})|=d:\forall\Lambda\subset\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})\textrm{ such that }s_{1}\leq|\Lambda|\leq s_{2},

\displaystyle\text{ with ${\mathbf{b}}={\mathbf{a}}|_{\Lambda}$, we have }\rho({\mathbf{b}})\geq 3C(t+1)\sqrt{k}/(p\sqrt{s_{1}})\Big\}.

Note that
t
is a varying integral parameter and we take
t\leq p
. In a way, the conditions say that there are lots of structures among the non-zero entries of
{\mathbf{a}}
.
The next result shows that this set has small size.

Proposition 6.18
.

For
C
sufficiently large given
\alpha
, the following holds for any sufficiently small
\delta
.
Let
p

be a prime and let
t\geq 1
and
1\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq d\leq N,
and
1\leq k\leq s_{1}/2

such that

(6.19)

\frac{p}{t}\sqrt{\frac{s_{1}}{k}}\leq\min\{e^{s_{1}/2k},(s_{1}/Ck)^{(1-\delta)k}\}.

Then we have that (when
d=s_{2}
)

(6.20)

|{\mathbf{G}}_{d,k,s_{1},d,\geq t}(N)|\leq\binom{N}{d}p^{d}(\delta t)^{-d+s_{1}}

and in general

(6.21)

|{\mathbf{G}}_{d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)|\leq\binom{N}{d}p^{d+s_{2}}(\delta t)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}.

We will apply this result for the choice of parameters from Eq. (
6.17
) and with
t
satisfying
1\leq t\leq p
.

Proof.

As suggested by Theorem
6.15
, it is natural to pass to the following analog of
{\mathbf{G}}_{d.k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)
,

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}}_{d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N):=

\displaystyle\Big\{{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N},|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})|=d,\forall\Lambda\subset\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})\textrm{ such that }s_{1}\leq|\Lambda|\leq s_{2},

\displaystyle\text{ with ${\mathbf{b}}={\mathbf{a}}|_{\Lambda}$, we have }R_{k}^{\delta}({\mathbf{b}})\geq t\frac{2^{2k}|{\mathbf{b}}|^{2k}}{p}\Big\}.

We observe that

{\mathbf{G}}_{d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)\subset{{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}}_{d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N).

Indeed, assume that
{\mathbf{a}}\notin{{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}}_{d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)
and
|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})|=d
. Then there exists
{\mathbf{b}}\subset{\mathbf{a}}_{\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})}\textrm{ such that }s_{1}\leq|{\mathbf{b}}|\leq s_{2}
and
R_{k}^{\delta}({\mathbf{b}})<t\frac{2^{2k}|{\mathbf{b}}|^{2k}}{p}
. Note
|{\mathbf{b}}|=|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{b}})|
.
By Lemma
6.14
and Theorem
6.15
(with
f(x)=x/k
) for
C
sufficiently large depending on
\alpha
, we have

\displaystyle\rho({\mathbf{b}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(R_{k}^{\delta}({\mathbf{b}})+(Ck^{1-\delta}|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{b}})|^{1+\delta})^{k}}{2^{2k}|{\mathbf{b}}|^{2k}\sqrt{|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{b}})|/k}}+e^{-|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{b}})|/2k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Ct\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{b}})|}}+\frac{(Ck^{1-\delta})^{k}}{|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{b}})|^{(1-\delta)k}}+e^{-|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{b}})|/2k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Ct\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}}+(\frac{Ck}{s_{1}})^{(1-\delta)k}+e^{-|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{b}})|/2k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3Ct\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}}.

Hence it suffices to bound the cardinality of
{{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}}_{d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)
. To this end we just need to apply
[
FJLS21
, Theorem 1.7]
to obtain Eq. (
6.20
) and
[
FJ19
, Corollary 3.11]
to obtain Eq. (
6.21
).
∎

Here we remark that the arguments of
[
FJLS21
, Theorem 1.7]
and
[
FJ19
, Corollary 3.11]
to bound
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
use a nice double counting trick to exploit the largeness of
R_{k}^{\delta}({\mathbf{b}})
. Very roughly speaking, because
R_{k}^{\delta}({\mathbf{b}})
is large for all
{\mathbf{b}}\subset{\mathbf{a}}_{\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})}
, there is a small index set
I
over which if we fix the values of
w_{i}
for
i\in I
, the values of other
w_{j}
will be determined.

While Theorem
6.18
above will suffice to study the symmetric and skew-symmetric case, for the Laplacian case we will need to modify a bit. In what follows
L
stands for Laplacian, we define

\displaystyle{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N):=

\displaystyle\Big\{{\mathbf{a}}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{N})\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N},|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})|=d,\forall{\mathbf{b}}\subset{\mathbf{a}}_{\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})}\textrm{ such that }s_{1}\leq|{\mathbf{b}}|\leq s_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ we have }\rho_{L}({\mathbf{b}})\geq 3C(t+1)\sqrt{k}/(p\sqrt{s_{1}})\Big\}.

and

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N):=

\displaystyle\Big\{{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N},|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})|=d,\forall\Lambda\subset\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})\textrm{ such that }s_{1}\leq|\Lambda|\leq s_{2},

\displaystyle\text{ with ${\mathbf{b}}={\mathbf{a}}|_{\Lambda}$, we have }R_{k}^{\delta}({\mathbf{b}}-a\mathbf{1})\geq t\frac{2^{2k}|{\mathbf{b}}|^{2k}}{p}\text{ for some $a$}\Big\}.

Arguing as in the non-Laplacian case, by Theorem
6.15
we have

(6.22)

{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)\subset{{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N).

We have the following

Proposition 6.23
.

For
C
sufficiently large given
\alpha
, the following holds for any sufficiently small
\delta
.
Let
p

be a prime and let
1\leq t\leq p
and
1\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq d\leq N,
and
1\leq k\leq s_{1}/2

such that

\frac{p}{t}\sqrt{\frac{s_{1}}{k}}\leq\min\{e^{s_{1}/2k},(s_{1}/Ck)^{(1-\delta)k/2}\}.

We have that

|{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)|\leq p\binom{N}{d}2^{d+4}p^{d+s_{2}}(\delta t)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}.

Proof.

Consider
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)
with
\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})=[d]
.
Without loss of generality we assume that
d
is even (as the reader will see, if this is not the case we just need to freeze another coordinate of
{\mathbf{a}}
). Define
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}=(a_{1}-a_{2},a_{3}-a_{4},\dots,a_{d-1}-a_{d})
and
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime}=(a_{2}-a_{3},a_{4}-a_{5},\dots,a_{d}-a_{1})
.

Let
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
be any subvector of
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}
with
s_{1}/2\leq|{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}|\leq s_{2}/2
, that is
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
has the form
(a_{i_{1}}-a_{i_{1}+1},\dots,a_{i_{|{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}|}}-a_{i_{|{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}|}+1})
. Then by (
5.10
)

(6.24)

\rho_{\alpha/2}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})\geq\rho_{L,\alpha}(a_{i_{1}},a_{i_{1}+1},\dots,a_{i_{|{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}|}},a_{i_{|{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}|}+1})\geq 3C(t+1)\sqrt{k}/(p\sqrt{s_{1}}),

where in the last estimate we used the fact that
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)
.

Given
d^{\prime}
, we will consider the
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}
with
|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}}^{\prime})|=d^{\prime}
. We always have
d^{\prime}\leq d/2
. First we consider the dominating case when
s_{2}/2\leq d^{\prime}
. We apply Theorem
6.18
with the
(s_{1},s_{2},d,N,k)
in that theorem replaced by
(s_{1}/2,s_{2}/2,d^{\prime},d/2,\lfloor k/2\rfloor)
.
By (
6.24
) and by Theorem
6.18
, the number of
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}
with
d^{\prime}=|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}}^{\prime})|
that can come from
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)

is bounded by

\binom{d/2}{d^{\prime}}p^{d^{\prime}+s_{2}/2}(\delta t)^{-d^{\prime}(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}\leq\binom{d/2}{d^{\prime}}p^{d/2+s_{2}/2}(\delta t)^{-(d/2)(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}=:{\mathcal{N}},

as long as
C
is large enough given
\alpha
.

Secondly, if
s_{1}/2\leq d^{\prime}\leq s_{2}/2
, by (
6.20
) the number of such
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}
is bounded by

\binom{d/2}{d^{\prime}}p^{d^{\prime}}(\delta t)^{-d^{\prime}+s_{1}/2}\leq\binom{d/2}{d^{\prime}}t^{s_{1}/2}(p/t)^{d^{\prime}}\delta^{-d^{\prime}+s_{1}/2}\leq\binom{d/2}{d^{\prime}}t^{(d/2)s_{1}/s_{2}}(p/t)^{d/2}\delta^{-(d/2)(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}\leq{\mathcal{N}}.

Thirdly, if
d^{\prime}\leq s_{1}/2
then the number of such vectors is simply bounded by
\binom{d/2}{d^{\prime}}p^{s_{1}/2}\leq{\mathcal{N}}
because as
t\leq p
,

p^{d/2+s_{2}/2}(\delta t)^{-(d/2)(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}\geq p^{d/2+s_{2}/2}p^{-d/2}\geq p^{s_{2}/2}.

Hence in total we see that the number of
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}
with
d^{\prime}=|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}}^{\prime})|
that can come from
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)
is at most
3{\mathcal{N}}
. Summing over
d^{\prime}
we see that the number of
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}
is at most

3\times 2^{d/2}p^{d/2+s_{2}/2}(\delta t)^{-(d/2)(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}.

We bound similarly for the number of
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime}
. Therefore the number of
({\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime})
is at most

2^{d+4}p^{d+s_{2}}(\delta t)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}.

Finally, we remark that when
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime}
are given, then any assignment of
a_{1}
would then determine the
{\mathbf{a}}
they came from.
Thus the number of
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)
with
\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})=[d]
can be bounded by
p2^{d+4}p^{d+s_{2}}(\delta t)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}
. Multiplying by a factor of
\binom{N}{d}
to include
{\mathbf{a}}
with any support of size
d
gives the result.
∎

In our application,
{\mathbf{a}}
will be a generalized normal vector (with respect to some given
Y_{0}
). In our next steps we will be working with overwhelming events defined below.

Remark 6.25
.

Let
N
be a positive integer,
\delta>0
,
p
be a prime such that
8N^{12\delta}\leq p\leq\exp(N^{\delta/2})
,
Y_{0}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
.

•

Let
G_{N}
be either
M_{N}
or
A_{N}
. Then let
{\mathcal{E}}_{non-sparse}
(implicitly depending on our choice of random matrix model and
N,p,Y_{0},\delta
) be the event that
for every nonzero
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
such that
L_{N}{\mathbf{a}}=Y_{0}
in at least
N-N^{\delta}
coordinates,
for any
\Lambda\subset\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})
with
s_{1}\leq|\Lambda|
(for
s_{1}
as in Definition
6.16
))
we have

N-s_{1}/2\leq|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})|.

•

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{L,non-sparse}
(implicitly depending on
N,p,Y_{0},\delta
) be the event that
for every nonzero
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
such that
L_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
in at least
N-N^{\delta}
coordinates,
for any
\Lambda\subset\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}})
with
s_{1}\leq|\Lambda|
(for
s_{1}
as in Definition
6.16
))
we have

|\Lambda|-s_{1}/2\leq|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}}-w\mathbf{1})_{\Lambda}|.

By Lemma
6.5
(applied to
\lambda_{N}=n^{-4\delta}/2
) we have

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{non-sparse}),{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{L,non-sparse})\geq 1-\exp(-(N^{1-\delta})).

Now for the set of non-sparse structured vectors, we decompose it into disjoint union of sets of type

{\mathbf{G}}_{d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t_{i}}(N)\backslash{\mathbf{G}}_{d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq 2t_{i}}(N)\mbox{ or }{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t_{i}}(N)\backslash{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq 2t_{i}}(N)

from which we will take advantage of the counting results from Theorems
6.18
and
6.23
to show that it is unlikely for
G_{N}
to have structured generalized normal vectors.

Lemma 6.26
.

Given
\alpha
, for any sufficiently small
\delta>0
and any sufficiently large
C
, there exists
C^{\prime}
depending on
\delta,\alpha
and
C
such that the following holds.
Let
N,s_{1},k,t,d
be positive integers and
p
a prime
such that
N^{12\delta}\leq p\leq\exp(N^{\delta/2})
,
and
\frac{p}{t}\sqrt{\frac{s_{1}}{k}}\leq\min\{e^{s_{1}/2k},(s_{1}/Ck)^{(1-\delta)k}\},
and
1\leq s_{1}\leq d\leq N,
and
C\leq k\leq s_{1}
.
We have the following

•

(symmetric and skew-symmetric) The event
{\mathcal{E}}_{non-sparse}
and the event that there exists
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbf{G}}_{d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}\backslash{\mathbf{G}}_{d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq 2t}
such that
G_{N}{\mathbf{a}}=Y_{0}
in at least
N-N^{\delta/2}
coordinates have probability at most

(C^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}}(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{3C(t+1)\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}\binom{N}{d}p^{d+s_{2}}(\delta t)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}.

•

(Laplacian) The event
{\mathcal{E}}_{L,non-sparse}
and the event that there exists
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}\backslash{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq 2t}
such that
G_{N}{\mathbf{a}}=Y_{0}
in at least
N-N^{\delta/2}
coordinates have probability at most

(C^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}}(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{3C(t+1)\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}\times p\times\binom{N}{d}2^{d+4}p^{d+s_{2}}(\delta t)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}.

Again, we will apply this result for the choice of parameters from Eq. (
6.17
) and with
t
satisfying
\frac{p}{t}\sqrt{\frac{s_{1}}{k}}\leq\min\{e^{s_{1}/2k},(s_{1}/Ck)^{(1-\delta)k}\}
.

Proof of Lemma
6.26
.

We will only explain the details in the Laplacian case, the cases of symmetric and skew-symmetric matrices are similar. For a fixed
{\mathbf{a}}
in
{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}(N)\backslash{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq 2t}(N)
we show that the event that
G_{N}{\mathbf{a}}=Y_{0}
in at least
N-N^{\delta/2}
coordinates has probability at most

(6.27)

2^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}}(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{3C(t+1)\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}.

Note that on
{\mathcal{E}}_{L,non-sparse}
of Lemma
6.5
,
|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{a}}-w\mathbf{1})|
has order
N
for all
w
. Let
{\mathbf{b}}
be the a subvector
{\mathbf{a}}_{\Lambda}
such that
\rho_{L}({\mathbf{b}})\leq\frac{3C(t+1)\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}}
. Recall our treatment from (
6.3
) and (
6.4
)
that we can write

G_{N}=\Big(\begin{array}[]{cc}G_{([N]\backslash\Lambda)\times([N]\backslash\Lambda)}&G_{([N]\backslash\Lambda)\times\Lambda}\\
G_{\Lambda\times([N]\backslash\Lambda)}&G_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}\end{array}\Big).

Assume that we have to work with an event of type
G_{N}{\mathbf{a}}=Y_{0}
in all coordinates (after adding
p^{N^{\delta/2}}
other possibilities for the missing equalities). We will decompose our matrix as above and then consider a system of
N-|\Lambda|
equations

G_{([N]\backslash\Lambda)\times([N]\backslash\Lambda)}{\mathbf{a}}_{[N]\backslash\Lambda}+G_{([N]\backslash\Lambda)\times\Lambda}{\mathbf{a}}_{\Lambda}=(Y_{0})_{[N]\backslash\Lambda}.

If we condition on the entries of
G_{([N]\backslash\Lambda)\times([N]\backslash\Lambda)}
, then we can view the above as
G_{([N]\backslash\Lambda)\times I}{\mathbf{b}}=D
, where
D
is deterministic (after expanding out the diagonals in
L_{N}
,
D
will depend on the other entries not belonging to the above block), and we notice that now the entries of
G_{([N]\backslash\Lambda)\times\Lambda}
are i.i.d.. So we can estimate the above probability by

(6.28)

{\mathbf{P}}(G_{([N]\backslash\Lambda)\times\Lambda}{\mathbf{b}}=D)\leq\prod_{i\in[N]\backslash\Lambda}(\frac{1}{p}+\rho({\mathbf{b}}-a_{i}\mathbf{1}))\leq(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{3C(t+1)\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}},

Summing over the support choice of
\binom{N}{N^{\delta/2}}
coordinates, and over other
p^{N^{\delta/2}}
assignments of the unknown entries (toward
G_{N}{\mathbf{a}}=Y_{0}
) we complete the proof of (
6.27
).

To complete the proof of Lemma
6.26
, we use Theorem
6.23
and (
6.27
) to estimate the probability under consideration by

(C^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}}(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{3C(t+1)\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{n-s_{2}}\times p\times\binom{N}{d}2^{d+4}p^{d+s_{2}}(\delta t)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}.

∎

Now we prove Proposition
5.19
(Proposition
5.18
can be shown similarly). We will restate in the following form for the reader’s convenience.

Proposition 6.29
.

Let
0<\lambda\leq 1
be a given constant. There exist
positive constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}
such that the following holds.
Let
n,N
be sufficiently large positive integers and let
L_{n}
be as in Theorem
1.22
, and let
L_{N}
be the
[N]\times[N]
submatrix of
L_{n}
.
Let
I_{0}\subset[N]
be a (random) index set of size
\lfloor\lambda N\rfloor
that might be depend on the randomness of
x_{kl},
for
N+1\leq k\text{ or }N+1\leq l
, but is independent of the randomness of
x_{kl},
for
1\leq k<l\leq N
.
Let
p
be a sufficiently large prime given
\lambda
but
p\leq\exp(N^{c_{1}})
.
Then for any non-zero fixed vector
Y_{0}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-N^{c_{2}})

for any vector
{\mathbf{v}}
such that
L_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
in at least
N-N^{c_{3}}
coordinates we have

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}})\leq\exp(-N^{c_{4}}).

It is clear that Proposition
5.19
would follow by taking
c=\min\{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}\}
.

Proof of Proposition
6.29
.

For the proof, we choose some small
\delta>0
. In several steps, we will require
\delta
sufficiently small (in an absolute sense) and we can work with any fixed
\delta
that satisfies all the requirements. Throughout the proof, all statements we make are assuming that
N
is sufficiently large with respect to
\lambda
and
\delta
(and since we take
\delta
an absolute constant in the end, this really only means
N
is sufficiently large with respect to
\lambda
). It suffices to prove the result assuming

(6.30)

\lambda\leq\delta

for some
0<\delta<1
because the statement for
\delta<\lambda\leq 1
will follow from the monotonicity of Fact
6.12
.

The choices for the parameters
c_{i}
is somewhat flexible, but for instance we can take

c_{1}=c_{3}=c_{4}=\delta/2,c_{2}=1-2\delta.

We let
{\mathcal{V}}
denote the vectors
{\mathbf{v}}
in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
for which
{\mathbf{v}}-a\mathbf{1}
has at least
N-\lambda N/4
non-zero coordinates for every
a
, and
{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}}
the set of non-zero vectors
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
that such that we have

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}})>e^{-N^{c_{4}}}.

Observe that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(\exists{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}},\mbox{$(L_{N}{\mathbf{v}})_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i}$ in at least $N-N^{c_{3}}$ coordinates })

\displaystyle={\mathbf{P}}(\exists{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}}\cap{\mathcal{V}},\mbox{ $(L_{N}{\mathbf{v}})_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i}$ in at least $N-N^{c_{3}}$ coordinates })

\displaystyle+{\mathbf{P}}(\exists{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}}\cap{\mathcal{V}}^{c},\mbox{ $(L_{N}{\mathbf{v}})_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i}$ in at least $N-N^{c_{3}}$ coordinates }).

By Lemma
6.5
(with
\lambda_{N}=\lambda/4
),

(6.31)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\exists{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{V}}^{c},\mbox{ $(G_{N}{\mathbf{v}})_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i}$ in at least $N-N^{c_{3}}$ coordinates}\Big)\leq\exp(-N^{1-\delta}).

Therefore, it suffices to focus on the event
{\mathcal{F}}
that there exists
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}},
such that
G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
in at least
N-N^{c_{3}}
coordinates. We first bound the event
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
that there exists
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}},
such that
(G_{N}{\mathbf{v}})_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i}
for all
i
.

If
p
is sufficiently large but
p\leq N^{12\delta}
, then as
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{V}}
, we have that
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}}
has highest multiplicity less than
|I_{0}|/4
(because in
{\mathcal{V}}
, for any
a
the vector
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}}-a\mathbf{1}
has at least
|I_{0}|-\lambda N/4
non-zero coordinates).
Hence we can apply Lemma
5.14
, provided that
\delta
is small,
to obtain that

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}})\leq\exp(-N^{1/16}).

We now assume
p>N^{12\delta}
. In what follows set

\tau:=N^{1/16}\mbox{ and $k:=\lfloor N^{1/4}\rfloor,s_{1}:=\lfloor N^{1-4\delta}\rfloor,s_{2}:=\lfloor N^{1-2\delta}/2\rfloor$}.

We let
d\leq N
be fixed with
d\geq s_{1}
, and let
C
be a constant which will have to be sufficiently large to an extent that will be specified later.

Note that any non-zero
{\mathbf{v}}
resides in
{\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},>p}
(because as
R^{\delta}_{k}({\mathbf{w}})\leq R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})\leq 2^{2k}|{\mathbf{b}}|^{2k}
, for
t>p
we have that
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}
is empty, and hence

{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq t}
is empty by (
6.22
)).

\clubsuit
Hoi: [added this eqn.]

So with
j_{0}
being the smallest integer such that
2^{j_{0}}\tau>p
, we have an increasing nested sequence
({\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},2^{j}\tau})_{j=0}^{j_{0}}

and we can partition
{\mathcal{V}}
into union of disjoint sets

{\mathcal{V}}=({\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq\tau})\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{j_{0}}({\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq 2^{j}\tau}\cap{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\geq 2^{j-1}\tau}).

Let
X_{i}
be the
i
th row of
L_{N}
. We then have that
{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}})
is

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\exists{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}}\cap{\mathcal{V}},{\mathbf{v}}\cdot X_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i},1\leq i\leq N\Big)={\mathbf{P}}\Big(\exists{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}}\cap{\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\tau},{\mathbf{v}}\cdot X_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i},1\leq i\leq N\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{j_{0}}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\exists{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}}\cap({\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},2^{j}\tau}\cap{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},2^{j-1}\tau}),{\mathbf{v}}\cdot X_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i},1\leq i\leq n\Big).

In what follows, without loss of generality we assume that
\lambda N
is an integer (otherwise one just replaces
\lambda N
by
\lfloor\lambda N\rfloor
in all estimates.)

Case 1.
We begin with vectors in
{\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}}\cap{\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\tau}
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
be the subevent of
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
that there exists a vector
{\mathbf{v}}
in
{\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}}\cap{\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\tau}
such that
L_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
.
Note that as
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\tau}
, we cannot hope for structures of
{\mathbf{v}}
. However, because of the lower bound
e^{-N^{c_{4}}}\leq\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}})
, by Lemma
5.15
there exists a generalized arithmetic progression
P
of rank one in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
and of size
O(p/N^{c_{4}})
that contains at least all but
N^{4c_{4}}
entries of
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}}
.
Note that the number of ways to choose such a
P
is bounded by
p^{O(1)}
. For a fixed
P
, the number of vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}}
with at least
\lambda N-N^{4c_{4}}

components in
P
is at most

(6.32)

\binom{\lambda N}{\lfloor N^{4c_{4}}\rfloor}|P|^{\lambda N-N^{4c_{4}}}p^{N^{4c_{4}}}\leq\binom{\lambda N}{\lfloor N^{4c_{4}}\rfloor}O(p/N^{c_{4}})^{\lambda N-N^{4c_{4}}}p^{N^{4c_{4}}}\leq O(1)^{\lambda N}(p/N^{c_{4}})^{\lambda N},

where we used the fact that
p\leq\exp(N^{c_{1}})
with small
c_{1},c_{4}
.

Together with
p^{O(1)}
(which is trivially bounded by
O(1)^{N}
as
p\leq\exp(N^{c_{1}})
) number of ways to choose
P
, we thus have the following.

Claim 6.33
.

The total number of
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}}
(where
|I_{0}|=\lambda N
) with
e^{-N^{c_{4}}}\leq\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}})
is at most
O(1)^{\lambda N}(p/N^{c_{4}})^{\lambda N}
.

Our next step is similar to the proof of Lemma
6.5
(where there we started with a subvector with high entry multiplicities) that we will try to saturate
{\mathbf{v}}
until there is almost no (GAP-type) structure left.

We will conditioned on the
(x_{kl})_{N+1\leq k\text{ or }N+1\leq l}
and treat
I_{0}
as fixed. Without loss of generality we assume that
I_{0}
is the set
[\lambda N]
of the first
\lambda N
indices.

Let
k_{0}:=\lfloor N^{1-\delta}\rfloor
.
We then divide the index set
[\lambda N+1,\dots,N]
into subsequences
J_{1},\dots,J_{\ell}
of consecutive numbers so that
|J_{i}|=k_{0}
for
i<\ell
and
k_{0}\leq|J_{\ell}|<2k_{0}
.

Given
{\mathbf{v}}
, we partition
[N]
into two subsets,
I_{m}
and
I_{s}
. We let
I_{s}
be the union of the
J_{i}
such that
\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{J_{i}})\geq e^{-N^{c_{4}}}
, along with
[\lambda N]
. We let
I_{m}=[N]\backslash I_{s}
. We write
{\mathbf{v}}_{m}:={\mathbf{v}}_{I_{m}}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}:={\mathbf{v}}_{I_{s}}
.

Notice that as the index set of the components of
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
has the form
I_{0}\cup_{l}J_{i_{l}}
, there are at most
2^{\ell}\leq 2^{N^{\delta}}
such subsets (where we used the fact that
N
sufficiently large given
\delta,\lambda
).

Subcase 1.1.
Let
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
be the event that there exists a
{\mathbf{v}}
as in the definition of
\tilde{{\mathcal{F}}}_{1}
such that
{\mathbf{v}}_{m}
is empty.
Then by repeatedly applying Claim
6.33
we obtain that the number of such vectors
{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{v}}_{s}
is bounded by

(6.34)

O(1)^{\lambda N}(p/N^{c_{4}})^{\lambda N}\left(O(1)^{\lfloor N^{1-\delta}\rfloor}(p/N^{c_{4}})^{\lfloor N^{1-\delta}\rfloor}\right)^{N^{\delta}-\lambda N^{\delta}}O(1)^{2\lfloor N^{1-\delta}\rfloor}(p/N^{c_{4}})^{2\lfloor N^{1-\delta}\rfloor}=O(p^{2N}N^{-c_{4}N/8}),

provided that
N
is sufficiently large, where the first factor comes from the number of
{\mathbf{v}}_{0}
, the second factor comes from the number of
{\mathbf{v}}_{J_{i}}
for all
i<\ell
, and the third factor comes from the number of
{\mathbf{v}}_{J_{\ell}}
.

Then
the probability that any
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}}\cap{\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},\tau}
of the above type and satisfies
{\mathbf{v}}\cdot X_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i},1\leq i\leq N
is bounded by (via (
6.27
), where we note that in the proof there we exploited the randomness of
(x_{kl})_{k,l\in[N],k\neq l}
only)

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1})\leq p^{2N}N^{-c_{4}N/8}\Big(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{3C(N^{1/16}+1)\sqrt{N^{1/4}}}{p\sqrt{N^{1/2}}}\Big)^{N-s_{2}}\leq N^{-c_{4}N/9},

where we used that
N^{12\delta}<p\leq\exp(N^{\delta/2}),s_{2}=\lfloor N^{1-2\delta}/2\rfloor
and
\tau=N^{1/16}
and
N
is sufficiently large and
\delta
is sufficiently small.

Subcase 1.2.
Let
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
be the event that there exists a
{\mathbf{v}}
as in the definition of
\tilde{{\mathcal{F}}}_{1}
such that
{\mathbf{v}}_{m}
is not empty.
We have that
k=\lfloor N^{1-\delta}\rfloor\leq|I_{m}|
and
|I_{s}|\geq\lambda N
.

Similarly to the proof of Lemma
6.5
, we now describe a function
f
from subsets of
[N]
(that can occur as
I_{s}
) to subsets of
[N]
. We describe
f(I_{s})
,
and write
I_{m}
for
[N]\setminus I_{s}
, but note that
f
does not depend
on
{\mathbf{v}}
. If
|I_{s}|>|I_{m}|
, we let
f(I_{s})
be the first
|I_{m}|
elements of
I_{s}
.
Otherwise, we choose
J_{i_{1}},\dots,J_{i_{l}}
arbitrarily from
I_{m}

so that
|I_{s}|-N^{1-\delta}\leq|J_{i_{1}}|+\dots+|J_{i_{l}}|<|I_{s}|

and let
f(I_{s}):=[N]\backslash(J_{i_{1}}\cup\dots\cup J_{i_{l}})
. (Hence, to relate to our proof of Lemma
6.5
, the set
J^{\ast}
there plays the role of
J_{i_{1}}\cup\dots\cup J_{i_{l}}
, and
f(I_{s})
is the union of
I_{s}
and the complement of
J^{\ast}
in
I_{m}
.)

In either case, we have

|f(I_{s})|\geq|I_{m}|\mbox{ and }|[N]\backslash f(I_{s})|\geq|I_{s}|-N^{1-\delta}.

Recall from the proof of Lemma
6.5
that
{\mathcal{E}}_{drop}
is the event that there is a square submatrix
G_{A\times B}
of
G_{N}

of dimension
\geq\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor

with rank less than
|A|-\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor
and we have
{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{drop})\leq e^{-c_{drop}N^{1+2\delta}},

for some
c_{drop}>0
depending on
\alpha
.

We wish to bound the probability of
\mathcal{F}_{2}\setminus{\mathcal{E}}_{drop}
.
Since for
{\mathbf{v}}
causing
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, we have
|I_{m}|\geq N^{1-\delta}
,
and hence
|I_{m}|\geq\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor
(by assuming
\delta<1/4
).
So outside of
{\mathcal{E}}_{drop}
, the matrix
G_{f(I_{s})\times I_{m}}
has rank
\geq|I_{m}|-\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor
,
and hence it has a square submatrix
G_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\times{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}
of dimension
|I_{m}|-\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor
which has full rank.

Given subsets
M
,
{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}
, and
{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}
of
[N]

such that
|{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}|=|{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}|
and
{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}\subset M
and
{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\subset f([N]\setminus M)
, and such that

M
is non-empty and can occur as
I_{m}
, let
\mathcal{F}_{2.1}(M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}})
be the event that there is a
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
such that
\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}})\geq e^{-N^{c_{4}}}
,
G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
, and
I_{m}=M
, and
G_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\times{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}
is full rank.
In particular, above we just saw that
\mathcal{F}_{2}\setminus{\mathcal{E}}_{drop}

implies

\mathcal{F}_{2.1}(M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}})
for some
M
,
{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}
, and
{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}
with
|M|-|{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}|=\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor

and
N-|M|\geq\lambda N
.

Claim 6.35
.

For all
M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{2.1}(M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}})
is defined, we have

{\mathbf{P}}(\mathcal{F}_{2.1}(M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}))\leq N^{-c_{4}\lambda N/9}.

Proof.

By using Claim
6.33
over the
J_{i}
, as
|I_{s}|=N-|M|
, for
{\mathbf{v}}
such that
\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{[\lambda N]})\geq e^{-N^{c_{4}}}
and
I_{m}=M
there are at most

O(1)^{\lambda N}(p/N^{c_{4}})^{\lambda N}\left(O(1)^{\lfloor N^{1-\delta}\rfloor}(p/N^{c_{4}})^{\lfloor N^{1-\delta}\rfloor}\right)^{(N-M-\lambda N)/N^{1-\delta}}=O(1)^{N-M}p^{N-|M|}N^{-c_{4}(N-|M|)/8}

choices of
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{s}}
, and
\binom{N}{N^{1/2+\delta}}p^{N^{1/2+\delta}}
choices for
({\mathbf{v}})_{I_{m}\backslash I_{m}^{\prime}}
. Let
F=f([N]\backslash M)
. We condition on
L_{F\times[N]}
and compute the conditional probability of
\mathcal{F}_{2.1}(M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}})
.
If
G_{{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}\times{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}
is not full rank, then the desired (conditional) probability is
0
. Otherwise, as in the argument of
Claim
6.8
, we have
{\mathbf{v}}_{I^{\prime}_{m}}
is determined (and hence the entire
{\mathbf{v}}
is determined).

We let
J_{*}
be one of the
J_{i}
that is in
M
but has no intersection with
F
.
For each
i\in([N]\setminus F)\setminus J_{*}
, we let
X_{i}
be the
i
th row of
G_{N}
. The equation
X_{i}{\mathbf{v}}=(Y_{0})_{i}
implies

(6.36)

\sum_{j\in J_{*}}x_{ij}(v_{j}-v_{i})=(Y_{0})_{i}-\sum_{j\in[N]\setminus(J_{*}\cup\{i\})}x_{ij}(v_{j}-v_{i})+\sum_{j\in[n+1]\setminus[N]}x_{ij}v_{i}.

The
x_{ij}
for
i\in([N]\setminus F)\setminus J_{*}
and
j\in J_{*}
are all independent.
We further condition on
x_{ij}
for
i\in([N]\setminus F)\setminus J_{*}
and
j\not\in J_{*}
.
Since
J_{*}\cap F=\emptyset
, none of the
x_{ij}
for
i\in([N]\setminus F)\setminus J_{*}
and
j\in J_{*}
have been conditioned on.
Thus after our conditioning, the probability of (
6.36
) holding is at most
\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{J_{*}}).

We have
N-|F|-|J_{*}|
independent such equations that are implied by
G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
.
Thus the probability that, for a given
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{m}\setminus{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}},
we have the event in the claim, is at most

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{J_{*}})^{N-|F|-|J_{*}|}.

Since
J_{*}
is one of the
J_{i}
that is a subset of
I_{m}
, by definition of
I_{m}
we have that

\rho_{L}({\mathbf{v}}_{J_{*}})\leq e^{-N^{c_{4}}}+1/p.

We have
|J_{*}|\leq 2\lfloor N^{1-\delta}\rfloor
and
|M|\leq|F|\leq|M|+N^{1-\delta}
. Thus the probability that, for a given
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{m}\setminus{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}},
we have the event in the claim, is at most

\left(e^{-N^{c_{4}}}+1/p\right)^{N-|M|-3\lfloor N^{1-\delta}\rfloor}.

Summing over the possible values for
{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{m}\setminus{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}}
(which are bounded in number above), we obtain
we thus obtain a probability bound

O(1)^{N-M}p^{N-|M|}N^{-c_{4}(N-|M|)/8}\binom{N}{N^{1/2+\delta}}p^{N^{1/2+\delta}}(e^{-N^{c_{4}}}+1/p)^{N-|M|-3\lfloor N^{1-\delta}\rfloor}\leq N^{-c_{4}\lambda N/9},

where we used the fact that
\delta
is sufficiently small (and
c_{1}=c_{4}=\delta/2
) and
p\leq e^{N^{\delta/2}}
and
|M|\leq N-\lambda N
.
∎

The event
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is the union of
{\mathcal{E}}_{drop}
with
\mathcal{F}_{2.1}(M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}})
over
M,{{I^{\prime}_{m}}},{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}
with
|M|-|{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}|=\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor

and
|N|-|M|\geq\lambda_{N}N
and
M
a possible value of
I_{m}
. There are at most
2^{\ell}\leq 2^{2N^{\delta}}
possible values of
I_{m}
(and hence
M
).
Given
M
, there are at most
\binom{|M|}{\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor}
choices of
{{I^{\prime}_{m}}}
, and
\binom{|f([N]\setminus M)|}{\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor}\leq\binom{|M|+N^{1-\delta}}{\lfloor N^{1/2+\delta}\rfloor}
choices of
{{{{I^{\prime}_{s}}}}}
. Hence,
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{F}}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c_{drop}N^{1+2\delta}}+2^{2N^{\delta}}N^{2N^{1/2+\delta}}N^{-c_{4}\lambda N/9}.

Thus, for
\delta
sufficiently small and
N
sufficiently large given
\delta
and
\lambda
,

{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\leq{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1})+{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{F}}_{2})\leq N^{-c_{4}\lambda N/10}.

For the next case, we use an argument similar to that in
[
FJLS21
]
, but with a modification for the Laplacian case from
Theorem
6.23
.

Case 2.
We next consider the subevent
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
that there exists a vector
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}}\cap\cup_{j=1}^{j_{0}}({\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},2^{j}\tau}^{c}\wedge{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},2^{j-1}\tau})

such that
L_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=Y_{0}
. By Lemma
6.26
(where we can check that all of the conditions are met
we have for some sufficiently large
C^{\prime}>0
depending on
\delta

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2})\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{j_{0}}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\exists{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}}\cap({\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},2^{j}\tau}\cap{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},2^{j-1}\tau}),{\mathbf{v}}\cdot X_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i},1\leq i\leq n\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{j_{0}}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\exists{\mathbf{v}}\in({\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathbf{G}}^{c}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},2^{j}\tau}\cap{\mathbf{G}}_{L,d,k,s_{1},s_{2},2^{j-1}\tau}),{\mathbf{v}}\cdot X_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i},1\leq i\leq n\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\binom{N}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{j_{0}}(C^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}}\left(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{3C(2^{j-1}\tau+1)\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}}\right)^{N-s_{2}}2^{d+4}p^{d+s_{2}+1}(2^{j-1}\tau)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\binom{N}{d}2^{d+4}\sum_{j=1}^{j_{0}}(C^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}}3^{N-s_{2}-1}\left[(\frac{1}{p})^{N-s_{2}}+(\frac{3C\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}+(\frac{3C2^{j-1}\tau\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}\right]p^{d+s_{2}+1}(2^{j-1}\tau)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{j_{0}}(12C^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}+d+s_{2}+1}(\frac{1}{p})^{N-s_{2}}(2^{j-1}\tau)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{j_{0}}(36CC^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}+d+s_{2}+1}(\frac{\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}(2^{j-1}\tau)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{j_{0}}(36CC^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}+d+s_{2}+1}(\frac{2^{j-1}\tau\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}(2^{j-1}\tau)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}.

Recall that
\tau=N^{1/16},k=\lfloor N^{1/4}\rfloor,s_{1}=\lfloor N^{1-4\delta}\rfloor,s_{2}=\lfloor N^{1-2\delta}/2\rfloor
. It is clear that the first two sums are bounded by
N^{-10\delta N}
as long as
\delta
is sufficiently small and
p\geq N^{12\delta}
and
N
are sufficiently large given
\delta
.

For the third sum, for
j=1
we have

(36CC^{\prime})^{N}(\frac{\tau\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}\tau^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}\leq(1/p)^{N/2}.

The sum of other terms can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{j_{0}}(36CC^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}+d+s_{2}+1}(\frac{2^{j-1}\tau\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}(2^{j-1}\tau)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}

\displaystyle=(36CC^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}+d+s_{2}+1}\sum_{j=2}^{j_{0}}(\frac{\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}\tau^{N-s_{2}-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}2^{(j-1)(N-s_{2}-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2}))}.

Note that
N-s_{2}-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})\geq N-s_{2}-N(1-s_{1}/s_{2})=Ns_{1}/s_{2}-s_{2}>0
as
N
is sufficiently large, the above sum is clearly bounded by
(j_{0}-1)
times the last summand when
j=j_{0}
,
and that last term is bounded by (where we recall that
p<2^{j_{0}}\tau\leq 2p
)

\displaystyle(36CC^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}+d+s_{2}+1}(\frac{2^{j_{0}-1}\tau\sqrt{k}}{p\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}(2^{j_{0}-1}\tau)^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}

\displaystyle\leq(36CC^{\prime})^{N}2^{d}p^{N^{\delta/2}+d+s_{2}+1}(\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}p^{-d(1-s_{1}/s_{2})}

\displaystyle\leq(72CC^{\prime})^{N}p^{N^{\delta/2}+s_{2}+ds_{1}/s_{2}}(\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\sqrt{s_{1}}})^{N-s_{2}}\leq N^{-N/4}

because
d\leq N
and
p\leq\exp(N^{c_{1}})
where
c_{1}=\delta/2
and we take
\delta
sufficiently small.

Combining the above estimates, by summing over all
d\leq N
, we can bound
{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
of Case 2 by

{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2})\leq N^{-10\delta N}+N^{-N/4}.

Putting the two cases together we conclude that

{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}})={\mathbf{P}}\Big(\exists{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}_{I_{0}},\mbox{$(G_{N}{\mathbf{v}})_{i}=(Y_{0})_{i}$ for all $i$}\Big)\leq N^{-c_{4}\lambda N/10}+N^{-10\delta N}+N^{-N/4}\leq 3N^{-c_{4}\lambda N/10}

as
c_{4}=\delta/2
and
\delta
is sufficiently small.

To bound the probability of
{\mathcal{F}}
, we need to account also for the analog of
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
where
Y_{0}
is replaced by any vector that shares at least
N-N^{c_{3}}

coordinates with it.
This gives

\binom{N}{N^{c_{3}}}p^{N^{c_{3}}}

total events with the same probability bound as we showed above for
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
, whose union is
{\mathcal{F}}
.
We then have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{F}})\leq

\displaystyle\left(\binom{N}{N^{c_{3}}}p^{N^{c_{3}}}\right)3N^{-c_{4}\lambda N/10}\leq N^{-c_{4}\lambda N/11},

provided that
\delta
is chosen sufficiently small. This, together with (
6.31
),
complete the proof of Proposition
6.29
.
∎

7.
Treatment for moderate primes: proof of Propositions
2.1
and
2.3

We will be focusing mostly on the Laplacian case, the skew-symmetric and symmetric cases will be discussed later (by a simpler argument). The plan for the Laplacian matrix works as follows.

(1)

We first sample
L_{n}
using Phase 1 from Definition
1.23
. For each
N_{0}=\lfloor cn\rfloor\leq N\leq n
(where
c
is a sufficiently small positive constant), we record the index set
I_{N+1}\subset[N]
of
1\leq j\leq N
where
v_{j}
has exactly one neighbor in
\{v_{N+1},v_{N+2}\}
. Recall (
1.27
), by Chernoff’s bound with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Theta(n))
(with respect to
L_{n}
) we have for all
N_{0}\leq N\leq n

(7.1)

(1/2-c)N\leq|I_{N+1}|\leq(1/2+c)N.

(2)

We notice that the random set
I_{N+1}
is independent of the entries
x_{ij},1\leq i<j\leq N
of
L_{N}
. Hence for each
N_{0}\leq N\leq n
we can apply Proposition
5.19
to
L_{N}
with
I_{0}=I_{N}
. To start with, when
N=N_{0}
let us call the event under consideration
{\mathcal{E}}_{N_{0}}^{\ast}
. This event belongs to the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the random entries of
L_{n}
, and

(7.2)

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{N_{0}}^{\ast})\geq 1-O(\exp(-N^{c}))=1-O(\exp(-\Theta(n^{c}))).

(3)

We next reshuffle the neighbors of
v_{N_{0}+1}
and
v_{N_{0}+2}
to create new randomness in the column corresponding to
v_{N_{0}+1}
. After adding this column to
L_{N_{0}}
we obtain
L_{N_{0}+1}^{\ast}
, where we use the asterisk to distinguish with
L_{N_{0}+1}
of
L_{n}
.

•

On the one hand, conditioning on
{\mathcal{E}}_{N_{0}}^{\ast}
, we can use the randomness of the column corresponding to
v_{N_{0}+1}
to study the rank relation of
L_{N_{0}+1}^{\ast}
with that of
L_{N_{0}}
.

•

By Fact
1.26
,
