L_{N_{0}+1}^{\ast}
has the same distribution as
L_{N_{0}+1}
(of
L_{n}
), hence we can apply Proposition
5.19
to
L_{N_{0}+1}^{\ast}
with respect to
I_{0}=I_{N_{0}+1}
. Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{N_{0}+1}^{\ast}
denote the overwhelming event obtained by this result (with probability similarly to (
7.2
)), which now belongs to the sigma-algebra generated by
L_{n}
and the new random variables of the column corresponding to
v_{N_{0}+1}
.

We will repeat the process until
v_{n}
.

L_{N}^{\ast}
x_{N+1}
I_{N+1}
X_{N+1}

Figure 3
.
Row and column exposure process during reshuffling.

With the outline in mind, we will study the ranking evolution when moving from
L_{N}^{\ast}
to
L_{N+1}^{\ast}
.

Definition 7.3
.

Given
I_{0}\subset[N]
. A random vector
X=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is called
I_{0}
-adapted if the
x_{i}
are i.i.d. copies of
\xi
satisfying Eq. (
1.2
) (for a given prime
p
) for all
i\in I_{0}
, while
x_{i},i\notin I_{0}
are deterministic (i.e. held fixed).

We first prove an elementary decoupling result to motivate our later treatment (see also
[
CTV06
,
Map13b
,
KN22
]
).

Lemma 7.4
.

Assume that
a_{ij}\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
and
a_{ij}=a_{ji}
and
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
. Assume
X
is as in
7.3
for a given
I_{0}
. Then for any
I\subset[n]
we have

\sup_{r}|{\mathbf{P}}(\sum_{ij}a_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}+\sum_{i}b_{i}x_{i}=r)-1/p|^{4}\leq|{\mathbf{P}}(\sum_{i\in I,j\in I^{c}}a_{ij}y_{i}y_{j}=0)-1/p|,

where
y_{i},i\in I_{0}
are i.i.d. copies of
\xi-\xi^{\prime}
.

Proof of Lemma
7.4
.

For short we write
f(X)=\sum_{ij}a_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}+\sum_{i}b_{i}x_{i}
. We write

|{\mathbf{P}}(f(X)=r)-\frac{1}{p}|\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\neq 0}|{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(-tf(X))|

where
e_{p}(x)=e^{2\pi ix/p}
.

We then use Cauchy-Schwarz to complete squares,

\displaystyle LHS^{2}\leq\frac{p-1}{p^{2}}\sum_{t\neq 0}|{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(-tf(X))|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{p-1}{p^{2}}\sum_{t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I}}|{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I^{c}}}e_{p}(-tf(X_{I},X_{I^{c}}))|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{p-1}{p^{2}}\sum_{t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I}}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I^{c}},X^{\prime}_{I^{c}}}e_{p}(-t[f(X_{I},X_{I^{c}})-f(X_{I},X_{I^{c}}^{\prime})])

\displaystyle=\frac{p-1}{p^{2}}\sum_{t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I^{c}},X^{\prime}_{I^{c}}}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I}}e_{p}(-t[f(X_{I},X_{I^{c}})-f(X_{I},X_{I^{c}}^{\prime})]).

Using Cauchy-Schwarz once more,

\displaystyle LHS^{4}

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{p-1}{p^{2}})^{2}(p-1)\sum_{t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I^{c}},X^{\prime}_{I^{c}}}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I},X_{I}^{\prime}}e_{p}(-t[f(X_{I},X_{I^{c}})-f(X_{I},X_{I^{c}}^{\prime})-f(X_{I}^{\prime},X_{I^{c}})+f(X_{I}^{\prime},X_{I^{c}}^{\prime})])

\displaystyle=(\frac{p-1}{p^{2}})^{2}p(p-1)\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}_{Y_{I},Y_{I^{c}}}e_{p}(-t\sum_{i\in I,j\in I^{c}}a_{ij}y_{i}y_{j})=(\frac{p-1}{p})^{3}({\mathbf{P}}(\sum_{i\in I,j\in I^{c}}a_{ij}y_{i}y_{j}=0)-1/p).

∎

By Lemma
5.5
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Theta(n))
we can assume that
L_{N}^{\ast}
has rank at least
N-cN/2
for each
N+0\leq N\leq n
.
Let us consider the event that
L_{N}^{\ast}
has rank exactly
N-k
(where
k\leq cN/2
).

Claim 7.5
.

Assume that
L_{N}^{\ast}
has rank
N-k
, then there is a set
I\subset[N],|I|=N-k
such that the principle minor matrix
L_{I\times I}^{\ast}
has full rank
N-k
.

Proof of Claim
7.5
.

Assume without loss of generality that
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}(L_{N}^{\ast}),\dots,{\mathbf{r}}_{n-k}(L_{N}^{\ast})
span the row vectors of
L_{N}^{\ast}
, then in particularly
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}|_{[N-k]},i\geq N-k+1
belong to the span of
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}|_{[N-k]},1\leq i\leq n-k
. This implies that the matrix spanned by the first
N-k
columns
X_{1},\dots,X_{N-k}
has rank at most
N-k
. On the other hand, as the matrix is symmetric, this column matrix has the same rank as that of the matrix generated by
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}(L_{N}^{\ast}),\dots,{\mathbf{r}}_{N-k}(L_{N}^{\ast})
, which is
N-k
. So the matrix
L_{[N-k]\times[N-k]}
generated by
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}|_{[N-k]},1\leq i\leq N-k
has rank
N-k
.
∎

In what follows, assume again without loss of generality that
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}(L_{N}^{\ast}),\dots,{\mathbf{r}}_{N-k}(L_{N}^{\ast})
span the row vectors of
L_{N}^{\ast}
. When we add a new column
X_{N+1}=(x_{1(N+1)},\dots,x_{(N+1)(N+1)}):=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N+1})
and its transpose to create
L_{N+1}^{\ast}
, if
{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N+1}^{\ast})<{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})+2
then the extended row vector
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}(L_{N+1}^{\ast}),\dots,{\mathbf{r}}_{N-k}(L_{N+1}^{\ast})
still generate the space of the vectors
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}(L_{N+1}^{\ast}),\dots,{\mathbf{r}}_{N}(L_{N+1}^{\ast})
. In particularly, this implies that

(7.6)

x_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{N-k}a_{ij}x_{j},\text{for $N-k+1\leq i\leq N$,}

where
a_{ij}
are determined from
L_{N}^{\ast}
via
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(L_{N}^{\ast})=\sum_{j=1}^{N-k}a_{ij}{\mathbf{r}}_{j}(L_{N}^{\ast})
. In other words, Eq. (
7.6
) says that the vector
(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
is orthogonal to the vectors
(a_{i1},\dots,a_{i(N-k)},0,\dots,-1,0,\dots,0)
, or equivalently it belongs to the hyperplane
H_{N}
generated by the column vectors of
L_{N}^{\ast}
.

Now, by using our result on the normal vectors in the previous section, we will obtain a bound
p^{-k}+O(e^{-n^{c}})
for this event.

Lemma 7.7
.

Assume that
1\leq k\leq cN
. There exists a positive constant
c
so that for any sufficiently large prime
p
at most
\exp(n^{c})
there exists an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast}
with
{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast})\geq 1-\exp(-n^{c})
such that the following holds

{\mathbf{P}}\big(X_{N+1}|_{[N]}\in H_{N}\big|{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})=N-k\wedge{\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast}\big)=\frac{1}{p^{k}}+O(\exp(-n^{c})).

We also refer the reader to
[
Map13a
, Theorem 1.1]
,
[
NP20
, Theorem A.1, A.4]
, and
[
NW22
, Theorem 8.2]
for similar results for random i.i.d. matrices as well as its i.i.d. Laplacian.

To prove Lemma
7.7
, we first show the following analog of
[
LMN20
, Lemma 7.1]
and
[
KN22
, Lemma 2.11]
for adapted vectors.

Lemma 7.8
.

Assume that
H
is a subspace in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
of codimension
k
, and such that for any
{\mathbf{w}}\in H^{\perp}
we have

\rho({\mathbf{w}}_{I_{0}})\leq\delta_{0}.

Then with
X
being adapted to
I_{0}
as in
7.3
we have

|{\mathbf{P}}(X\in H)-1/p^{k}|\leq 2\delta_{0}.

Proof of Lemma
7.8
.

We have the following identity,

\displaystyle 1_{a_{1}=0\wedge\dots\wedge a_{k}=0}=\frac{1}{p^{k}}\sum_{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}e_{p}(a_{1}t_{1}+\dots+a_{k}t_{k}).

Therefore, letting
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{k}
be a basis for
H^{\perp}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(X\in H)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{P}}(X\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge X\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{k})

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}\Big[\frac{1}{p^{k}}\sum_{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}e_{p}(t_{1}[X\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{1}]+\dots+t_{k}[X\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{d}])\Big]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}\Big[\frac{1}{p^{k}}+\frac{1}{p^{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\\
\text{ not all $t_{i}$ are zero}\end{subarray}}e_{p}(t_{1}[X\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{1}]+\dots+t_{k}[X\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{k}])\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{k}}+\frac{1}{p^{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\\
\text{ not all $t_{i}$ are zero}\end{subarray}}{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(t_{1}[X\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{1}]+\dots+t_{k}[X\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{k}]).

We now split the sum into projective equivalence classes. Let
\sim
be the equivalence relation given by
(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})\sim(t_{1}^{\prime},\dots,t_{k}^{\prime})
if there exists
t\neq 0
such that
(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})=(t\cdot t_{1}^{\prime},\dots,t\cdot t_{k}^{\prime})
. Not worrying about our choice of representative on account of our inner sum,

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{k}}+\frac{1}{p^{k}}\sum_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})\in({\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N})^{\times}/\sim}\bigg[\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}}e_{p}(t(t_{1}[X\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{1}]+\dots+t_{k}[X\cdot{\mathbf{v}}_{k}]))\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{k}}+\frac{1}{p^{k}}\sum_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})\in({\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N})^{\times}/\sim}p\bigg[\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}}e_{p}(t(X\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}{\mathbf{v}}_{i}))\bigg].

Now observe,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}}e_{p}(t(X\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}{\mathbf{v}}_{i}))={\mathbf{P}}(X\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}{\mathbf{v}}_{i}=0)-1/p.

The
{\mathbf{v}}_{i}
are a basis and the
t_{i}
are not all zero so
\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}{\mathbf{v}}_{i}
is a nonzero element of
H^{\perp}
. By assumption then this is bounded by
\delta_{0}
. There are
\frac{p^{k}-1}{p-1}
elements in
(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})\in({\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{n})^{\times}/\sim
. We have
\frac{p(p^{k}-1)}{p^{k}(p-1)}\leq 2
. So by the triangle inequality

\displaystyle|{\mathbf{P}}(X\in H)-1/p^{k}|\leq 2\delta_{0}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
7.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast}
be the event from Proposition
5.19
applied to
L_{N}^{\ast}
for
I_{0}=I_{N+1}
. Under
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast}
we have learned that for any
{\mathbf{w}}\in H_{N}
then
\rho_{L}({\mathbf{w}}_{I_{N+1}})=O(\exp(-n^{c}))
, where
I_{N+1}
is the given index set where
X_{N+1}
is adapted.
∎

For symmetric matrices
{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N+1}^{\ast})\leq{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})+1
does not automatically implies that
{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N+1}^{\ast})={\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})
, so we have to consider the events
L_{N+1}^{\ast})={\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})
and
{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N+1}^{\ast})={\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})+1

separately
. Let’s consider the first event that

{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N+1}^{\ast})={\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast}).

Here beside the event
X_{N+1}|_{[N]}\in H_{N}
considered in Lemma
7.7
,
X_{N+1}={\mathbf{r}}_{N+1}(L_{N+1}^{\ast})
also belongs to the subspace generated by
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}(L_{N+1}^{\ast}),\dots,{\mathbf{r}}_{N}(L_{N+1}^{\ast})
. This latter condition can be interpreted in quadratic forms as follows. Conditioning on
{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})=N-k\wedge{\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast}
, by Claim
7.5
we can assume that
L_{I\times I}^{\ast}
is a submatrix of full rank
N-k
in
L_{N}^{\ast}
, for some
I\subset[N]
and
|I|=N-k
. Let
B=(b_{ij})
be the inverse of
L_{I\times I}^{\ast}
in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
. The exposure of
(x_{1},\dots,x_{N-k},x_{N+1})
would then increase the rank of
L_{N+1}^{\ast}
except when

(7.9)

\sum_{ij}b_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}+x_{N+1}=0.

This leads us to consider the following for adapted vectors.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
k\geq 0
. Let
H_{N}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast}
be as in the proof of Lemma
7.7
. Let
X
be as in
7.3
for a given index set
I_{0}
satisfying (
7.1
). For any sufficiently large prime
p
at most
\exp(n^{c})
, we have

\Big|{\mathbf{P}}\big(X\in H_{N}\wedge f(X)=0|{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})=N-k\wedge{\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast}\big)-p^{-k-1}\Big|=O(\exp(-\Theta(n^{c}))),

where
f(X)=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq N-k}b_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}+x_{N+1}
.

Proof of Lemma
7.10
.

We write

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(X\in H_{N}\wedge f(X)=0)

\displaystyle=p^{-k-1}\sum_{\xi\in H_{N}^{\perp},t\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(X\cdot\xi+f(X)t)

\displaystyle=p^{-k-1}+p^{-k-1}\sum_{\xi\in H_{N}^{\perp},t\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}},t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(X\cdot\xi+f(X)t)+p^{-k-1}\sum_{\xi\neq 0,\xi\in H_{N}^{\perp}}{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(X\cdot\xi).

Note that the third sum is

p^{-k-1}\sum_{\xi\neq 0,\xi\in H_{N}^{\perp}}{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(X\cdot\xi)=p^{-k-1}(\sum_{\xi\in H_{N}^{\perp}}{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(X\cdot\xi)-1)=\frac{1}{p}{\mathbf{P}}(X\in H_{N})-p^{-k-1}.

Hence the third sum can be bounded by
O(\exp(-n^{c}))
in absolute value using the condition on
G_{n}
from Lemma
7.7
.

For the second sum, we have

|p^{-k-1}\sum_{\xi\in H_{N}^{\perp},t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(X\cdot\xi+f(X)t|\leq\Big(p^{-k-1}\sum_{\xi\in H_{N}^{\perp},t\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}},t\neq 0}|{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(X\cdot\xi+f(X)t|^{4}\Big)^{1/4}.

Recall that
|I_{0}|
satisfies (
7.1
). For now we let
I_{2}\subset I_{0}
of size
\lfloor cN\rfloor
, and let
I_{1}=I\backslash I_{2}
.
By using Cauchy-Schwarz as in the proof of Lemma
7.4
, we then bound this by

\displaystyle\ \ \ p^{-k-1}\sum_{\xi\in H_{N}^{\perp},t\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}},t\neq 0}\big|{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(X\cdot\xi+f(X)t\big|^{4}

\displaystyle\leq p^{-k-1}\sum_{\xi\in H_{N}^{\perp},t\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}},t\neq 0}\Big|{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I_{1}}}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I_{2}},X_{I_{2}}^{\prime}}e_{p}\big((X_{I_{2}}-X_{I_{2}}^{\prime})\cdot\xi+(f(X_{I_{1}},X_{I_{2}})-f(X_{I_{1}},X_{I_{2}}^{\prime}))t\big)\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq p^{-k-1}\sum_{\xi\in H_{N}^{\perp},t\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}},t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I_{2}},X_{I_{2}}^{\prime}}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{I_{1}},X_{I_{2}}}e_{p}\Big((f(X_{I_{1}},X_{I_{2}})-f(X_{I_{1}},X_{I_{2}}^{\prime})-f(X_{I_{1}}^{\prime},X_{I_{2}})+f(X_{I_{1}}^{\prime},X_{I_{2}}^{\prime}))t\Big)

\displaystyle=p^{-k-1}\sum_{\xi\in H_{N}^{\perp},t\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}},t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}e_{p}(\sum_{i\in I_{1},j\in I_{2}}b_{ij}y_{i}y_{j}t)=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}},t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}e_{p}(\sum_{i\in I_{1},j\in I_{2}}b_{ij}y_{i}y_{j}t)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{P}}(Y_{I_{1}}^{\prime}\cdot BY_{I_{2}}^{\prime}=0)-\frac{1}{p},

where
y_{i},i\in I_{0}
are i.i.d. copies of the the random variable
\xi-\xi^{\prime}
, and where
Y_{I_{1}}^{\prime}
and
Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime}
are the vectors in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{I}
obtained from
Y_{I_{1}}
(i.e.
Y_{I}|_{I_{1}}
) and
Y_{I_{2}}
(i.e.
Y_{I}|_{I_{2}}
) resp. by appending zero entries.

Let
J=[N]\backslash I
, so
|J|=k
and that
[N]=I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup J
. We let
B^{\prime}
be the symmetric matrix of size
n
obtained from
B
by simply adding zero entries. Similarly, let
Y_{I_{1}}^{\prime\prime}
and
Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime\prime}
be the vectors in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
obtained from
Y_{I_{1}}^{\prime}
and
Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime}
by appending zero components. Then
Y_{I_{1}}^{\prime}\cdot BY_{I_{2}}^{\prime}=0
is equivalent to

Y_{I_{1}}^{\prime\prime}\cdot B^{\prime}Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime\prime}=0.

Furthermore, notice that the vector
Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime\prime}
is non-zero (in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
) with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Theta(n))
(because
Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime\prime}
has
\lfloor cN\rfloor
i.i.d. entries) and that

L_{N}^{\ast}(B^{\prime}Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime\prime})=(L_{N}^{\ast}B^{\prime})Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime\prime}=Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime\prime},

where we used the fact that
B=(b_{ij})
is the inverse of
L_{I\times I}^{\ast}
in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
.

It follows by Proposition
5.19
that on
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast}

\rho_{L}(B^{\prime}Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime\prime}|_{I_{0}\cap I_{1}})\leq\exp(-\Theta(n^{c})).

Conditioned on such
Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime\prime}
, by (
5.3
)

|{\mathbf{P}}_{Y_{I_{1}}^{\prime}}(Y_{I_{1}}^{\prime}\cdot BY_{I_{2}}^{\prime}=0)-\frac{1}{p}|=|{\mathbf{P}}_{Y_{I_{1}}^{\prime}}(Y_{I_{1}}^{\prime\prime}\cdot B^{\prime}Y_{I_{2}}^{\prime\prime}=0)-\frac{1}{p}|\leq\exp(-\Theta(n^{c})).

∎

Having obtained the necessary concentration bounds, we will next put things together to obtain the rank statistics for the three matrix models, our method is similar to that of
[
Map13b
,
KN22
]
.

Proposition 7.11

(Rank relations for Laplacian matrices)
.

There exists a positive constant
c
such that the following holds. Assume that
p
is a sufficiently large prime and
p\leq\exp(n^{c})
. For
N_{0}\leq N\leq n
there exists an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast}
on the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
L_{n}
and by the neighbor reshuffling process up to step
N
such that
{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast})\geq 1-\exp(-n^{c})
and for
k\leq cN

•

assume that
k\geq 1
then

\Big|{\mathbf{P}}\big({\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N+1}^{\ast})\leq{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})+1|{\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast}\wedge{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})=N-k)\big)-\frac{1}{p^{k}}\Big|\leq\exp(-\Theta(n^{c}));

•

assume that
k\geq 0
then

\Big|{\mathbf{P}}\big({\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N+1}^{\ast})={\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})|{\mathcal{E}}_{N}^{\ast}\wedge{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{N}^{\ast})=N-k)\big)-\frac{1}{p^{k+1}}\Big|\leq\exp(-\Theta(n^{c})).

By a similar much much simpler method (where we don’t have to do the reshuffling process, and where
I_{0}=[N]
at each step, but we still need variants of Lemma
7.7
and Lemma
7.10
with obvious modifications) we also obtain the following for symmetric matrices.

Proposition 7.12

(Rank relations for symmetric matrices)
.

There exists a positive constant
c
such that the following holds. Assume that
p
is a sufficiently large prime and
p\leq\exp(n^{c})
. For
N_{0}\leq N\leq n
there exists an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
on the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the first
N
rows (and columns) of the matrix
M_{N}=M_{N\times N}
(as a principle minor of
M_{n}
from Theorem
1.3
) such that
{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{N})\geq 1-\exp(-n^{c})
and for
k\leq cN

•

assume that
k\geq 1
then

\Big|{\mathbf{P}}\big({\operatorname{rank}}(M_{N+1})\leq{\operatorname{rank}}(M_{N})+1|{\mathcal{E}}_{N}\wedge{\operatorname{rank}}(M_{N})=N-k)\big)-\frac{1}{p^{k}}\Big|\leq\exp(-\Theta(n^{c}));

•

assume that
k\geq 0
then

\Big|{\mathbf{P}}\big({\operatorname{rank}}(M_{N+1})={\operatorname{rank}}(M_{N})|{\mathcal{E}}_{N}\wedge{\operatorname{rank}}(M_{N})=N-k)\big)-\frac{1}{p^{k+1}}\Big|\leq\exp(-\Theta(n^{c})).

Next we comment on random skew-symmetric matrices. Here the rank evolution is slightly simpler and different. We note that, similarly to random symmetric matricers, Lemma
7.7
also works for random skew-symmetric matrices. Let us call
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
the event under consideration. The key difference here is that the event that for skew-symmetric matrices,
{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N+1})\leq{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N})+1
implies that
{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N+1})={\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N})
. This is because if
X_{N+1}|_{[N]}=A_{N}Y_{0}
for some
Y_{0}
, then
Y_{0}^{T}\cdot X_{N+1}|_{[N]}=0
, and so
Y_{0}^{T}A_{[N]\times[N+1]}=X_{N+1}^{T}
, that is the
(N+1)
-th row also belongs the the linear space spanned by the first
n
rows. Thus Lemma
7.7
applied to skew-symmetric matrices imply

Proposition 7.13

(Rank relations for skew-symmetric matrices)
.

There exists a positive constant
c
such that the following holds.
Assume that
p
is a sufficiently large prime and
p\leq\exp(n^{c})
. For
N_{0}\leq N\leq n
there exists an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
on the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the first
N
rows (and columns) of the matrix
A_{N}=A_{N\times N}
(as a principle minor of
A_{N}
from Theorem
1.4
) such that
{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{N})\geq 1-\exp(-n^{c})
and for
k\leq cN

4
4

4

Strictly speaking, Lemma
7.7
just gave
k\geq 1
, but for
k=0
the bound automatically holds with probability one.
then

\Big|{\mathbf{P}}\big({\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N+1})={\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N})|{\mathcal{E}}_{N}\wedge{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N})=N-k\big)-\frac{1}{p^{k}}\Big|=O(\exp(-n^{c})).

One can then deduce the asymptotic probability of the event
{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N+1})={\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N})+2
under
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}\wedge{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N})=N-k
. We note that the transition probability
{\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N+1})={\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N}))
of the skew-symmetric case above is quite similar to that of the i.i.d. models considered in
[
Map13a
,
NW22
,
NP20
]
(where there we expose the first
N
column vectors of the matrix of size
n
). However the difference here is that
{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N+1})
jumps by 2 (rather than 1) in the complement event.

To complete the subsection, we remark that Propositions
7.12
and
7.13
automatically extend to the uniform models where
M_{N},A_{N}
are uniformly chosen from the set of all symmetric matrices and skew-symmetric matrices in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
because the uniformly chosen entries are clearly
\alpha
-balanced.

7.1.
The rank evolving process: completing the proof of Proposition
2.1
and Proposition
2.3

Let
G_{N}
be either
A_{N},M_{N}
or
L_{N}^{\ast}
. We will start from
N=N_{0}=\lfloor cn\rfloor
, which is assumed to possess
{\mathcal{E}}_{N_{0}}
and have rank at least
N_{0}-N_{0}^{1/2+c}
, where we have learned that the latter event has probability at least
1-\exp(\Theta(n))
by Lemma
5.5
. We will apply Propositions
7.13
and
7.11
for
G_{N_{0}+1},\dots,G_{n}
. To compare with the rank evolution of the uniform model, for convenience we will use the following result from
[
NW22
, Theorem 5.3]
.

Theorem 7.14
.

Let
x_{N_{0}},\dots,x_{n},g_{N_{0}-1},\dots,g_{n-1}
be a sequence of random variables.
Let
y_{N_{0}},\dots,y_{n}
be a sequence of random variables where
y_{N_{0}}=x_{N_{0}}
. We assume each
x_{N},y_{N}
takes on at most countably many values, and
g_{N}\in\{0,1\}
.
Suppose that for
0\leq N\leq n-1
,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(y_{N+1}=s|y_{N}=r)={\mathbf{P}}(x_{N+1}=s|x_{N}=r\textrm{ and }g_{N}=1)+\delta(N,r,s)

\displaystyle\textrm{ for all $r$ and $s$
s.t. ${\mathbf{P}}(y_{N}=r){\mathbf{P}}(x_{N}=r\textrm{ and }g_{N}=1)\neq 0$}.

Then for any set
A
of values taken by
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle|{\mathbf{P}}(x_{n}\in A)-{\mathbf{P}}(y_{n}\in A)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{N=N_{0}}^{n-1}\sum_{r}\sum_{s}|\delta(N,r,s)|{\mathbf{P}}(x_{N}=r)+\sum_{N=N_{0}}^{n-1}{\mathbf{P}}(g_{N}\neq 1),

where
r
is summed over
\{r\ |\ {\mathbf{P}}(x_{N}=r)\neq 0\textrm{ and }{\mathbf{P}}(y_{N}=r)\neq 0)\}
and

s
is summed over
\{s\ |\ {\mathbf{P}}(x_{N+1}=s)\neq 0\textrm{ or }{\mathbf{P}}(y_{N+1}=s)\neq 0)\}.

For the detailed statistics of skew-symmetric matrices, let
\mu_{alt,n}
be the rank distribution of the uniform skew-symmetric model in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
(i.e. each non-diagonal entry is independent uniform in
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
). By
[
Mac69
]
we have

\mu_{alt,n}(k)={\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(A_{n,uniform}=n-k)=\frac{N_{0}(n,n-k)}{p^{\binom{n}{2}}}

where

N_{0}(n,2h)=\prod_{i=1}^{h}\frac{p^{2i-2}}{p^{2i}-1}\prod_{i=0}^{2h-1}(p^{n-i}-1)\mbox{ and }N_{0}(2h+1)=0.

However we cannot apply Theorem
7.14
directly to
x_{N}={\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N})
and
y_{N}={\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N,uniform})
and
g_{N}=1_{{\mathcal{E}}_{N}}
because the above uniform statistics is grown from a zero-dimension matrix. To amend this, we can start from any realization of
A_{N_{0}}^{\prime}=A_{N_{0}}
and add rows and columns according to the uniform model until
A_{n}^{\prime}
. We call this model uniform with initial matrix
A_{N_{0}}
. Then
[
KN22
, Proposition 3.13]
applied to this
A_{n}^{\prime}
shows that its rank statistics is extremely close to the uniform model, that

d_{TV}(n-{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{n}^{\prime}/p),\mu_{alt,n})\leq p^{-\Theta(n)}.

Now we can apply Theorem
7.14
and use the triangle inequality to conclude that

d_{TV}(n-{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{n}/p),\mu_{alt,n})\leq e^{-n^{c}}.

On the other hand, by
[
FG15
, Section 5]

d_{TV}(\mu_{alt,n},\mu_{alt,e})=O(\frac{1}{p^{n+1}})\mbox{ if $n$ is even}

and

d_{TV}(\mu_{alt,n},\mu_{alt,o})=O(\frac{1}{p^{n+1}})\mbox{ if $n$ is odd}

where the limiting distributions are given by

{\mathbf{P}}(\mu_{alt,e}=k):=\begin{cases}\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1})\frac{p^{k}}{\prod_{i=1}^{k}(p^{i}-1)},&\mbox{$k$ is even}\\
0,\mbox{ $k$ is odd}\end{cases}

and

{\mathbf{P}}(\mu_{alt,o}=k):=\begin{cases}\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1})\frac{p^{k}}{\prod_{i=1}^{k}(p^{i}-1)},&\mbox{$k$ is odd}\\
0,\mbox{ $k$ is even}\end{cases}

Putting together

Theorem 7.15

(Ranks statistics of random skew-symmetric matrices)
.

Assume that
p
is a sufficiently large prime and
p\leq\exp(n^{c})
. Assume that
A_{n}
is as in Theorem
1.4
, then

d_{TV}(n-{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{n}/p),\mu_{alt,e})=O(e^{-n^{c}}),\text{ $n$ is even}

and

d_{TV}(n-{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{n}/p),\mu_{alt,o})=O(e^{-n^{c}}),\text{ $n$ is odd.}

The claim of Proposition
2.3
then follows easily because

\sum_{k\geq 3}{\mathbf{P}}(\mu_{alt,e}=k)=\sum_{k\geq 4}{\mathbf{P}}(\mu_{alt,e}=k)=O(\frac{1}{p^{6}})

and

\sum_{k\geq 3}{\mathbf{P}}(\mu_{alt,o}=k)=O(\frac{1}{p^{3}}).

Now for symmetric and Laplacian matrices, let with
\mu_{sym,n}
be the rank distribution of the random uniform symmetric matrix of size
n
where each
x_{ij},1\leq i\leq j\leq n
are independent uniform over
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
. By
[
Car54
,
Mac69
]
we have

\mu_{sym,n}(k)={\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(M_{uniform}=n-k))=\frac{N(n,n-k)}{p^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}

where

N(n,2h)=\prod_{i=1}^{h}\frac{p^{2i}}{p^{2i}-1}\prod_{i=0}^{2h-1}(p^{n-i}-1),2h\leq n

and

N(n,2h+1)=\prod_{i=1}^{h}\frac{p^{2i}}{p^{2i}-1}\prod_{i=0}^{2h}(p^{n-i}-1),2h+1\leq n.

Arguing as in the skew-symmetric case, we can grow the uniform model from
G_{N_{0}}^{\prime}=G_{N_{0}}
, and add rows and columns according to the uniform model to form
G_{N_{0}+1}^{\prime}
and so on until
G_{n}^{\prime}
. Then
[
KN22
, Proposition 3.13]
applied to this uniform model
G_{n}^{\prime}
with initial
G_{N_{0}}
shows that its rank statistics is extremely close to the uniform model, and hence by Theorem
7.14
and by the triangle inequality

d_{TV}(n-{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{n}/p),\mu_{sym,n})\leq e^{-n^{c}}.

On the other hand, by
[
FG15
, Section 4]
we have

d_{TV}(\mu_{sym,n},\mu_{sym})=O(\frac{1}{p^{n+1}})

where

{\mathbf{P}}(\mu_{sym}=k)=\frac{\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(1-p^{-2i-1})}{\prod_{i=1}^{k}(p^{i}-1)}.

Putting together we have thus obtained

Theorem 7.16

(Ranks statistics of random symmetric matrices)
.

Assume that
p
is a sufficiently large prime and
p\leq\exp(n^{c})
. Assume that
M_{n}
is as in Theorem
1.3
, then

d_{TV}(n-{\operatorname{rank}}(M_{n}/p),\mu_{sym})=O(e^{-n^{c}}).

Theorem 7.17

(Ranks statistics of random Laplacian matrices)
.

Assume that
p
is a sufficiently large prime and
p\leq\exp(n^{c})
. Assume that
L_{n}
is as in Theorem
1.22
, then

d_{TV}(n-{\operatorname{rank}}(L_{n}/p),\mu_{sym})=O(e^{-n^{c}}).

Proposition
2.1
then follows because

\sum_{k\geq 2}{\mathbf{P}}(\mu_{sym}=k)=O(\frac{1}{p^{3}}).

To conclude the section, as
G_{n}=A_{n},M_{n},L_{n}
are integral, if
\det(G_{n})=0
then for any prime
p
we have
G_{n}/p
is singular, i.e.
{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{n}/p)\leq n-1
. Using our result of corank comparison, by choosing
p
sub-exponentially large we obtain the following bound for singularity.

Corollary 7.18

(Singularity of skew-symmetric, symmetric and Laplacian matrices)
.

There exists a positive constant
c>0
so that

{\mathbf{P}}(\det(A_{2n})=0)=O(\exp(-n^{c}));{\mathbf{P}}(\det(M_{n})=0)=O(\exp(-n^{c}));

and

{\mathbf{P}}(\det(L_{n})=0)=O(\exp(-n^{c})).

For the random symmetric model we also refer the reader to
[
CMMM21
,
FJ19
,
Ver14
]
for analogous sub-exponential bounds (with explicit constants), and to a more recent work
[
CJMS21
]
for exponential bounds. Our results for the skew-symmetric and Laplacian cases are new.

Remark 7.19
.

While the current paper was under preparation, a recent paper by Ferber et. al.
[
FJSS21
]
has obtained a similar result to Theorem
7.16
for Bernoulli matrices with explicit
c
.
Here in the symmetric case we established this result for general random matrices of balanced entries. We also obtained similar results for the skew-symmetric model, which has different and interesting rank statistics. However, as the reader can see, our main difficulty for the moderate primes lies in Laplacian matrices, where we had to look at the local structures of the generalized normal vectors of
L_{N}
over a random index set
I_{N+1}
. In fact, prior to this work, not much has been known about this complicated model from the viewpoint of rank statistics and singularity. The only results that we are aware of for the Laplacian are from
[
Woo17
]
, where it justifies Theorem
7.17
for relatively small
p
, and that
{\mathbf{P}}(\det(L_{n})=0)=1-o(1)
for a somewhat implicit rate of convergence.

8.
Treatment for large primes: control of blowing up and quadratic inverse theorems

While in the previous sections we are able to control all primes up to
\exp(n^{c})
, our treatment for larger primes cannot follow the same way because taking union bound over all large primes is extremely costly. We cannot take union bound even with the conjectural forms of Theorems
7.15
,
7.16
and
7.17
that the error bounds are of form
\exp(-cn)
in place of
\exp(-n^{c})
because there are
n^{cn}
primes to handle.
To avoid this obstacle, we will find a common structure that is passable to
all
large primes simultaneously. In general, this idea was also applied in
[
NW22
]
to treat with random non-symmetric matrices, but the extension to symmetric and Laplacian matrices does require significantly new ideas.

Our main results of this section are Theorem
8.3
and Theorem
8.14
. To start with, we first recall from
[
TV10b
]
a notion of additive structures in abelian groups. Let
G
be an (additive) abelian group.

Definition 8.1
.

A set
Q
is a
generalized arithmetic progression
(GAP) of
rank
r
if it can be expressed as in the form

Q=\{a_{0}+x_{1}a_{1}+\dots+x_{r}a_{r}|M_{i}\leq x_{i}\leq M_{i}^{\prime}\hbox{ and $x_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}$ for all }1\leq i\leq r\}

for some elements
a_{0},\ldots,a_{r}
of
G
, and for some integers
M_{1},\ldots,M_{r}
and
M^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,M^{\prime}_{r}
.

It is convenient to think of
Q
as
the image of an integer box
B:=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{r}|M_{i}\leq x_{i}\leq M_{i}^{\prime}\}
under the linear map

\Phi:(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})\mapsto a_{0}+x_{1}a_{1}+\dots+x_{r}a_{r}.

Given
Q
with a representation as above

•

the numbers
a_{i}
are
generators
of
Q
, the numbers
M_{i}
and
M_{i}^{\prime}
are
dimensions
of
Q
, and
{\operatorname{Vol}}(Q):=|B|
is the
volume
of
Q
associated to this presentation (i.e. this choice of
a_{i},M_{i},M_{i}^{\prime}
);

•

we say that
Q
is
proper
for this presentation if the above linear map is one to one, or equivalently if
|Q|=|B|
;

•

If
-M_{i}=M_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i\geq 1
and
a_{0}=0
, we say that
Q
is
symmetric
for this presentation.

We note again that unlike in other applications of additive structures in random matrix theory that researchers used structures to enumerate vectors satisfying certain properties, here we use the above structure as it is to pass to all large primes; so the structure itself is important.

The following inverse-type result established by the current authors from
[
NW22
]
(which was in turn motivated by the inverse-type idea from
[
TV09
]
) will allow us to prove bounds sharper than Theorem
5.11
.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
\varepsilon<1
and
C
be positive constants. Assume that
p\geq C^{\prime}N^{C}
is a prime where
C^{\prime}
is sufficiently large. Let
\xi
be a random variable taking values in
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
which is
\alpha
-balanced.
Assume
{\mathbf{w}}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})\in({\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}})^{N}
such that

\rho_{l}({\mathbf{w}}):=\sup_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbf{P}}(\xi_{1}w_{1}+\dots+\xi_{N}w_{N}=a)\geq N^{-C},

where
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N}
are i.i.d. copies of
\xi
. Then for any
N^{\varepsilon/2}\alpha^{-1}\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
there exists a proper symmetric GAP
Q
of rank
r=O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)
which contains all but
N^{\prime}
elements of
w
(counting multiplicity), where

|Q|\leq\max\left\{1,O_{C,\varepsilon}(\rho_{l}^{-1}/(\alpha N^{\prime})^{r/2})\right\}.

We remark that
\rho_{l}(.)
(where the subscript stands for linear) is slightly different from
\rho(.)
defined in (
5.8
). We removed
1/p
in
\rho_{l}(.)
as it has little effect when
p
is large. Note that this result continues to hold for
\alpha
could be as small as
n^{-1+o(1)}
, but we just assume
\alpha
to be a constant here as usual. We also refer the reader to
[
KNP21
]
for various versions in general Abelian groups where GAP is replaced by coset-progressions.

We now introduce an inverse result for bilinear forms, which is another contribution of the current paper.

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
C>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. There exists a constant
A
such that the following holds for any prime
p\geq N^{A}
. Assume that
B=(b_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
is an array of elements of
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
so that

\rho_{b}:=\sup_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbf{P}}_{X,Y}(\sum_{i,j\leq n}b_{ij}x_{i}y_{j}=a)\geq N^{-C},

where
X=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}),Y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{N})
, and
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
are i.i.d. copies of
\xi
as in Theorem
8.2
.
Then, there exist an integer
k\neq 0,|k|=N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
, a set of
r=O(1)
rows
{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{1}},\dots,{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{r}}
of
B=(b_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
, and set
I
of size at least
N-N^{\varepsilon}
such that for each
i\in I
, there exist integers
k_{ii_{1}},\dots,k_{ii_{r}}
, all bounded by
N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
, with the following property

(8.4)

{\mathbf{P}}_{Z}\Big(Z\cdot(k{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B)+\sum_{j=1}^{r}k_{ii_{j}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{j}}(B))=0\ \operatorname{mod}\ p\Big)\geq N^{-O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)},

where
Z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})
and
z_{i}
are i.i.d. copies of
\xi
.

In connection to our previous sections, the parameter
N
will be chosen to satisfy
cn\leq N\leq n
throughout this section. In our later application we just need to consider
p\geq\exp(n^{c})
, so the above assumption on the range of
p
is natural. On the other hand, it is of independent interest to extend the results of Theorem
8.3
(and Theorem
8.14
below) to small
p
, we hope to be able to address this issue elsewhere.

So in a way our result says that the rows of
B
have low rank modulo some GAP noise. (Indeed it follows from (
8.4
) and from Theorem
8.2
that for each
i\in I
, most of the entries of
k{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B)+\sum_{j}k_{ii_{j}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{j}}(B)
belong to a symmetric GAP
P_{i}
of rank
O(1)
and size
N^{O(1)}
. One can in fact unify these structures into one but we will not do it here.) We will also introduce a quadratic version later (Theorem
8.14
) and prove it in the appendix. For the rest of this section we give a proof of Theorem
8.3
, adapting the method of
[
Ngu12
]
toward
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
.

We first rely on the following simple fact about generalized arithmetic progressions of small rank and large characteristic.

Fact 8.5
.

Let
C>
be given. Assume that
q_{1},\dots,q_{r+1}
are elements of a GAP of rank
r
and of cardinality
N^{C}
in
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
p\geq N^{A}
for sufficiently large
A
depending on
r
and
C
, then there exist integer coefficients
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}
with
|\alpha_{i}|\leq N^{rC}
, not all zero, such that in
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}

\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}\alpha_{i}q_{i}=0.

To prove Theorem
8.3
, we begin by applying Theorem
8.2
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
\varepsilon<1
and
C
be positive constants. With the assumption as in Theorem
8.2
, assume that
\rho_{b}\geq N^{-C}
. Then the following holds with probability at least
3\rho_{b}/4
with respect to
Y
. There exist a proper symmetric GAP
Q_{Y}
in
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
of rank
O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)
and size
O_{C,\varepsilon}(1/\rho_{b})
and a set
I_{Y}
of
N-N^{\varepsilon}
indices such that for each
i\in I_{Y}
we have
\sum_{j}b_{ij}y_{j}\in Q_{Y}
.

Proof.

For short we write
\sum_{i,j}b_{ij}x_{i}y_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}B_{i}(Y)
, where

B_{i}(Y):=\sum_{j}b_{ij}y_{j}.

We call a vector
Y

good
if
{\mathbf{P}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}B_{i}(Y)=a)\geq\rho_{b}/4
. We call
Y

bad
otherwise. Let
G
be the collection of good vectors. By averaging, one can show that the probability of random vector
Y
being good is at least, say
3\rho_{b}/4
.

Next, we consider good vectors
Y\in G
. By definition,
{\mathbf{P}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}B_{i}(Y)=a)\geq\rho_{b}/4
. A direct application of Theorem
8.2
to the sequence
B_{i}(Y)
,
i=1,\dots,N
yields the desired result.
∎

By a useful property of GAP containment (see for instance
[
TV09
, Section 8]
and
[
Ngu12
, Theorem 6.1]
), we may assume that the
q_{i}(Y)
span
Q_{Y}
in
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
. From now on we fix such a
Q_{Y}
for each
Y
. Recall that
G
is the collection of good vectors and we have

(8.7)

{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(Y\in G)\geq 3\rho_{b}/4.

Now we state a main lemma for the proof of Theorem
8.3
.

Lemma 8.8
.

There exits an index set
I
of size at least
N-2N^{\varepsilon}
, an index set
I_{0}
of size
O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)
, and an integer
k\neq 0
with
|k|\leq N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
such that for any index
i
from
I
, there are numbers
k_{ii_{0}}\in{\mathbb{Z}},i_{0}\in I_{0}
, all bounded by
n^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
, such that

{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}\Big(kB_{i}(Y)+\sum_{i_{0}\in I_{0}}k_{ii_{0}}B_{i_{0}}(Y)=0\ \operatorname{mod}\ p\Big)=\rho_{b}/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}.

Assume this result for a moment.

Proof of Theorem
8.3
.

From Lemma
8.8
, for any fixed
i\in I
, we note that

kB_{i}(Y)+\sum_{i_{0}\in I_{0}}k_{ii_{0}}B_{i_{0}}(Y)=\sum_{j}(kb_{ij}+\sum_{i_{0}}k_{ii_{0}}b_{i_{0}j})y_{j}.

By the conclusion of Lemma
8.8
, we have

\sup_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(\sum_{j}(kb_{ij}+\sum_{i_{0}\in I_{0}}k_{ii_{0}}b_{i_{0}j})y_{j}=a\ \operatorname{mod}\ p)\geq\rho_{b}/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}.

∎

We now give a proof for Lemma
8.8
.

Proof of Lemma
8.8
.

For each
Y\in G
, we choose from
I_{Y}

s
indices
i_{(1,Y)},\dots,i_{{(s,Y)}}
such that
q_{i_{{(j,Y)}}}(Y),1\leq j\leq s
, span
Q_{Y}
in
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
s
is the rank of
Q_{Y}
. We note that
s=O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)
for all
Y\in G
.

Consider the tuples
(i_{{(1,Y}},\dots,i_{{(s,Y}})
for all
Y\in G
. Because there are
\sum_{s}O_{C,\varepsilon}(N^{s})=N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
possibilities these tuples can take, by the pigeon-hole principle there exists a tuple, say
(1,\dots,r)
(by rearranging the rows of
B=(b_{ij})
if needed), such that
(i_{{(1,Y}},\dots,i_{{(s,Y}})=(1,\dots,r)
for all
Y\in G^{\prime}
, where
G^{\prime}
is a subset of
G
satisfying

(8.9)

{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(Y\in G^{\prime})\geq{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(Y\in G)/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}=\rho_{b}/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}.

For each
1\leq i\leq r
, we express
q_{i}(Y)
in terms of the generators of
Q_{Y}
for each
Y\in G^{\prime}
,

q_{i}(Y)=c_{i1}(Y)g_{1}(Y)+\dots+c_{ir}(Y)g_{r}(Y)\ \operatorname{mod}\ p,

where
c_{i1}(Y),\dots c_{ir}(Y)
are integers bounded by
n^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
, and
g_{i}(Y)
are the generators of
Q_{Y}
. Furthermore, as the
q_{i}(Y)
span
Q_{Y}
, the vectors
(c_{11},\dots,c_{1r}),\dots,(c_{r1},\dots c_{rr})
span
{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(Q_{Y})}
.

We show that there are many
Y
that correspond to the same coefficients
c_{i_{1}i_{2}}
.

Claim 8.10
.

There exists a (“dense”) subset
G^{\prime\prime}\subset G^{\prime}
such that the following holds

•

{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(Y\in G^{\prime\prime})\geq{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(Y\in G^{\prime})/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}\geq\rho_{b}/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)};

•

there exist
r
tuples
(c_{11},\dots,c_{1r}),\dots,(c_{r1},\dots c_{rr})
, whose components are integers bounded by
n^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
and
(c_{11},\dots,c_{1r}),\dots,(c_{r1},\dots c_{rr})
span
{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(Q_{Y})}
such that for all
Y\in G^{\prime\prime}

q_{i}(Y)=c_{i1}g_{1}(Y)+\dots+c_{ir}g_{r}(Y)\mbox{ for $i=1,\dots,r$.}

Proof of Claim
8.10
.

Consider the collection
\mathcal{C}
of the coefficient-tuples

\mathcal{C}:=\Big\{\Big(\big(c_{11}(Y),\dots,c_{1r}(Y)\big);\dots;\big(c_{r1}(Y),\dots c_{rr}(Y)\big)\Big),Y\in G^{\prime}\Big\}.

Because the number of possibilities these tuples can take is at most
(N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)})^{r^{2}}=N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
, again by the pigeon-hole principle there exists a coefficient-tuple, say
\Big((c_{11},\dots,c_{1r}),\dots,(c_{r1},\dots c_{rr})\Big)\in\mathcal{C}
, such that

\displaystyle\quad\Big(\big(c_{11}(Y),\dots,c_{1r}(Y)\big);\dots;\big(c_{r1}(Y),\dots c_{rr}(Y)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big((c_{11},\dots,c_{1r}),\dots,(c_{r1},\dots c_{rr})\Big)

for all
Y
from a subset
G^{\prime\prime}
of
G^{\prime}
which satisfies

(8.11)

{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(Y\in G^{\prime\prime})\geq{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(Y\in G^{\prime})/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}\geq\rho_{b}/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}.

∎

Now we focus on the elements of
G^{\prime\prime}
. Because
|I_{Y}|\geq N-N^{\varepsilon}
for each
Y\in G^{\prime\prime}
, by an averaging argument we can obtain the following.

Claim 8.12
.

There is a set
I
of size
N-3N^{\varepsilon}
such that
I\cap\{1,\dots,r\}=\emptyset
and for each
i\in I
we have

(8.13)

{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(i\in I_{Y},Y\in G^{\prime\prime})\geq{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(Y\in G^{\prime\prime})/2.

Now we conclude the proof of Lemma
8.8
. Fix an arbitrary index
i
from
I
. We concentrate on those
Y\in G^{\prime\prime}
where the index
i
belongs to
I_{Y}
. Because
q_{i}(Y)\in Q_{Y}
, we can write

q_{i}(Y)=c_{1}(Y)g_{1}(Y)+\dots c_{r}(Y)g_{r}(Y)\ \operatorname{mod}\ p,

where
c_{1}(Y),\dots,c_{r}(Y)
are integers bounded by
N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
.

For short, we denote by
v_{i,Y}
the vector
(c_{1}(Y),\dots c_{r}(Y))
, we also use the shorthand
v_{j}
for the vectors
(c_{j1},\dots,c_{jr})
obtained from Claim
8.10
.

Because
Q_{Y}
is spanned by
q_{1}(Y),\dots,q_{r}(Y)
, we must have
k:=\det({v}_{1},\dots{v}_{r})\neq 0\ \operatorname{mod}\ p
(and hence
k\neq 0
because
p\geq n^{A}
with sufficiently large
A
) and that in
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle kq_{i}(Y)+\det({v}_{i,Y},{v}_{2},\dots,{v}_{r})q_{1}(Y)+\dots

\displaystyle+\det({v}_{i,Y},{v}_{1},\dots,{v}_{r-1})q_{r}(Y)=0.

Furthermore, because each coefficient of the identity above is bounded by
n^{O_{C,\varepsilon,\mu}(1)}
, there exists a subset
G_{i}^{\prime\prime}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
such that all
Y\in G_{i}^{\prime\prime}
correspond to the same identity, and

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(Y\in G_{i}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\geq({\mathbf{P}}_{Y}(Y\in G^{\prime\prime})/2)/(N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)})^{r}

\displaystyle\geq\rho_{b}/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}.

In other words, there exist integers
k_{1},\dots,k_{r}
, all bounded by
N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
, such that

kq_{i}(Y)+k_{1}q_{1}(Y)+\dots+k_{r}q_{r}(Y)=0

for all
Y\in G_{i}^{\prime\prime}
.

Note that
k
is independent of the choice of
i
and
Y
. Finally recall that
q_{i}=\sum_{j}b_{ij}y_{j}=B_{i}(Y)
, we thus complete the proof of Lemma
8.8
.
∎

After proving our inverse result for bilinear forms, by using a decoupling method (similarly to Lemma
7.4
) we can also obtain the following analog of Theorem
8.3
for quadratic forms, which is another highlight of the section.

Theorem 8.14
.

Let
C>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists a constant
A
such that the following holds for any prime
p\geq n^{A}
. Assume that
B=(b_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
is a symmetric array of elements of
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
so that

\rho_{q}:=\sup_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbf{P}}_{X}(\sum_{i,j\leq n}b_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}=a)\geq N^{-C},

where
X=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, and
x_{i}
are i.i.d. copies of
\xi
.
Then, there exist an integer
k\neq 0,|k|=N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
, a set of
r=O(1)
rows
{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{1}},\dots,{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{r}}
of
B
, and set
I
of size at least
N-2N^{\varepsilon}
such that for each
i\in I
, there exist integers
k_{ii_{1}},\dots,k_{ii_{r}}
, all bounded by
N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
, such that the following holds.

(8.15)

{\mathbf{P}}_{Z}\Big(Z\cdot(k{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B)+\sum_{j=1}^{r}k_{ii_{j}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{j}}(B))=0\ \operatorname{mod}\ p\Big)\geq N^{-O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)},

where
Z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})
and
z_{i}
are i.i.d. copies of
(u-u^{\prime})(\xi-\xi^{\prime})
, where
u,u^{\prime},\xi,\xi
are independent and
u,u^{\prime}
are Bernoulli random variables of parameter
1/2
.

A proof of this result is given in Appendix
A
.

To conclude the section, when working with Laplacian matrices our random vectors will be adapted along an index set
I_{0}
as in Definition
7.3
. However the above result automatically applies if we restrict to the coefficients indexed from
I_{0}
. In other words we have

Corollary 8.16
.

Let
C>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists a constant
A
such that the following holds for any prime
p\geq n^{A}
. Assume that
X
is adapted to
I_{0}
as in Definiotion
7.3
where
|I_{0}|\geq\varepsilon N
and

\sup_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbf{P}}_{X}(\sum_{i,j\in I_{0}}b_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}=a)\geq N^{-C}.

Then, there exist an integer
k\neq 0,|k|=N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
, a set of
r=O(1)
rows
{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{1}},\dots,{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{r}}
of
B
, and set
I\subset I_{0}
of size at least
|I_{0}|-2N^{\varepsilon}
such that for each
i\in I
, there exist integers
k_{ii_{1}},\dots,k_{ii_{r}}
, all bounded by
N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
, such that the following holds.

(8.17)

{\mathbf{P}}_{Z}\Big(Z_{I_{0}}\cdot(k{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B)+\sum_{j=1}^{r}k_{ii_{j}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{j}}(B))_{I_{0}}=0\ \operatorname{mod}\ p\Big)\geq N^{-O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)},

where
Z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})
and
z_{i}
are as in Theorem
8.14
.

Theorem
8.2
will be exploited many times in our next section. Also, Corollary
8.16
will play a key role in the proof of Lemma
9.4
.

9.
Treatment for large primes: proof of Propositions
2.5
and
2.7

For a sufficiently positive small constant
c
(to be fixed throughout the section) we let

{\mathfrak{P}}_{n}:=\Big\{p\textrm{ prime},e^{n^{c}}\leq p\leq(C_{\xi}\sqrt{n})^{n}\Big\}.

9.1.
Outline for the symmetric and Laplacian models

As usual,
G_{N}
stands for the principle minor
M_{N}
for symmetric matrices, or the principle minor
L_{N}^{\ast}
(after the neighbor reshuffling process) for laplacian matrices.

Let us start from
G_{N_{0}}
, where

N_{0}=\lfloor cn\rfloor.

Consider the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{N,non-sing}
that the matrix
G_{N}
is non-singular in
{\mathbb{R}}
(or
{\mathbb{Z}}
); we are going to work on the intersection of all these events.

Definition 9.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{non-sing}
be the intersection of all
{\mathcal{E}}_{N,non-sing}
, where
N_{0}\leq N\leq n
. Note that this event is independent of
p
. By Corollary
7.18
,

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{non-sing})=1-\exp(-\Theta(n^{c})).

Let
{\mathfrak{W}}_{N_{0}}
be the list of primes
p\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{n}
such that
p|\det(G_{N_{0}})
, this is the list of bad primes. Notice that as by Hadamard’s bound,
|\det(G_{N})|\leq(C_{\xi}N)^{N/2}
, and so, counting multiplicities we have

|{\mathfrak{W}}_{N_{0}}|\leq\frac{(N_{0}/2)\log(C_{\xi}N)}{n^{c}}\leq n.

Notice that for
p\in{\mathfrak{W}}_{N_{0}}
and
p\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{n}
, the
{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N_{0}}/p)
could be as small as
N_{0}-\frac{(N_{0}/2)\log(C_{\xi}N)}{n^{c}}\geq N_{0}-n^{1-c/2}
, but we will show that throughout the column exposure process below, this rank will be improved fast and achieves the value at least
n-1
in the last step.

For
N_{0}\leq N\leq n-1
, we consider the process of adding the
N+1
-th column and
N+1
-th row to form
G_{N+1}
, note that for the Laplacian case, this is Phase 2, obtained via the neighbors reshuffling.

After each step, let
{\mathfrak{W}}_{N+1}
be the collection of primes
p\in{\mathfrak{W}}_{N}
that
G_{N+1}/p
does not have full rank and the new primes
p
that did not belong to
{\mathfrak{W}}_{N}
but
{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N+1}/p)\leq N
. Notice that for these new primes, as
p\notin{\mathfrak{W}}_{N}
, we have
{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N}/p)=N
, and so
{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N+1}/p)
must be
N
in this case. As of now, we will want to make sure that for the newly arising primes the rank are not very small, especially in the very last steps, because otherwise adding the last few rows and columns will not increase the ranks to at least
n-1
as desired. Another remark here is that, as
0<|\det(G_{N+1})|\leq(C_{\xi}N)^{N/2}
, the number of newly arising primes is bounded by
(N/2)\log(C_{\xi}N)/n^{c}\leq n
. So we trivially have

|{\mathfrak{W}}_{N+1}|\leq|{\mathfrak{W}}_{N}|+n\leq(N+1)n.

Our key result of this section is the following proposition.

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
C>0
be a given large constant. There is an event
{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{E}}_{N}
in characteristic zero with probability at least
1-n^{-C}
such that under this event, for any
p\in{\mathfrak{W}}_{N}
, and for all
N_{0}\leq N\leq n-1
we have

{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N+1}/p)=\min\{{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N}/p)+2,N+1\}.

It is clear that this proposition would then imply Proposition
2.5
because with probability at least
1-O(n^{-C+3})
(on the intersection of
\cap_{N_{0}\leq N\leq n}{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
), for all
p\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{n}
we have
{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{n-1}/p)\geq n-1
as
{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{n}/p)
becomes full after exposing the last
n
-th row and column for all
p\in{\mathfrak{W}}_{n-1}
, and for newly arising primes
p
we have
{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{n}/p)={\operatorname{rank}}(G_{n-1}/p)=n-1
as
p\notin{\mathfrak{W}}_{n-1}
.

Before moving to discuss the technical details, we pause to compare the current method with that of
[
NW22
, Section 6]
for the i.i.d. model. Both methods rely on the “watch list” argument. However, unlike in the i.i.d. case, here at the starting point
N=N_{0}
some prime
p
in the list
{\mathfrak{W}}_{N}
might have very high multiplicities. Fortunately, each exposure step usually improves the rank by 2 (rather than by 1 as in the i.i.d. case) thanks to Proposition
9.2
. So the rank over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
will become almost full very fast. When the rank becomes full we remove
p
from the watch list
{\mathfrak{W}}_{N}
, but this
p
might reappear later in the process, the difference now is that the corank over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
will be at most one as we have seen above.

In the remaining part we prove Proposition
9.2
, which is an innovative part of the treatment. We will mainly focus on the Laplacian model (and hence
G_{N}=L_{N}^{\ast}
) as the symmetric case will follow almost automatically. Our proof consists of three steps outlined below

•

Step 1. If

{\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N+1}/p)=\min\{{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N}/p)+2,N+1\})\leq 1-n^{-C},

where the randomness is on the
N+1
-th column and
N+1
-th row (of the reshuffling process), then by Theorem
8.14
and Corollary
8.16
there is a “local”
O(1)
-normal vector of
G_{N}
which has
partially rich
structure in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
. Here
C
is large enough to compensate with the loss of the watching list argument after taking union bound over all primes from
{\mathfrak{W}}_{N},N\leq n-1
. We refer the reader to Subsection
9.2
for precise statements.

•

Step 2. We then use Definition
1.28
to pass to the symmetric model, showing that the event that a local normal vector is
partially structured
but not
fully structured
has probability
n^{-An}
with large
A
, for which we can take union bound over all
p\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{n}
. This step is carried out in Subsection
9.3
.

•

Step 3. It remains to estimate the event that there is a fully structured normal vector for each
p
in the watch list. We then use the low rank and rich structure to show that this event for
p\geq e^{n^{c}}
can be passed to characteristic zero, and henceforth to
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
for some
e^{n^{c/2}}\leq p\leq e^{n^{c}}
, while for the latter setting we have shown in the previous section that this hold with probability
\exp(-\Theta(n^{c}))
. We will complete this final step in Subsection
9.4
.

From now on, if not specified otherwise,
p
is a prime from
{\mathfrak{P}}_{n}
.

9.2.
Step 1: normal vectors with adapted structures.

In what follows, for the Laplacian case we recall that the randomness is from Phase 2 of Definition
1.23
. That is
I_{0}=I_{N+1}
is the set of indices from
v_{1},\dots,v_{N}
that is connected to exactly one vertex from the pair
\{v_{N+1},v_{N+2}\}
. Recall (
1.27
), by Chernoff’s bound with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Theta(n^{1-c}))
, for all
N_{0}\leq N\leq n-1
we have

(9.3)

|I_{0}|\in[N/2-N^{1-c/2},N/2+N^{1-c/2}].

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1,C>0
be given constants. Under
{\mathcal{E}}_{non-sing}
, assume that
N\geq N_{0}
, and

{\mathbf{P}}\Big({\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N+1}/p)=\min\{{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N}/p)+2,N+1\}\Big)\leq 1-n^{-C},

where the randomness is on the random reshuffling process with respect to
v_{N+1}
and
v_{N+2}
. Then there exist a constant
C_{\ast}
depending on
C,c,\varepsilon
and there exist a non-zero vector
{\mathbf{v}}
and a subset
J_{0}\subset I_{0}
such that

•

|J_{0}|\geq|I_{0}|-2N^{1-c/2}
;

•

all of the entries of
{\mathbf{v}}_{J_{0}}
belong to a GAP of size at most
n^{C_{\ast}}
and rank at most
C_{\ast}
,

•

{\mathbf{v}}
is orthogonal to all but at most
C_{\ast}
rows of
G_{N}
.

Proof of Lemma
9.4
.

As we are working with primes
p>\exp(n^{c})
, if we are on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{non-sing}
(in particularly
\det(G_{N})\neq 0
) then as
|\det(G_{N})|\leq(C_{\xi}N)^{N/2}
, we have that

k={\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N}/p)\geq N-N^{1-c/2}.

Case 1.
Assume that
k\leq N-1
. Let
G_{J\times J}
be a submatrix in
G_{N}
of full rank,
|J|=k
, where
J=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}
. We add one more column corresponding to the vertex
v_{N+1}
and consider the matrix
G_{J\times(J\cup\{v_{N+1}\})}
. Let
H
be the subspace generated by the rows of this matrix, and let
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}=(v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{k+1}})
be a normal vector of
H
. When we expose the
N+1
-th vector
X_{N+1}
, as we have seen in Section
7
, if
(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{k}},x_{N+1})\notin H
then
{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N+1}/p)={\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N}/p)+2
. So we must have
(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{k}},x_{N+1})\in H
, and hence

(9.5)

{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\cdot(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{k}},x_{N+1})=0.

We will restrict the above event to the randomness over
x_{i_{j}}
where
i_{j}\in I_{0}\cap J
. Recall that as
|J|\geq N-N^{1-c/2}
and
I_{0}\subset[N]
satisfying (
9.3
), we have

|I_{0}\cap J|\geq N/2-2N^{1-c/2}.

By Theorem
8.2
, the event of (
9.5
) has probability smaller than
N^{-C}
except all but
N^{\prime}=N^{1-c/2}
of the entries of the
v_{i_{j}},i_{j}\in I_{0}\cap J
belong to a GAP structure of size
O(\rho_{l}({\mathbf{v}}^{\prime})^{-1}/\sqrt{N^{1-c/2}})
. To complete the proof, by definition
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}
is orthogonal to all columns of
G_{J\times[N]}
(because
G_{J\times J}
has the same rank as
G_{N}
), and hence the vector
{\mathbf{v}}
obtained from
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}
by appending
N-k-1
components of value zero is orthogonal to all columns of
G_{N}
.

Case 2.
Assume that
k=N
. In this case
G_{J\times J}=G_{N}
. Let
B=(b_{ij})
be the inverse matrix
G_{N}^{-1}
. As we have seen in (
7.9
), by adding a new row and column (associated to
v_{N+1}
) we see that the rank does increase unless
\sum_{i,j\in I_{0}}b_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}=0
. Assume that this holds with probability at least
n^{-C}
, then we can apply Corollary
8.16
. For each
i\in I
obtained by this corollary (where we recall that
I
is of subset size at least
|I_{0}|-2n^{\varepsilon}
of
I_{0}
) let

{\mathbf{r}}_{i}^{\prime}=k{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B)+\sum_{j=1}^{r}k_{ii_{j}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{j}}(B).

Because
B
has rank
m
, the rows
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B)
are linearly independent, and so
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}^{\prime}
is non-zero for
i\notin\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}
. We fix one such non-zero vector
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{r}}_{i_{0}}^{\prime}
, and in this case set
{\mathbf{v}}:={\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}
. By definition
{\mathbf{v}}
is orthogonal to all but only
r+1
columns of indices from
{i_{0},i_{1},\dots,i_{r}}
. Furthermore by (
8.4
) we have that

{\mathbf{P}}_{Z}\Big(Z\cdot{\mathbf{v}}=0\ \operatorname{mod}\ p\Big)\geq n^{-O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)},

where
Z
is adapted to
I_{0}
. As a direct application of Theorem
8.2
, we then infer that all but
n^{\varepsilon}
of the entries of
{\mathbf{v}}_{I_{0}}
belongs to a GAP structure of size
O(n^{O(1)})
and rank
O(1)
as claimed.
∎

We note from the proof above that
{\mathbf{v}}
is defined via
B
, and hence
G_{J\times J}
, a submatrix of full rank in
G_{N}
. Hence it is natural to call
{\mathbf{v}}
a
local
(w.r.t.
J
) almost normal vector of
G_{N}
. In what follows, for given
C
and
c,\varepsilon
, the parameter
C_{\ast}=C_{\ast}(C,c,\varepsilon)
is always chosen as in the conclusion of Lemma
9.4
. Motivated by this result, it is natural to define the following notion of partially structured vectors.

Definition 9.6
.

Let
J_{0}\subset[N]
be given. Let
C_{\ast},C_{\ast}^{\prime},\varepsilon>0
be constants (where
C_{\ast}=C_{\ast}(C,c,\varepsilon)
).

•

(partially structured) We say
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
is a
J_{0}
-structured almost normal vector with respect to
G_{N}
(and with respect to the parameter
C_{\ast}
) if all components of
{\mathbf{v}}_{J_{0}}
come from a GAP (over
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
) with size at most
n^{C_{\ast}}
and rank at most
C_{\ast}
and
{\mathbf{v}}
is orthogonal to all but at most
C_{\ast}
columns of
G_{N}
.

•

(fully structured) We say that
{\mathbf{v}}
is a fully structured almost normal vector (with respect to the parameters
C_{\ast}^{\prime},\varepsilon
) if all but
n^{\varepsilon}
components of
{\mathbf{v}}
come from a GAP (over
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
) with size at most
n^{C_{\ast}^{\prime}}
and rank at most
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{v}}
is orthogonal to all but
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
columns of
G_{N}
.

By Lemma
9.4
, if
G_{N}
has an almost normal vector that is not locally structured then we would be done with the proof of Proposition
9.2
. Hence we need to work with the event that
G_{N}
has a non-trivial almost normal vector that is
J_{0}
-structured for some
J_{0}
. On the other hand, for the step of passing to all primes in
{\mathfrak{P}}_{n}
at once it is desirable to have fully structured vectors instead, which motivated us to introduce Definition
9.6
and add another twist into the plan to treat with locally but not fully structured below. We notice that this problem only occurs in the Laplacian case; for the random symmetric model
I_{0}
is already the whole set
[N]
and in this case we can completely skip these extra treatments.

9.3.
Step 2: partially but not fully structured almost normal vectors of Laplacian matrices

Note that in the Laplacian model, after Phase 1 of Definition
1.28
the degree sequence
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n+1})
is fixed, and also by (
1.25
) with probability
1-\exp(-\Theta(n))

(9.7)

d_{1},\dots,d_{n+1}\in[(1/2-\varepsilon)n,(1/2+\varepsilon)n].

For convenience we define

Definition 9.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{mixing}
be the intersection of the events from (
9.7
) and from (
9.3
) and for all
N_{0}\leq N\leq n-1
. Note that this event is on the graph and is independent of
p
and

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{mixing})\geq 1-\exp(-\Theta(n^{1-c})).

We will show the following

Lemma 9.9

(structure propagation in perturbed symmetric matrices)
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
and
A,C>0
be given constants. Then there exists a constant
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
such that the following holds for all
p\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{n}
, and on
{\mathcal{E}}_{mixing}
. The probability of the intersection of the events that all principle minors
G_{N},N_{0}\leq N\leq n
have non-zero determinant and that there exist
N
in the above range and a local almost normal vector of
G_{N}/p
that is
J_{0}
-structured (with respect to
C_{\ast}
as in Lemma
9.4
for given
C,\varepsilon,c
) for some
J_{0}\subset[N]
of size at least
N/2-2N^{1-c/2}
but not fully structured with respect to
C_{\ast}^{\prime},\varepsilon
is bounded by
O(n^{-An})
, where the implied constants are allowed to depend on the given constants.

Note again that it would be more natural to have the probability bound of the form
O(N^{-AN})
for each
N
, but by changing
A
we can replace the bounds by
O(n^{-An})
for convenience. We will prove Lemma
9.9
by passing to random symmetric matrices of given diagonal entries, via Definition
1.28
of the Laplacians. We can do this thanks to the key lemma below which roughly says that the rare event is very rare.

Lemma 9.10

(partial structure in perturbed random symmetric matrices)
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
and
A,C>0
be given constants. Then there exists a constant
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
such that the following holds. Let
p\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{n}
. Let
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n+1})
be an degree sequence so that

{\mathbf{P}}\big(G(n+1,1/2)\in G_{{\mathbf{d}}}\big)\geq n^{-4n}.

Assume that
M_{n}
is the random symmetric matrix as in Theorem
1.3
. Then on the intersection of the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{non-sing}
in
{\mathbb{Z}}
that all principle minors
M_{N}-D_{N},N_{0}\leq N\leq n
have non-zero determinant (where
D_{N}={\operatorname{diag}}(d_{1},\dots,d_{N})
is the diagonal matrix with entries
d_{i}
), the event that there exists
N
in the above range and a local almost normal vector
{\mathbf{v}}
of
(M_{N}-D_{N})/p
that is
J_{0}
-structured (with respect to
C_{\ast}
) for some
J_{0}\subset[N]
satisfying
|J_{0}|\geq N/2-2N^{1-c/2}
but
{\mathbf{v}}
is not fully structured with respect to
C_{\ast}^{\prime},\varepsilon
has probability bounded by
O(n^{-An})
.

We then deduce the following version for random matrices of given degree sequence.

Corollary 9.11
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
and
C>0
be given. There there exists a constant
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
such that the following holds. Let
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n+1})
be an degree sequence with
(1/2-c)n\leq d_{i}\leq(1/2+c)n
so that

{\mathbf{P}}(G(n+1,1/2)\in G_{{\mathbf{d}}})\geq n^{-4n}.

Let
G_{\mathbf{d}}
be a random graph in
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{d}}}
defined in Phase 2 of Definition
1.28
,

{\mathbf{P}}(G_{{\mathbf{d}}}=G):=\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(G(n+1,1/2)=G)}{{\mathbf{P}}(G(n+1,1/2)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{d})},\mbox{ for each $G$ of degree sequence ${\mathbf{d}}$}.

Then the conclusion of Lemma
9.10
holds for the matrix
L_{n,{\mathbf{d}}}
obtained from
M_{G_{\mathbf{d}}}-{\operatorname{diag}}({\mathbf{d}})
by removing the last row and column.

Proof of Corollary
9.11
.

Let
M_{n+1}
be the adjacency matrix of
G(n+1,1/2)
and let
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbf{d}}}
be the event that
G(n+1,1/2)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{d}}
(that is
\{M_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbf{d}}\}=\{G(n+1,1/2)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{d}}\}
). Then by definition
{\mathbf{P}}(M_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbf{d}})\geq n^{-4n}
and for each
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{d}}

{\mathbf{P}}(M_{G_{\mathbf{d}}}=M_{G})=\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(M_{n+1}=M_{G})}{{\mathbf{P}}(M_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbf{d}})}.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be the set of square matrices (of size
n
) where every
J_{0}
-structured normal vectors is fully structured, and let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the non-singularity event
{\mathcal{E}}_{non-sing}
considered in Lemma
9.10
. We have (where
L_{n}(M_{n+1})
is the principle minor of
M_{n+1}-{\operatorname{diag}}({\mathbf{d}})
)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(L_{n,{\mathbf{d}}}\in{\mathcal{E}}\cap\bar{{\mathcal{F}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(L_{n}(M_{n+1})\in{\mathcal{E}}\cap\bar{{\mathcal{F}}}\wedge M_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbf{d}})}{{\mathbf{P}}(M_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbf{d}})}\leq\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(L_{n}(M_{n+1})\in{\mathcal{E}}\cap\bar{{\mathcal{F}}}\wedge M_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbf{d}})}{n^{-4n}}

\displaystyle=O(n^{-(A-4)n}),

where in the last estimate we applied Lemma
9.10
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
9.9
.

On
{\mathcal{E}}_{mixing}
we have (recalling that
G_{n}=L_{n}
)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(L_{n}\in{\mathcal{E}}\cap\bar{{\mathcal{F}}}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{mixing})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{\mathbf{d}};{\mathbf{P}}(G(n+1,1/2)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{d}}})\geq n^{-4n}}{\mathbf{P}}(M_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{E}}\cap\bar{{\mathcal{F}}}|M_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbf{d}}}){\mathbf{P}}(M_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbf{d}}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathbf{d}};{\mathbf{P}}(G(n+1,1/2)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{d}}})\geq n^{-4n}}{\mathbf{P}}(L_{n,{\mathbf{d}}}\in{\mathcal{E}}\cap\bar{{\mathcal{F}}}){\mathbf{P}}(M_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbf{d}}})

\displaystyle=O(n^{-(A-4)n}).

∎

What remains is to justify the symmetric matrix model.

Proof of Lemma
9.10
.

Our proof is somewhat similar to those of Proposition
5.19
and Lemma
6.5
. It suffices to consider for a fixed
N
in the range
N_{0}=\lfloor cn\rfloor\leq N\leq n
. Assume that there is a local almost orthogonal vector
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}
(from both cases of the proof of Lemma
9.4
, not including the
N-k
appended zero components) that is
J_{0}
-structured for some
J_{0}
but not fully structured. We will show that the probability of this event is as small as expected via two stages.
In what follows we will use the notation from the proof of Lemma
9.4
.

Stage 1.
Let
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}=({\mathbf{v}}_{0},{\mathbf{v}}_{1})
be the decomposition into the
J_{0}
-structured part
{\mathbf{v}}_{0}
and the remaining part
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
, where
J_{0}=\operatorname{supp_{index}}({\mathbf{v}}_{0})
.
We will fix
J_{0}
(there are crudely at most
2^{N}
such
J_{0}
), where
|J_{0}|\geq N/2-2N^{1-c/2}
on
{\mathcal{E}}_{mixing}
, and
|J_{1}|=|\operatorname{supp_{index}}({\mathbf{v}}_{1})|=|J\backslash J_{0}|+1\leq N/2+2N^{1-c/2}
(see the left picture from Figure
4
.)

We will argue that
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
can be determined via
{\mathbf{v}}_{0}
and some part of the matrix
G_{N}
. We will be focusing on Case 1 of the proof of Lemma
9.4
, the other case is similar. Using notation from that proof, we have
G_{J\times(J\cup\{N+1\})}{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}=0
, and so
M_{0}{\mathbf{v}}_{0}+M_{1}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}=0
where
M_{0}=G_{J\times J_{0}}
and
M_{1}=G_{J\times J_{1}}
. As the matrix
G_{J\times(J\cup\{N+1\})}
has full rank, the matrix
G_{J\times J_{1}}
has rank at least
|J_{1}|-1
. Without loss of generality we assume that
G_{J\times J_{1}}
has rank
|J_{1}|
, and hence it has a square submatrix
M_{1}^{\prime}
of size
|J_{1}|
which is non-singular.

Claim 9.12
.

With probability at least
1-O(\exp(-n^{1+\varepsilon}))
,
M_{1}^{\prime}
can take the form
M_{B\times J_{1}}
where

|B\cap J_{1}|\leq 5N^{1-c/2}.

Proof.

We first choose
B^{\prime}=J_{0}\cup B^{\prime\prime}
for any
B^{\prime\prime}\subset J_{1}
so that
|B^{\prime}|=J_{1}
. Then
|B^{\prime}\cap J_{1}|\leq 4N^{1-c/2}
. By the proof of Lemma
5.5
, we see that the matrix
M_{B\times J_{1}}
already has rank at least
|J_{1}|-n^{1/2+\varepsilon}
with probability at least
O(\exp(-n^{1+2\varepsilon}))
. Hence by interchanging with at most
n^{1/2+\varepsilon}
row vectors of
M_{(J\backslash B^{\prime})\times J_{1}}
, we can obtain
B
for which
M_{B\times J_{1}}
has full rank, and that
|B\cap J_{1}|\leq|B^{\prime}\cap J_{1}|+n^{1/2+\varepsilon}\leq 5N^{1-c/2}
.
∎

Using the matrix
M_{1}^{\prime}=M_{B\times J_{1}}
above, we will extract from
M_{J\times(J\cup\{v\})}{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}=0
the portion
M_{0}^{\prime}{\mathbf{v}}_{0}+M_{1}^{\prime}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}=0
, where
M_{0}^{\prime}=M_{B\times J_{0}}
. After fixing
M_{0}^{\prime}
and
M_{1}^{\prime}
we have

{\mathbf{v}}_{1}=(M_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}M_{0}{\mathbf{v}}_{0}.

In summary, by conditioning on
M_{0}^{\prime}
and
M_{1}^{\prime}
, and on a realization of
{\mathbf{v}}_{0}
, the vector
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
is determined. Note that by fixing
M_{0}^{\prime}
and
M_{1}^{\prime}
, we have fixed a submatrix of size
J_{1}\times[N]
in
G_{N}
.

Set
A_{0}:=C_{\ast}
. There are
N^{C_{\ast}N/2}p^{O(1)}=N^{A_{0}N/2}e^{O(n^{c})}
ways to choose the structured vector
{\mathbf{v}}_{0}
, and
O(2^{N}\times 2^{N})=O(4^{N})
ways to choose
J_{0}
and the row index for
M_{1}^{\prime}
. Thus in total we have a collection
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
of at most
N^{A_{0}N}
ways to choose the vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{0},{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
.

M_{0}^{\prime}
M_{1}^{\prime}
{\mathbf{v}}_{0}
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\prime}
{\mathbf{v}}_{0}
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\prime}
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
M_{0}^{\prime\prime}
M_{1}^{\prime\prime}
M_{0}^{\prime\prime}

Figure 4
.
Finding non-structured subvectors.

Next we show that for a fixed
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
in the way above, by exploiting the remaining randomness and the remaining equations from
M_{0}{\mathbf{v}}_{0}+M_{1}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}=0
, the most part of
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
must be structured with extremely high probability. To do this, let
(J_{1}\backslash B)^{+}
and
(J_{1}\backslash B)^{-}
be any two parts of
J_{1}\backslash B
of size almost equal. If the part
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
restricted to
J_{1}\backslash B
has the property that

(9.13)

\rho_{l}(({\mathbf{v}}_{1})_{(J_{1}\backslash B)^{+}})\leq n^{-K_{0}}\mbox{ or }\rho_{l}(({\mathbf{v}}_{1})_{(J_{1}\backslash B)^{-}})\leq n^{-K_{0}}

for some large constant
K_{0}
chosen depending on
A_{0}
(i.e. on
C_{\ast}
) and
A
. Without loss of generality assume that
\rho_{l}(({\mathbf{v}}_{1})_{(J_{1}\backslash B)^{+}})\leq n^{-K_{0}}
. Then by using the i.i.d. decomposition as in (
6.3
) and (
6.4
) we can bound the probability that
M_{(J\cup\{N+1\})\times J}{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}=0
by

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(\exists{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0},M_{J\times(J\cup\{v\})}{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}=0)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mathbf{P}}(\exists{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0},M_{(J\backslash B)\times(J_{1}\backslash B)}{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{a}})

\displaystyle\leq(1/n^{K_{0}})^{|(J_{1}\backslash B)^{-}|}N^{A_{0}N}4^{n}\leq 1/n^{NK_{0}/4}\leq 1/n^{An}.

Hence it remains to assume that
\rho_{l}(({\mathbf{v}}_{1})_{(J_{1}\backslash B)^{+}})\geq n^{-K_{0}}
and
\rho_{l}(({\mathbf{v}}_{1})_{(J_{1}\backslash B)^{-}})\geq n^{-K_{0}}
. Under these assumptions, however, Theorem
8.2
implies that
({\mathbf{v}}_{1})_{J_{1}\backslash B}
is structured in the sense that all but
n^{\varepsilon}
element of
({\mathbf{v}}_{1})_{J_{1}\backslash B}
belongs to a GAP of size bounded by
n^{K_{0}}
. Hence we have just shown that all but at most
n^{\varepsilon}
exceptional entries of the vector
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}|_{J_{0}\cup(J_{1}\backslash B)}
belong to a GAP of size
n^{O(1)}
and rank
O(1)
, where the implied constants depend on
\varepsilon,K_{0}
. So crudely the collection
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
of such structured vectors is now bounded by
n^{A_{1}n}
for some sufficiently constant depending on
A_{0},A,\varepsilon
.

Stage 2.
Because the contribution of
n^{\varepsilon}
exceptional entries can be easily absorbed into the total number of unstructured entries, let us focus only on the vector
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}|_{B\cap J_{1}}
, where we will show that with extremely high probability all but
n^{O(\varepsilon)}
entries of this vector are structured. We have learned that
N^{\prime}:=|B\cap J_{1}|\leq 4N^{1-c/2}
. If
N^{\prime}\leq n^{\varepsilon}
then we are done. Assume otherwise, we will then apply the following variant of Lemma
5.5
and Claim
9.12
above.

Claim 9.14
.

Assume that
I
is an index set with
|I|=N^{\prime}
where
n^{\varepsilon}\leq N^{\prime}\leq 4N^{1-c/2}
. Then with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Theta(N\times{N^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}))
the matrix
G_{([N]\backslash I)\times I}
has a square submatrix of size
|I|
of rank at least
|I|-{N^{\prime}}^{1/2+\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

We decompose the matrix
G_{([N]\backslash I)\times I}
into
k=\lfloor N/N^{\prime}\rfloor
disjoint square matrices of type
M_{B_{1}\times I},\dots,M_{B_{k}\times I}
where
B_{1},\dots,B_{k}
are arbitrary but disjoint, each has size
N^{\prime}
in
[N]\backslash I
. For each such matrix, the probability it has rank at most
|I|-{N^{\prime}}^{1/2+\varepsilon}
is bounded by
\exp(-\Theta({N^{\prime}}^{1+2\varepsilon}))
by the argument of Lemma
5.5
(with
N^{\prime}
in place of
n
). Because these matrices are independent, the probability that at least one of these matrices has rank at least
|I|-{N^{\prime}}^{1/2+\varepsilon}
is bounded below by

1-\exp(-\Theta(k{N^{\prime}}^{1+2\varepsilon}))\geq 1-\exp(-\Theta(N\times{N^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon})).

∎

In our next step we work with
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}|_{B\cap J_{1}}
as with
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
from Stage 1. By using the argument in Stage 1 (relying on
M_{0}{\mathbf{v}}_{0}+M_{1}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}=0
, but now with a fixed structured vector
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}|_{J_{0}\cup(J_{1}\backslash B)}
from
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
) to passing to a potential unstructured vector
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
(see the right-side illustration in Figure
4
) whose support is a subset of size at most
{N^{\prime}}^{1/2+\varepsilon}
in
J_{1}
, where
\rho_{l}({\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\prime\prime})\leq n^{-K_{2}}
for some
K_{2}
chosen sufficiently large compared to
A_{1},A,\varepsilon
.

One then iterate Claim
9.14
until we get a subvector supported on an index set of size
O(n^{\varepsilon})
. It is clear that this process terminates after
O(1)
steps because the support size of the potential unstructured vectors decreases from
N^{\prime}
to
{N^{\prime}}^{1/2+\varepsilon}
each time.

By gathering the structures together, we obtain a unified GAP (over
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
), which might have large size and rank, but still of order
O(n^{O(1)})
and
O(1)
respectively. Let
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
be the maximum of these constants (which depend on the choices of
K_{i}
, and hence of
A
and
\varepsilon
), we are done with the proof.
∎

To conclude this subsection, by combining Lemma
9.4
Lemma
9.9
we obtain the following

Lemma 9.15
.

Let
C,\varepsilon>0
be given, where
C
can be large and
\varepsilon
can be small. There exists a constant
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
, and there exists an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{partial-full}
of probability
1-n^{-An}
such that on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{mixing}\wedge{\mathcal{E}}_{non-sing}\wedge{\mathcal{E}}_{partial-full}
, assume that
N\geq cn
, and
{\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N+1}/p)=\min\{{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N}/p)+2,N+1\})\leq 1-n^{-C}
for some
p\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{n}
(where the randomness is on the
N+1
-th column and
N+1
-th row of the reshuffling process if in the Laplacian case.) Then there exists a non-zero vector
{\mathbf{v}}
such that all but
n^{\varepsilon}
of its entries belong to a GAP in
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
of size at most
n^{C_{\ast}^{\prime}}
and rank at most
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
such that
{\mathbf{v}}
is orthogonal to all but at mot
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
rows of
G_{N}
.

We end this section with a result for some restricting range of
p
, that will be useful in Step 3. This result is an analog of Proposition
5.18
and
5.19
for GAP structures.

Proposition 9.16
.

Let
\varepsilon,C_{\ast}^{\prime}
be given. Assume that
N\geq cn
and
e^{n^{c/2}}\leq p
.
The probability that there exists a non-sparse vector
{\mathbf{v}}
whose all but
n^{\varepsilon}
entries belong to a GAP of size at most
n^{C_{\ast}^{\prime}}
and rank at most
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{v}}
is orthogonal to all but at mot
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
rows of
G_{N}
is bounded by
n^{-\Theta(n)}
.

We refer the reader to Appendix
B
for a proof of this result. We also refer the reader to
[
NW22
, Lemma 7.6]
for a similar statement for non-symmetric matrices.

9.4.
Step 3: passing to characteristic zero and back

In what follows we let
W_{m}/p
be the subspace generated by the columns (rows) of
G_{m}
over
p
. We first need the following

Lemma 9.17

(Lifting sparse normal vectors from
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
to
{\mathbb{R}}
)
.

[
NW22
, Lemma 6.5]
Let
T>0
be fixed
5
5

5

In our application, as the matrix entries in all models under consideration are bounded by
O(1)
, we can let
T=1
, say, and assume
n
to be sufficiently large.
. Let
k,l,n
be positive integers with
l\leq k
, and
M
a
l\times k
matrix with integer entries
|M_{ij}|\leq n^{T}
.
If
p
is a prime larger than
e^{(k\log k)/2+kT\log n}
, then the rank of
M
over
{\mathbf{Q}}
is equal to the rank of
M/p
over
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
. This has the following corollaries.

(1)

If
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{l}
are vectors with entries
|Z_{ij}|\leq n^{T}
, and
Z_{1}/p,\dots,Z_{k}/p
are linearly dependent in
({\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}})^{l}
, then
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{k}
are also linearly dependent in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{l}
.

(2)

Let
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
be vectors with entries
|Z_{ij}|\leq n^{T}
. If there is a non-zero vector
{\mathbf{w}}\in({\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}})^{m}
with at most
k
non-zero entries that is normal to
Z_{1}/p,\dots,Z_{l}/p
, then there is a non-zero vector
{\mathbf{w}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
with at most
k
non-zero entries and normal to
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{l}
.

(3)

The kernel of the map
M:{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}^{l}
surjects onto the kernel of the map

M:({\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}})^{k}\rightarrow({\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}})^{l}
.

We say a submodule of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
is
admissible
if it is generated by vectors with coordinates at most
n^{T}
in absolute value. Recall that a vector is structured (or fully structured) if it satisfies the hypothesis and conclusion of Lemma
9.15
.

Lemma 9.18

(Lifting and reducing structured vectors)
.

Let
\varepsilon
be given sufficiently small (given
c,C,A,T
). Let
M_{0}
be an admissible submodule of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
, and
p
be a prime
\geq e^{n^{c}}
.
Then
M_{0}
has a structured almost normal vector in
{\mathbb{Z}}
(that is all but
n^{\prime}=n^{\varepsilon}
entries of it belongs to a symmetric GAP with integral generators which has size at most
n^{C_{\ast}^{\prime}}
and rank at most
C_{\ast}^{\prime}
, where
{\mathbb{C}}_{\ast}^{\prime}
is allowed to depend on
\varepsilon
as in the conclusion of Lemma
9.15
) if and only if
M_{0}/p
has a structured almost normal vector (of the same parameters).

We note that this result is similar to
[
NW22
, Lemma 7.7]
, where almost normal vectors were replaced by normal vectors. A proof of this result is included in Appendix
C
for convenience.

We now complete our main result of the section.

Proof of Proposition
9.2
for the Laplacian model (and hence also for the symmetric model).

Here we assume
N\geq cn
. Let
W_{N}
be the submodule in
{\mathbb{Z}}
generated by the columns of
G_{N}
. We let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
be the set of submodules
H_{N}
of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
such that for all primes
p>e^{n^{c}}
, the vector space
H_{N}/p
has no structured almost normal vector
{\mathbf{w}}
. First, we will bound
{\mathbf{P}}(W_{N}\not\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
. By Lemma
9.18
, for
p\geq e^{n^{c}}
, if
W_{N}/p
has a structured almost normal vector, then
W_{N}
has a structured almost normal vector, and then

W_{N}/p^{\prime}
has a structured almost normal vector for every prime
p^{\prime}
with
e^{n^{c/2}}\leq p^{\prime}<e^{n^{c}}
. So it suffices to bound the condition that
W_{N}/p
has a structured almost normal vector for
p
is a prime
e^{n^{c/2}}\leq p<e^{n^{c}}.

We will include in our upper bound the probability that
W_{N}/p
has a non-zero almost normal vector
{\mathbf{w}}
with
|\operatorname{supp}({\mathbf{w}})|\leq cN
for some prime
p<e^{n^{c}}
, which is at most

e^{-\Theta(n)}
by Lemmas
5.4
. Then, otherwise, by Proposition
9.16
, it is of probability at most

n^{-\Theta(n)}
that, for some prime
e^{n^{c/2}}\leq p\leq e^{n^{c}}
, the space
W_{N}/p
has a non-sparse structured almost normal vector
{\mathbf{w}}
. We conclude that, unconditional on all overwhelming events such as
{\mathcal{E}}_{mixing},{\mathcal{E}}_{non-sing},{\mathcal{E}}_{partial-full}
we have

{\mathbf{P}}(W_{N}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\geq 1-e^{-\Theta(n^{c})}.

Let
H_{N}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, then
H_{N}/p
has no structured almost normal vectors for any
p\geq e^{n^{c}}
. Then by Lemma
9.15
we must have

{\mathbf{P}}({\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N+1}/p)=\min\{{\operatorname{rank}}(G_{N}/p)+2,N+1\})\geq 1-n^{-C},

completing the proof.
∎

9.5.
Outline for the skew-symmetric model

As this case is fairly simple compared to the symmetric and especially the Laplacian case, we just sketch the proof. Our initial setting is similar to the case of symmetric and Laplacian matrices, where we rely on Corollary
7.18
for
A_{2n}
and the following analog of Proposition
9.2
.

Proposition 9.19
.

There is an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{alt}
in characteristic zero with probability
1-e^{-O(n^{c})}
such that under this event, for any primes
p\geq e^{n^{c}}
in the watch list
{\mathfrak{W}}_{2N}
, and for all
cn\leq 2N\leq 2n-2
we have

{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{2N+1}/p)={\operatorname{rank}}(A_{2N}/p)+2

and

{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{2N+2}/p)={\operatorname{rank}}(A_{2N+1}/p)+2.

Using this we can conclude Proposition
2.7
as follows.

Proof of Proposition
2.7
.

We will condition on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{alt}
from Proposition
9.19
. We first show that

{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{2n}/p)\geq 2n-2,\forall p\geq e^{n^{c}}.

Indeed, if
{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{2n}/p)\leq 2n-4
, then
p\in{\mathfrak{W}}_{2n-2}
. Let
N_{\min}\leq n-1
be a smallest index where for all
N\in[N_{\min},n-1]
we have
p\in{\mathfrak{W}}_{2N}
. Because of Proposition
9.19
, we certainly have
n/2\leq N_{\min}
