. Now if
N_{\min}<n-1
, then we have learned that
p\notin{\mathfrak{W}}_{2N_{\min}-1}
, and hence
A_{2N_{\min}-1}/p
has full rank but
A_{2N_{\min}}/p
has rank
2N_{\min}-2
. In this case, by Proposition
9.19

A_{2N_{\min}+2}
has rank
2N_{\min}+2
, so
p\notin{\mathfrak{W}}_{2(N_{\min}+1)}
, a contradiction. So we must have
N_{\min}=n-1
. This implies that
A_{2(n-2)}/p
has full rank and
A_{2(n-1)}/p
has rank
2n-4
. Another application of Proposition
9.19
then yields that
A_{2n}/p
has rank
2n-2
, another contradiction.

Now we show that

{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{2n+1}/p)\geq 2n,\forall p\geq e^{n^{c}}.

If
A_{2n}/p
has rank
2n
then there is nothing to prove. If
A_{2n}/p
has rank
2n-2
, then
p\in{\mathfrak{W}}_{2n}
, and hence by Proposition
9.19
we have
A_{2n+1}/p
has rank
2n
.
∎

In what follows we sketch the idea to prove Proposition
9.19
. Our method follows the arguments from the previous section (especially for the symmetric matrices case) without the need of Subsection
9.3
. Indeed, assume that

{\mathbf{P}}\Big({\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N+1}/p)=\min\big\{{\operatorname{rank}}(A_{N}/p)+2,N+(N\ \operatorname{mod}\ 2)\big\}\Big)\leq 1-n^{-C},

where
C
is chosen sufficiently large (so after taking union bound over
O(n^{2})
primes from the watch lists we still have the above event with overwhelming probability.) Then by Lemma
7.7
(applied to the skew-symmetric matrix case) and by Theorem
8.2
there is an almost normal vector of
A_{N}
(the vectors of
a_{ij}
from (
7.6
)) which is fully structured. (We remark that here there is no need to use Theorem
8.14
as in the Laplacian case because it suffices to consider
X_{N+1}\notin W_{N}
(where
X_{N+1}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{N}
is the last column of
A_{N+1}/p
without the last entry, and for this Theorem
8.2
suffices). Finally we then use the methods from Subsection
9.4
to show that these events over different
p
can be simultaneously treated by working only on one
p
in the range
e^{n^{c/2}}\leq p\leq e^{n^{c}}
, and to this end we use Proposition
9.16
.

10.
Further remarks and directions

Our treatment for Laplacian graphs can be extended to random Erdős-Rényi graphs of other parameters.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
q>0
be fixed. The results of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.22
also hold for the Laplacian of
G(n,q)
.

We sketch the main ideas below, omitting the details.

•

First, the neighbor reshuffling process remains the same where we reshuffle the pairs of edge and non-edge in Definition
1.23
by a fair coin flip (hence the randomness created by reshuffling are i.i.d. Bernoulli taking values
0,1
with probability 1/2). Here by Chernoff we just need to modify (
1.25
) to
{\mathbf{P}}(\wedge_{i=1}^{n+1}qn-t\sqrt{n}\leq d_{i}\leq qn+t\sqrt{n})\geq 1-n\exp(-\Theta_{q}(t^{2}))
and (
1.27
) to
{\mathbf{P}}\big(\wedge_{k\geq cn}|I_{k}|\in(2q(1-q)k-t\sqrt{n},2q(1-q)k+t\sqrt{n}\big)\geq 1-n\exp(-\Theta_{q}(t^{2}))
. We also need to modify (
9.3
) and (
9.7
) accordingly.

•

Next, for our treatment of small primes, by
[
Woo17
, Theorem 1.1]
, Theorem
1.20
extends to the Laplacian model of
G(n,q)
.

•

For moderate primes, to establish Proposition
2.1
we will need Theorem
7.17
. For this, the main results of Sections
5
and
6
, Lemma
5.5
, Proposition
5.19
, Lemma
6.5
, and Proposition
6.29
are all valid in the more general setting of
G(n,q)
with the modified reshuffling process. Here we note that Theorem
5.11
and Theorem
6.15
work under fairly general assumption of randomness (such as (
1.2
)).

•

Lastly, for large primes, to establish Proposition
2.5
we use the “watch list” method of Section
9
. Here, again we rely on the inverse results of Theorem
8.2
and
8.14
of Section
8
, as well as Lemma
9.9
by passing to the matrix model of Definition
1.28
.

It is an interesting problem to extend our results to random sparse graphs and matrices. This is not impossible, especially when
q\geq n^{-1+\varepsilon}
, but the proofs are expected to be extremely technical. It is also interesting to generalize our results to Laplacian of general random matrices. (In this paper we relied on the neighbor reshuffling process, which is rather specific to
\{0,1\}
matrices.) Furthermore, it would be interesting to extend our results to adjacency matrices of random graphs of given degrees
d_{i}
(say all of order
n
, for instance the model
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{d}}
from Def.
1.28
). Some of our results, such as Corollary
9.11
, are applicable to this setting, but other ingredients such as Theorem
1.20

6
6

6

Heuristically one can hope to use the method of
[
M2́0
]
for this, but the implementation seems highly non-trivial.
and Theorem
7.17
are largely missing.

Finally, there are other interesting global statistics that we do not access in this paper, such as the probability that the group has square-free order (or cube-free order in the even dimensional skew-symmetric case), it would be interesting to address these issues.

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
8.14

Let
f(X)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq N}b_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}
. We write

\displaystyle|{\mathbf{P}}(f(X)=a)-\frac{1}{p}|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}|\sum_{t\neq 0,\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(f(X)t)e_{p}(-at)|\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\neq 0,\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}|{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(f(X)t)|.

For now we let
U\subset[N]
and let
U^{c}=[N]\backslash U
. This set
U
will be randomized at the end, but now it is deterministic. By using Cauchy Schwarz as in the proof of Lemma
7.4

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\neq 0,\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}|{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(f(X)t)|)^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}|{\mathbf{E}}e_{p}(f(X)t)|^{4}\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}|{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{U}}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{U^{c}},X_{U^{c}}^{\prime}}e_{p}((f(X_{U},X_{U^{c}})-f(X_{U},X_{U^{c}}^{\prime}))t)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{U^{c}},X_{U^{c}}^{\prime}}{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{U},X_{U}^{\prime}}e_{p}((f(X_{U},X_{U^{c}})-f(X_{U},X_{U^{c}}^{\prime})-f(X_{U}^{\prime},X_{U^{c}})+f(X_{U}^{\prime},X_{U^{c}}^{\prime}))t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p},t\neq 0}{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}e_{p}(\sum_{i\in U,j\in U^{c}}b_{ij}y_{i}y_{j}t)={\mathbf{P}}(Y_{U}BY_{U^{c}}=0)-\frac{1}{p}={\mathbf{P}}(Y_{1}B_{U}Y_{2}=0)-\frac{1}{p},

where
B_{U}
is the matrix
B_{U}(ij)=b_{ij}
iff
i\in U,j\in U^{c}
and
B_{U}(ij)=0
otherwise, and
Y_{1},Y_{2}
are independent with entries as i.i.d. copies of
\xi-\xi^{\prime}
.

If
{\mathbf{P}}(f(X)=a)\geq n^{-C}
and if
p\geq n^{A}
with sufficiently large
A
(given
C
) then
|{\mathbf{P}}(f(X)=a)-1/p|\geq N^{-C}/2
, and hence
{\mathbf{P}}(Y_{1}B_{U}Y_{2}=0)\geq n^{-4C}/16+1/p\geq N^{-4C}/16
. We can then apply Lemma
8.3
to this bilinear form to obtain

Corollary A.1
.

There exist a set
I_{0}(U)
of size
O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)
and a set
I(U)
of size at least
n-n^{\varepsilon}
such that for any
i\in I
, there are integers
0\neq k(U)
and
k_{ii_{0}}(U),i_{0}\in I_{0}(U)
, all bounded by
n^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
, such that

{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}\big(\langle k(U){\mathbf{r}}_{B_{U}}(i),Y\rangle+\langle\sum_{i_{0}\in I_{0}}k_{ii_{0}}(U){\mathbf{r}}_{B_{U}}(i_{0}),Y\rangle=0\big)=N^{-O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)},

where
Y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{N})
and
y_{i}
are i.i.d. copies of
\xi-\xi^{\prime}
.

Note that Corollary
A.1
holds for all
U
. In what follows we gather the information together to obtain structures for the entire matrix
B
(rather than for
B_{U}
).

As
I_{0}(U)\subset[N]^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
and
k(U)\leq n
, there are only
N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
possibilities that
(I_{0}(U),k(U))
can take. Thus there exists a tuple
(I_{0},k)
such that

I_{0}(U)=I_{0}
and
k(U)=k
for
2^{N}/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
different
U
. Let us denote this set of
U
by
\mathcal{U}
. Thus

|\mathcal{U}|\geq 2^{N}/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}.

Next, let
I
be the collection of
i
which belong to at least
|\mathcal{U}|/2
index sets
I_{U}
. Then we have

\displaystyle|I||\mathcal{U}|+(N-|I|)|\mathcal{U}|/2

\displaystyle\geq(N-N^{\varepsilon})|\mathcal{U}|

\displaystyle|I|

\displaystyle\geq N-2N^{\varepsilon}.

Fix an
i\in I
. Consider the tuples
(k_{ii_{0}}(U),i_{0}\in I_{0})
where
i\in I_{U}
. Because there are only
N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
possibilities such tuples can take, there must be a tuple, say
(k_{ii_{0}},i_{0}\in I_{0})
, such that
(k_{ii_{0}}(U),i_{0}\in I_{0})=(k_{ii_{0}},i_{0}\in I_{0})
for at least
|\mathcal{U}|/2N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}=2^{N}/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
sets
U
.

Because
|I_{0}|=O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)
, there is a way to partition
I_{0}
into
I_{0}^{\prime}\cup I_{0}^{\prime\prime}
such that there are
2^{N}/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
sets
U
above satisfying that
I_{0}^{\prime\prime}\subset U
and
U\cap I_{0}^{\prime}=\emptyset
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{I_{0}^{\prime},I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}
denote the collection of these
U
.

By passing to consider a subset of
\mathcal{U}_{I_{0}^{\prime},I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}
if needed, we may assume that either
i\notin U
or
i\in U
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}_{I_{0}^{\prime},I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}
. Without loss of generality, we assume the first case that
i\notin U
. (The other case can be treated similarly).

Let
U\in\mathcal{U}_{I_{0}^{\prime},I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}
and
{\mathbf{u}}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{N})
be its characteristic vector, that is
u_{j}=1
if
j\in U
, and
u_{j}=0
otherwise. Then, by the definition of
B_{U}
, and because
I_{0}^{\prime\prime}\subset U
and
I_{0}^{\prime}\cap U=\emptyset
, for
i_{0}^{\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime}
and
i_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime\prime}
we can respectively write

\langle{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{0}^{\prime}}(B_{U}),Y\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{i_{0}^{\prime}j}u_{j}y_{j},\mbox{ and }\langle{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}}(A_{U}),Y\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}j}(1-u_{j})y_{j}.

Also, because
i\notin U
, we have
\langle{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B_{U}),Y\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{ij}u_{j}y_{j}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\langle k{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B_{U}),Y\rangle+\sum_{i_{0}\in I_{0}}\langle k_{ii_{0}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{0}}(B_{U}),Y\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle k{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B_{U}),Y\rangle+\langle\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{0}^{\prime}}(B_{U}),Y\rangle+\langle\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime\prime}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}}(B_{U}),Y\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}kb_{ij}u_{j}y_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime}j}u_{j}y_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}j}(1-u_{j})y_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(kb_{ij}+\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime}j}-\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}j})u_{j}y_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}j}y_{j}

Next, by Corollary
A.1
, for each
U\in\mathcal{U}_{I_{0}^{\prime},I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}
we have
{\mathbf{P}}_{Y}\big(\langle k{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B_{U}),Y\rangle+\sum_{i_{0}\in I_{0}}\langle k_{ii_{0}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{0}}(B_{U}),Y\rangle=0\big)=N^{-O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
. Also, note that
|\mathcal{U}_{I_{0}^{\prime},I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}|=2^{N}/N^{O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}
. Hence,

{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}{\mathbf{E}}_{U}\big(k\langle{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B_{U}),Y\rangle+\sum_{i_{0}\in I_{0}}\langle k_{ii_{0}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{0}}(B_{U}),Y\rangle=0\big)\geq n^{-O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}.

Finally, when
U
runs through the subsets of
[N]
, we can view
{\mathbf{u}}
as a random vector with i.i.d.
\{0,1\}
-Bernoulli with parameter 1/2. By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle N^{-O_{C,\varepsilon}(1)}

\displaystyle\leq\left({\mathbf{E}}_{Y}{\mathbf{E}}_{U}(k\langle{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B_{U}),Y\rangle+\sum_{i_{0}\in I_{0}}\langle k_{ii_{0}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{0}}(B_{U}),Y\rangle=0)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\left({\mathbf{E}}_{U}(k\langle{\mathbf{r}}_{i}(B_{U}),Y\rangle+\sum_{i_{0}\in I_{0}}\langle k_{ii_{0}}{\mathbf{r}}_{i_{0}}(B_{U}),Y\rangle=0)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\left({\mathbf{E}}_{{\mathbf{u}}}(\sum_{j=1}^{n}(kb_{ij}+\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime}j}-\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}j})u_{j}y_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}j}y_{j}=0)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}{\mathbf{E}}_{{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}}\big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}(kb_{ij}+\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime}j}-\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}j})(u_{j}-u_{j}^{\prime})y_{j}=0\big)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{Z}\big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}(kb_{ij}+\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime}j}-\sum_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in I_{0}^{\prime\prime}}k_{ii_{0}^{\prime\prime}}b_{i_{0}^{\prime\prime}j})z_{j}=0\big)

where
Z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
and
z_{j}:=(u_{j}-u_{j}^{\prime})y_{j}
, and in the last inequality we used the simple observation that
{\mathbf{E}}_{u,u^{\prime}}(f(u)=0,f(u^{\prime})=0)\leq{\mathbf{E}}_{u,u^{\prime}}(f(u)-f(u^{\prime})=0)
.

Appendix B
Proof of Proposition
9.16

First of all, by definition the number of structured vectors
{\mathbf{v}}
is bounded by
n^{C_{\ast}^{\prime}n}p^{n^{\varepsilon}}\leq n^{2C_{\ast}^{\prime}n}
. Let
\lambda>0
be a constant to be chosen sufficiently small later (for instance
\lambda\leq\varepsilon c/24C_{\ast}^{\prime}
would work). We divide
[N]
into
\lfloor\lambda^{-1}\rfloor
index intervals
I_{i}
of length approximately
\lambda N
each. Let
\rho_{i}=\rho_{l}({\mathbf{v}}_{I_{i}})
, and let

\rho^{\ast}({\mathbf{v}})=\min_{i}\rho_{i}.

First assume that
{\mathbf{v}}
is such that

(B.1)

\rho^{\ast}({\mathbf{v}})\leq n^{-3C_{\ast}^{\prime}c^{-1}}.

Then, supposing that
\rho^{\ast}
is attained at
I_{0}
, by the decomposition from (
6.3
) we thus obtain that

{\mathbf{P}}(G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=0\mbox{ in $n-O(1)$ coordinates})\leq n^{O(1)}(1/p+\rho^{\ast})^{(1-\lambda)n}\leq n^{-2.5C_{\ast}^{\prime}c^{-1}N}\leq n^{-2.5C_{\ast}^{\prime}n}.

Here we used the assumption that
p
is larger than
e^{n^{c/2}}
.

So the contribution of
{\mathbf{P}}(G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=0)
over
{\mathbf{v}}
satisfying (
B.1
) is bounded by
n^{2C_{\ast}^{\prime}n}n^{-2.5C_{\ast}^{\prime}n}\leq n^{-C_{\ast}^{\prime}n/2}
.

Now we assume that

n^{-3C_{\ast}^{\prime}c^{-1}}\leq\rho^{\ast}=O(n^{-1/2}),

where we note that
n^{-1/2}
is the upper bound because our vectors are non-sparse. We divide this range into intervals
L_{i}
of forms
[n^{-(i+1)\delta},n^{-i\delta}]
where
\delta
is sufficiently small (such as
\delta\leq\varepsilon/4
) and consider the class
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
where
\rho^{\ast}\in L_{i}
.

We claim that this class
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
has at most
(n^{(i+1)\delta}/n^{\varepsilon/2})^{n}p^{n^{\varepsilon/2}\lambda^{-1}}
vectors. Indeed this is because over each interval
I
of length
\lambda n
we have
\rho({\mathbf{v}}_{I})\geq\rho^{\ast}
. So by Theorem
8.2
, we then have all but
n^{\varepsilon}
entries of
{\mathbf{v}}_{I}
belong to a GAP of size
\rho^{\ast}/\sqrt{n^{\varepsilon}}
. If we glue all the subvectors together we then obtain the bound as claimed, where
p^{\lambda^{-1}n^{\varepsilon}}
is the number of ways to choose the unstructured entries altogether.

Now for each
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
, by using the decomposition from (
6.4
) we have

{\mathbf{P}}(G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=0)\leq(1/p+\rho^{\ast})^{(1-\lambda)n}\leq(2n^{-i\delta})^{(1-\lambda)n},

where we again used the assumption that
p\geq e^{n^{c/2}}
. Taking union bound over
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(\exists{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i},G_{N}{\mathbf{v}}=0\mbox{ in $n-O(1)$ coordinates})

\displaystyle\leq(n^{(i+1)\delta}/n^{\varepsilon/2})^{n}p^{n^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-1}}(2n^{-i\delta})^{(1-\lambda)n}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}p^{n^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-1}}n^{-(\varepsilon/2-\delta-i\delta\lambda)n}

\displaystyle\leq n^{-(\varepsilon/8)n},

provided that
\delta\leq\varepsilon/4
and
\lambda\leq\varepsilon c/8C_{\ast}^{\prime}
(and so
i\delta\lambda\leq 3C_{\ast}^{\prime}c^{-1}\varepsilon c/24C_{\ast}^{\prime}=\varepsilon/8
). Summing over all
i
we thus obtain the claim.

Appendix C
Proof of Lemma
9.18

With room to spare, we use
O(1)
to replace any quantity that might depend on
C,A,c,\varepsilon,C_{\ast},C_{\ast}^{\prime}
. By restricting to the
n-O(1)
columns that are orthogonal to
{\mathbf{w}}
, it suffices to consider the case that all column vectors generating
M_{0}
are orthogonal to
{\mathbf{w}}
. In what follows
n^{\prime}=n^{\varepsilon}
.

We will first prove the “if” direction.
Assume that the first
m=n-n^{\prime}
entries of the normal vector
{\mathbf{w}}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})
belong to a symmetric well-bounded GAP
Q
with
r
generators
a_{1},\dots,a_{r}
in
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
w_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{r}x_{jl}a_{l}
for
1\leq j\leq m
.
Let
M
be the matrix with entries at most
n^{T}
in absolute value whose columns generate
M_{0}
. Let
R_{1},\dots,R_{n}
be the rows of
M
. We have the equality modulo
p

0=\sum_{j=1}^{m}w_{j}R_{j}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}w_{j}R_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{r}a_{l}(\sum_{j=1}^{m}x_{jl}R_{j})+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}w_{j}R_{j}.

Now for
1\leq l\leq r
, let
Z_{l}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}x_{jl}R_{j}
.
We have
|x_{jl}|\leq|Q|\leq n^{O(1)}
.
The entries of
Z_{l}
are then bounded by
O(n^{T+O(1)})
, while the entries of
R_{{m+1}},\dots,R_{n}
are bounded by
n^{T}
.
Let
M^{\prime}
be the matrix whose columns are
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{r},R_{m+1},\dots R_{n}
.
The above identity then implies that
(a_{1},\dots,a_{r},w_{m+1},\dots w_{n})^{T}
is in the kernel of
M^{\prime}
.
Lemma
9.17
(
3
) applied to
M^{\prime}
, with
k=r+n^{\prime}
implies that as long as
p\geq e^{(k\log k)/2+k(T+O(1))\log n}
(which is satisfied because
p\geq e^{n^{c}}
, and
n^{\prime}=n^{\varepsilon}
, and
n
is sufficiently large), then
there exist integers
a_{l}^{\prime},w_{j}^{\prime}
, reducing mod
p
to
a_{l},w_{j}
, for
1\leq l\leq r
and
m+1\leq j\leq n
,
such that

\sum_{k=1}^{r}a_{l}^{\prime}Z_{l}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}w_{j}^{\prime}R_{j}=0.

Let
{\mathbf{w}}^{\prime}=(w_{1}^{\prime},\dots,w_{n}^{\prime})
where
w_{j}^{\prime}=\sum_{l=1}^{r}x_{jl}a_{l}^{\prime}
for
1\leq j\leq m
. By definition the
w_{j}^{\prime}
for
1\leq j\leq m
belong to the symmetric GAP with generators
a^{\prime}_{l}
and with the same rank and dimensions as
Q
, and
{\mathbf{w}}^{\prime}
is normal to
M_{0}
. Further
{\mathbf{w}}^{\prime}
is non-zero since it reduces to
w
mod
p
.

The “only if” direction appears easier at first—if we start with a structured normal vector, we can reduce the generators of the GAP and the normal vector mod
p
for any prime
p
. However, the difficulty is that for general primes
p
it is possible for the generators
a_{l}
of the GAP to be not all
0
mod
p
, but yet the resulting normal vector
{\mathbf{w}}
to be
0
mod
p
.
Given
M_{0}
, we choose
{\mathbf{w}}
minimal (e.g. with
\sum_{i}|w_{i}|
minimal) so that the first
m=n-n^{\prime}
entries (without loss of generality) of the normal vector
{\mathbf{w}}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})
to
M_{0}
belong to a symmetric well-bounded GAP
Q
with
r
generators
a_{1},\dots,a_{r}
in
{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
w_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{r}x_{jl}a_{l}
for
1\leq j\leq m
and
w
is non-zero.
Let
M_{\mathbf{x}}
be the
n\times(r+n^{\prime})
matrix with entries
x_{jl}
in the first
m
rows and
r
columns, the
n^{\prime}\times n^{\prime}
identity matrix in the last
n^{\prime}
rows and columns, and zeroes elsewhere.
So for
{\mathbf{a}}:=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r},w_{m+1},\dots w_{n})^{T}
, we have
M_{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{a}}={\mathbf{w}}^{T}.

Certainly by minimality of
{\mathbf{w}}
at least some coordinate of
{\mathbf{w}}
is not divisible by
p
(else we could divide the
a_{l}
and
w_{j}
all by
p
and produce a smaller structured normal
{\mathbf{w}}
).
Suppose, for the sake of contradiction that all of the coordinates of
{\mathbf{w}}
are divisible by
p
.
The entries of
M_{\mathbf{x}}
are bounded by
n^{O(1)}
,
so, as above, for
p\geq e^{n^{c}}
, by Lemma
9.17
(
3
) we have that
\ker M_{\mathbf{x}}|_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{r+n^{\prime}}}
surjects onto
\ker M_{\mathbf{x}}/p.
So
{\mathbf{a}}/p
is in the kernel of
M_{\mathbf{x}}/p
, and choose some lift
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}:=(a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{r},w^{\prime}_{m+1},\dots w^{\prime}_{n})^{T}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
of
{\mathbf{a}}/p
in the kernel of
M_{\mathbf{x}}
. Then
{\mathbf{a}}-{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}\in p{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
, and
M_{\mathbf{x}}(\frac{1}{p}({\mathbf{a}}-{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}))=\frac{1}{p}{\mathbf{w}}
. Note that
\frac{1}{p}{\mathbf{w}}
is non-zero integral normal vector to
A
, and the equality
M_{\mathbf{x}}(\frac{1}{p}({\mathbf{a}}-{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}))=\frac{1}{p}{\mathbf{w}}
shows that all but
n^{\prime}
of the coordinates of

\frac{1}{p}{\mathbf{w}}
belong to a symmetric well-bounded GAP with integral generators and the same rank and volume as
Q
, contradicting the minimality of
{\mathbf{w}}
. Thus we conclude that
{\mathbf{w}}/p
is non-zero and thus a structured normal vector of
M_{0}/p
for the GAP
Q/p
.
